[6 Normal] This is a work of fiction. Names, characters, departments, institutions and incidents are the product of the author’s imagination or are used fictitiously. Several characters are inspired by real physicists and carry altered names; their science is represented as faithfully as the story allows, and their private lives, dialogue and opinions are invented.

[46 Heading 2] Chapter One
[47 Heading 3] Outside the Permitted Options
[48 First Paragraph] On the fourteenth of March, inside the same six hours, fourteen people who had spent sixteen months unable to make up their minds stopped being unable, and became something nobody had offered them. It took the Department of Remaining Possibilities most of a week to notice, and it was the department whose job it was.
[49 Body Text] The department occupied the second and part of the third floor of a building in Marsham Street that had been designed in 1971 for a purpose no one could now establish. After sixteen months it had forty-one staff, a budget, a mission statement Lolly Wren had refused to write, and a corridor smell.
[50 Body Text] The corridor smell was the thing that had finally convinced her it was real.
[51 Body Text] You could found an institution, she had discovered, and appoint to it, and give it powers, and it would remain provisional — a thing being tried — right up until the morning you walked in and recognised the smell of the corridor as belonging to you. After that it was permanent, and had always been permanent, and would outlive everyone in it, and would in the fullness of time do something appalling that no one currently employed would have countenanced.
[52 Body Text] She had put that observation in the notebook, which by then was in its fourth volume, and Stephanie Fainrose had read it and said, “Yes. Now write the mission statement.”
[53 Body Text] She had not written the mission statement.
[54 Body Text] Gideon Wigglesworth had read the same page the night before. He had come up from the bench room with a mug, set it where her hand would find it, and said, a little behind the thought, “You don’t have to write what the place is. You already do the work.”
[55 Body Text] She had not thanked him. He had not required it. That was the arrangement, and it had survived the Annex, the eleven weeks, and a job title the Ministry had invented because no existing grade covered a man who could find a relay in a building designed to hide one.
[56 Body Text] He was downstairs now. She could hear him. A clank, a pause, a shorter clank: the doorframe, and then the rest of the sentence. The corridor smell she trusted had solder in it. She had told nobody that this was the part she recognised as hers.
[57 Body Text] The department’s work was, in the language of the Act, the identification, monitoring, and protection of unresolved states within the civil estate. In practice it meant three things. It meant reviewing every proposed instrument that touched a citizen’s affairs and asking whether it demanded conjugate certainties, which was now unlawful and which departments still attempted roughly nine times a month, mostly through sincere ignorance. It meant maintaining the register of the three hundred and eight — the P-9 states recovered from the horizon of Singularity Asset 4C during the eleven weeks, returned to the districts they had come out of, and not one of them yet finished.
[58 Body Text] And it meant Thursdays.
[59 Body Text] Thursdays were Anomalies.
[60 Body Text] “Wren.”
[61 Body Text] Miss Adaeze Pike put her head round the door at nine forty on a Thursday morning in the third week of March. In sixteen months she had become the best analyst in the building and exchanged her brittle brightness for a level delivery of bad news. Lolly recognised the delivery before Miss Pike spoke.
[62 Body Text] “P-9 register,” she said. “One of them’s resolved.”
[63 Body Text] “That’s good.”
[64 Body Text] “No it isn’t.”
[65 Body Text] She came in and put a single sheet on Lolly’s desk, which was the departmental convention for this cannot be sent by email yet.
[66 Body Text] “Register number one-forty-one,” she said. “Ellen Prosper, aged thirty-four, Outer Fenwick, on the ridge above Sub-District 6. Pending state at time of transfer: whether to accept a place on a postgraduate course in Newcastle or remain and take over her mother’s shop.”
[67 Body Text] Lolly knew it. She knew all three hundred and eight, which was not a policy, merely a thing that had happened to her.
[68 Body Text] “She’s decided?”
[69 Body Text] “She’s resolved,” said Miss Pike, “into neither.”
[70 Body Text] Lolly looked up.
[71 Body Text] “Say that properly.”
[72 Body Text] “She is not in Newcastle. She has not taken the shop. As of Tuesday at approximately four in the afternoon, Ellen Prosper is a licensed operator of a small marine survey vessel out of Peterhead, in a partnership with two people she had never met, in a trade she had — and I have checked this six ways — no prior knowledge of, no contact with, no qualification for, and no route to.” Miss Pike put a second sheet down. “The state resolved outside its permitted options.”
[73 Body Text] Lolly sat back slowly.
[74 Body Text] “That’s not possible,” she said. “That’s — Adaeze, that’s not a slightly surprising outcome. A pending state has a set. It’s a superposition over the alternatives that were live when it was frozen. Two options in, one option out. That’s what resolution is.”
[75 Body Text] “Yes.”
[76 Body Text] “There is no amplitude on Peterhead!”
[77 Body Text] “No,” said Miss Pike. “I checked it twice. There is nothing there.”
[78 Body Text] “Then either the register is wrong about what her options were—”
[79 Body Text] “It isn’t. It’s her own declaration, countersigned, and she is quite clear that in November of the year before last she had two possibilities and hated both.”
[80 Body Text] “—or she simply changed her mind, in the ordinary way, like a person, over sixteen months, which is allowed and is in fact the entire point of what we gave back to them.”
[81 Body Text] Miss Pike had been waiting for that objection. Lolly could see the answer in the papers still in her hand.
[82 Body Text] “That’s what I assumed on Tuesday,” she said. “So I ran the coherence trace. Standard post-recovery monitoring, we do it monthly on all three hundred and eight, it’s the dullest job in the building.” She put down a third sheet, and this one was a graph. “If somebody changes their mind, the state resolves — the amplitude moves from one option to the other and then it settles, and the trace goes flat and stays flat. That’s what a decision looks like. We ran it on volunteers when we set the monitoring up. They all look the same.”
[83 Body Text] “And this one?”
[84 Body Text] “This one didn’t move.”
[85 Body Text] Lolly looked at the graph.
[86 Body Text] The two options — Newcastle, and the shop — sat where they had sat for sixteen months, in the shallow untroubled way that unresolved things sit. Neither had grown. Neither had won.
[87 Body Text] And then, on the fourteenth, both of them had simply gone out, together, at the same instant, and something entirely else had come up somewhere off the edge of the chart.
[88 Body Text] “It didn’t choose,” said Lolly.
[89 Body Text] “It didn’t choose. It left.” Miss Pike’s voice stayed level. “Both options extinguished simultaneously and a third appeared that was not in the state space. That’s not a decision. There’s no mechanism in anything Dr Fainrose has ever written that permits it.”
[90 Body Text] When she looked up from the graph Gideon was in the doorway. He had heard her voice change from the bench room and taken the stair too fast. He filled the office the way a workbench does: sleeves pushed back, a burn on the wrist he had not noticed, the pencil behind his ear shorter than it had been on Monday.
[91 Body Text] “Decisions leave a warm trace,” he said, carefully, as if volume were a thing he might trip over. “Both lanes went cold at the same instant. That’s a contact opening somewhere you didn’t wire.”
[92 Body Text] Miss Pike looked at him, then at the graph, and did not argue. Lolly was glad he had come before she had decided to be glad. She always was. She had stopped treating that as a failure of rank.
[93 Body Text] She was already reaching for the telephone.
[94 Body Text] “Are there others?”
[95 Body Text] “That,” said Miss Pike, “is the reason I brought paper.”
[96 Body Text] Fainrose came over from the oversight body at ten and stood looking at the wall for a long time.
[97 Body Text] Fourteen coherence traces were pinned along the plasterboard of the third-floor briefing room. Each showed the same event. Two options, flat, patient, sixteen months of nothing. And then both extinguished within the same six-hour window, on the fourteenth of March, and something appearing outside the set.
[98 Body Text] “Fourteen out of three hundred and eight,” said Fainrose.
[99 Body Text] “So far,” said Miss Pike. “I’ve got the rest running. It takes three minutes a trace.”
[100 Body Text] “Are they geographically clustered?”
[101 Body Text] “No. Twelve districts, three counties.”
[102 Body Text] “Demographically?”
[103 Body Text] “No.”
[104 Body Text] “Related by content? Similar decisions?”
[105 Body Text] “No. A course, a shop, two marriages, an apprenticeship, a house purchase, a name change, a reconciliation, a decision about a dog.” Miss Pike consulted nothing; she also knew all three hundred and eight. “There is exactly one thing they have in common and I don’t like it.”
[106 Body Text] “Go on.”
[107 Body Text] “They were the fourteen that came out of the horizon last,” said Miss Pike. “In the final nine days of the recovery window, when 4C was in its terminal phase and von Wittenberg’s people were unscrambling faster and dirtier than they wanted to. The last fourteen recovered. Nothing else connects them.”
[108 Body Text] Fainrose said nothing at all for about half a minute.
[109 Body Text] Then she said, “Lolly. What does a state do when it cannot resolve into any of its permitted options?”
[110 Body Text] “Nothing. It waits. It stays in superposition until something couples to it.”
[111 Body Text] “And if it waits sixteen months, and the barrier between it and its permitted options is high — if the two available outcomes have both become, in some sense, unreachable — what then?”
[112 Body Text] “Then it goes on waiting.”
[113 Body Text] “Classically, yes.” Fainrose turned round. “Lolly, you spent ten years in electronics. What is a tunnel diode.”
[114 Body Text] And Lolly felt the floor of sixteen months slide, in the way it had slid in a car park and on a wall and in a school hall, and she said, “Oh.”
[115 Body Text] “Say it.”
[116 Body Text] “A barrier the thing shouldn’t get through,” she said slowly, “and it gets through anyway. Not over. Through. Because the amplitude doesn’t stop at the wall — it drops off inside it, but it doesn’t reach zero, and if the wall is thin enough there’s a bit of it on the other side, and so there’s a probability of finding the thing somewhere it did not have the energy to reach.”
[117 Body Text] “Yes.”
[118 Body Text] “You can’t tunnel to a state that isn’t in the space.”
[119 Body Text] “You cannot tunnel to a state that is not in your description of the space,” said Fainrose. “Which is a different sentence, and the difference has cost this country nine thousand houses once already.”
[120 Body Text] Miss Pike said, “Meaning what, exactly?”
[121 Body Text] “Meaning that our register lists two options for Ellen Prosper because Ellen Prosper listed two options for Ellen Prosper,” said Fainrose, “and both of us may have been describing a subspace of something larger. And if the true state space is larger, then there was always a barrier, and there was always a small amplitude on the far side of it, and it was always negligible.” She looked at the wall of traces. “Something has made it not negligible. In fourteen cases. In a six-hour window. And barriers do not thin themselves.”
[122 Body Text] The briefing room was quiet.
[123 Body Text] “There’s one more thing,” said Miss Pike, “and I’ve been saving it because I wanted you both in the room.”
[124 Body Text] She took out a fourth sheet, folded, from her inside pocket.
[125 Body Text] “Ellen Prosper’s survey vessel,” she said. “It’s a real business, it’s registered, it’s insured, it’s boring. The two partners are a man from Aberdeen and a woman from Sligo. Both of them have full and unremarkable histories and neither of them has ever been near a P-9 register.” She unfolded the sheet. “But the contract that put the three of them together — the initial charter, the thing that got them into a room — was let by a client through an agent in Vienna, and the client is a holding company, and the holding company has one named director.”
[126 Body Text] Lolly took the sheet.
[127 Body Text] She read the name.
[128 Body Text] She laid it on the briefing room table as carefully as a live circuit.
[129 Body Text] A. Eilstein.
[130 Body Text] “He said he would go on looking,” said Lolly. “For as long as it takes.”
[131 Body Text] “He said,” said Fainrose, “that they had stopped asking on an industrial scale.” She was looking at the fourteen traces, and her shoulders had not come down, and Lolly had learned sixteen months ago what that meant. “He has been on short rations since the fourteenth of November the year before last, and what he carries is eleven thousand years old, and he has just been found chartering a survey vessel.”
[132 Body Text] Miss Pike asked, “Surveying what?”
[133 Body Text] Nobody answered her. At that moment the telephone in the briefing room rang, which it never did, and Lolly picked it up, and listened, and said “yes” twice and “when” once and “no, keep it off the system” and put it down.
[134 Body Text] “That was the observatory liaison,” she said.
[135 Body Text] “We have an observatory liaison?”
[136 Body Text] “We have a form that generates one.” She was already gathering the traces off the wall. “Adaeze, get the remaining traces running two at a time and I don’t care what it does to the machine. Stephanie — the fourteenth of March. Six-hour window. Between about two in the morning and eight.”
[137 Body Text] “Yes.”
[138 Body Text] “They’ve had a detection.” She got her coat. “Nothing dramatic, nothing that made the news, they’ve been arguing about calibration for a week because nobody wanted to file it. A coincident event across four instruments. Not a signal, they’re very clear it isn’t a signal, they were quite insistent about that.”
[139 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Then what is it.”
[140 Body Text] “They don’t have a word,” said Lolly. “The best the man could manage was that for a period of about six hours, in a particular direction, the vacuum appeared to be cheaper than it ought to be.”
[141 Body Text] She held the door.
[142 Body Text] “Direction?”
[143 Body Text] “Out,” said Lolly Wren. “Along the arm. Nothing local at all.”
[144 Heading 2] Chapter Two
[145 Heading 3] The Cost of Nothing
[146 First Paragraph] The observatory liaison generated by the form turned out to be a Dr Priya Ramanathan, who met them at the gate of a facility outside Cheltenham that had been built to do radio astronomy and now did four other things as well, none of which it advertised.
[147 Body Text] She looked at Lolly’s identification for slightly too long.
[148 Body Text] “Remaining Possibilities,” she said.
[149 Body Text] “Yes.”
[150 Body Text] “Is that a real department.”
[151 Body Text] “There’s a corridor smell,” said Lolly. “You get used to the question.”
[152 Body Text] Inside, Dr Ramanathan and her colleagues were conducting an argument with great courtesy. They had memorised one another’s positions and were now defending their own chiefly out of loyalty to the time already spent.
[153 Body Text] “A week,” said Dr Ramanathan, not looking up from the trace. “We’ve been at it a week. Nobody will sign the note.”
[154 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Show us the event.”
[155 Body Text] They showed them the event.
[156 Body Text] It was, as advertised, not a signal. There was no structure in it, no pulse, no repetition, nothing that could be read. What there was, across four instruments of three different kinds — two interferometers, an atomic clock comparison, and a torsion balance that had been running unattended in a mineshaft in Cumbria for sixteen years and had never once done anything interesting — was a six-hour period in which all four had reported the same tiny departure from what they should have reported, in the same direction on the sky.
[157 Body Text] “That’s the whole of it,” said Dr Ramanathan. “Four instruments. Different physics in each case. Same window. Same bearing. And the departure isn’t a disturbance. That’s what we’ve been arguing about. A disturbance is noise, and noise makes readings worse. These readings didn’t get worse. They got—”
[158 Body Text] “Cheaper,” said Lolly.
[159 Body Text] Everybody in the room looked at her.
[160 Body Text] “That’s the word I used on the telephone,” said one of the men, a little defensively. “It’s not a technical term.”
[161 Body Text] “It’s a very good term,” said Fainrose, “and it is the reason we drove here. Explain it to me as though I were a committee.”
[162 Body Text] The man rubbed his face.
[163 Body Text] “Right. The clock comparison first, it’s the simplest. We run two clocks and compare. They disagree by a known amount and the disagreement is extremely stable, and buried in that disagreement is a contribution from — well, from the vacuum. From empty space.” He glanced at Lolly. “You’ll know that empty space isn’t empty.”
[164 Body Text] “I’ve spent sixteen months finding out.”
[165 Body Text] “Then you’ll know it has an energy. A floor. You cannot get rid of it and you cannot switch it off and it shows up in a great many places whether you want it to or not.” He turned to the screen. “That floor is a constant. It has always been a constant. For six hours on the fourteenth of March, in one direction, it was slightly less.”
[166 Body Text] Fainrose said, “By how much.”
[167 Body Text] “Eleven parts in ten to the twenty-second.”
[168 Body Text] “Consistently across all four?”
[169 Body Text] “Consistently across all four, which is why nobody will sign the note, because there is no mechanism, and four instruments agreeing on an impossible thing is historically how one discovers that four instruments share a cable.” He shrugged. “We’ve checked. There isn’t a cable.”
[170 Body Text] Dr Ramanathan set the cursor at the edge of the noise floor. “There is also a residue underneath it that I cannot source. It behaves as if it belonged to two files at once. I have been calling it noise, because the alternative is a paper.”
[171 Body Text] It was at this point that a voice from the back of the room, which had said nothing for twenty minutes, remarked:
[172 Body Text] “There is a mechanism. It is in a paper from 1948 and it is three pages long and one of you has almost certainly cited it.”
[173 Body Text] Lolly turned round.
[174 Body Text] The man at the back was in his sixties or perhaps older, very tall, very upright, in a cardigan and good shoes, with a face of settled amiability and the particular manner of somebody who has spent a career in an industrial laboratory rather than a university — which is to say, he looked less like a man who wanted to be right and more like a man who wanted the thing on the bench to work by Friday.
[175 Body Text] “Forgive me,” he said. “Hendrik van Casteel. I am here about the torsion balance. It is mine. Well. It was mine when it was built, and then it was a company’s, and then the company sold the division, and now it belongs to nobody and I visit it.”
[176 Body Text] “You’re not academic,” Lolly said.
[177 Body Text] “I was never academic,” said van Casteel. “I spent thirty-one years at a lighting concern. The best physics I ever did, I did because a manager asked me why the colloidal suspensions in a product line were sticking together, and I said I did not know, and he said find out by March.” He came forward. “This is not a boast. It is the reason I know what happened on the fourteenth, and these good people do not, because they are looking for a source and there is no source. There is a boundary condition.”
[178 Body Text] Fainrose said carefully, “Go on.”
[179 Body Text] “May I use the board?”
[180 Body Text] “Please.”
[181 Body Text] Van Casteel drew two vertical lines, close together, and nothing else.
[182 Body Text] “Empty space,” he said, “is full of fluctuation. Not particles as such — fields, restless, everywhere, at every wavelength. You cannot remove it. It is the ground floor and there is no basement. Everybody in this room accepts that, and one may go a whole career finding it a nuisance in the noise budget and never once find it useful.”
[183 Body Text] He tapped between the two lines.
[184 Body Text] “Now. Put two plates here, facing each other, uncharged, very close. What happens between them?”
[185 Body Text] Lolly, who had spent ten years around metal plates that were closer together than they looked, said, “You’ve restricted the wavelengths.”
[186 Body Text] Van Casteel turned round, delighted.
[187 Body Text] “You are the electronics man.”
[188 Body Text] “Ten years.”
[189 Body Text] “Then say the rest of it.”
[190 Body Text] “Between the plates only certain modes fit. It’s a cavity. Long wavelengths don’t.” Lolly was seeing it. “Outside, everything fits, because outside is unbounded.”
[191 Body Text] “And therefore?”
[192 Body Text] “Therefore there’s more fluctuation outside than in.” She stopped. “The energy density between the plates is lower. Not zero. Lower than the ordinary vacuum.”
[193 Body Text] “Lower than the ordinary vacuum,” said Hendrik van Casteel. “The vacuum between the plates is cheaper than the vacuum everywhere else, and the difference shows up as a force. The plates are pushed together by nothing at all, by the mere fact that there is more nothing outside them than in.” He set down the pen. “A man at my old company calculated it in 1948 while trying to work out why paint didn’t behave. It has been measured many times since, it is not in the least controversial, and it is the entire mechanism these people have spent a week not finding.”
[194 Body Text] There was a considerable silence in the room.
[195 Body Text] Dr Ramanathan said, “You are suggesting that something imposed a boundary condition on the vacuum. Along a bearing. For six hours.”
[196 Body Text] “I am suggesting,” said van Casteel, “that your instruments reported a lower vacuum energy density in one direction, and that physics knows exactly one way to lower the vacuum energy density, which is to impose boundaries. I have no idea what the boundaries were or how one imposes them across that baseline. I am a lighting engineer and I find the question ludicrous.” He picked up his coat. “But I decline to spend a week arguing about calibration when there is a three-page paper on the shelf that says precisely what a cheaper vacuum means. I came to say it out loud so that somebody would write it down, and I have, and now I should like my train.”
[197 Body Text] Lolly already was.
[198 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Mr van Casteel. If the vacuum in a region is cheaper — if the energy density of the ground state there is lower — what happens to a barrier in that region?”
[199 Body Text] Van Casteel stopped with one arm in his coat.
[200 Body Text] He looked at her for a long moment, and something in his amiability shut off, and Lolly understood that they had reached the part of the afternoon that the man had not seen coming.
[201 Body Text] “Ah,” he said. “You are not here about my torsion balance at all.”
[202 Body Text] “No.”
[203 Body Text] “A barrier,” said van Casteel slowly, “is a difference. It is the height of a region a system does not have the energy to occupy, measured against the floor. If you lower the floor beneath a barrier and you do not lower the barrier—”
[204 Body Text] “—then the barrier is taller,” said Lolly.
[205 Body Text] “Yes. And nothing gets through.”
[206 Body Text] “But if you lower the floor outside the barrier,” said Fainrose, “and leave the region inside it where it was—”
[207 Body Text] “Then the barrier is thinner,” said van Casteel, “in the only sense the exponent cares about, and since transmission depends exponentially on that thickness, eleven parts in ten to the twenty-second off the floor is not a small effect on a barrier. It could be the difference between never and sometimes.”
[208 Body Text] Fainrose took a folded sheet from her inside pocket. She had done this calculation before they got in the car, and had said nothing for two hours.
[209 Body Text] “I ran the fourteen cases last night,” she said. “Fourteen protected states, each with a barrier between its permitted options and whatever lay outside the register’s description. For each I computed the fractional drop in the local ground state needed to take the tunnelling probability from negligible to observed once in sixteen months.” She handed him the sheet. “The cases differ, so there is a range.”
[210 Body Text] Van Casteel read it.
[211 Body Text] Then he sat down on the edge of a desk, which for a man of his height and posture was very nearly a collapse.
[212 Body Text] “It is the same number,” he said. “To within your range.”
[213 Body Text] “Nine to fourteen,” said Fainrose. “Your number is in it. Comfortably. Not at the edge.”
[214 Body Text] “Dr Fainrose, this is one number matching another number.”
[215 Body Text] “Yes.”
[216 Body Text] “That is not a proof. I have spent thirty-one years in industry and I could show you a cabinet of coincidences that cost people their jobs.”
[217 Body Text] “I am aware,” said Fainrose. “I am not proposing to publish. I am proposing to act, and the difference has been my whole professional life since the year before last.” She took the sheet back. “Fourteen people in twelve districts went somewhere they had no route to, in a six-hour window, and during that window the price of nothing fell by just enough to let them.”
[218 Body Text] Dr Ramanathan said, into the quiet, “Along a bearing.”
[219 Body Text] “Along a bearing.”
[220 Body Text] “Which is out. Twelve degrees off the plane, along the arm, and there is nothing in that direction for a very long way.” She looked at the four traces on her own screen. “Dr Fainrose, if this is real, then whatever imposed the boundary is not on this planet, and the effect swept across us for six hours the way a shadow sweeps across a field, and it will have done the same thing to everything else it passed over.”
[221 Body Text] “Yes,” said Fainrose.
[222 Body Text] “There are three hundred and eight names on your register.”
[223 Body Text] “Three hundred and eight,” said Fainrose, “because those are the only unresolved states in this country that anybody has been monitoring. There are, at a conservative estimate, several billion others, held by people who have never heard of us, and no instrument anywhere has been watching a single one of them.”
[224 Body Text] The room was silent for some time.
[225 Body Text] Gideon got up from behind the rack. He had gone there when the man said cable, and he had stayed on the floor through the entire lecture, torch in his teeth, insulting the earth. He clipped the rack with his shoulder on the way up and did not apologise to it. He apologised to people. Furniture, in his view, had been warned.
[226 Body Text] “He’s right about the cable,” Gideon said, once his mouth was free, and a little behind the thought. “There isn’t one. The earth is filthy, and it is not your six hours. I can clean it tonight. It will not explain the sky.”
[227 Body Text] Lolly caught his eye across the room. He tipped his head: are you all right. She tipped hers: not yet. Nobody else in the building spoke that dialect, and she was grateful there was still one person who did.
[228 Body Text] Miss Pike rang at half past four, while they were in the car park. Lolly set the phone on the roof, on speaker, between herself and Fainrose.
[229 Body Text] “The remaining traces.”
[230 Body Text] “Go on.”
[231 Body Text] “It isn’t fourteen.” Miss Pike’s voice was as level as it had been that morning, which by now Lolly recognised as effort rather than temperament. “I’ve done two hundred and nine of the three hundred and eight, which is every one that’s on a continuous recorder. The fourteen we flagged were the ones that had already resolved outside their options, so they were visible. But that’s not the right thing to count.”
[232 Body Text] “What’s the right thing to count.”
[233 Body Text] “Whether the trace shows the event,” said Miss Pike. “Whether both permitted options were suppressed during the window, regardless of whether anything came back.”
[234 Body Text] Fainrose put both hands flat on the roof of the car.
[235 Body Text] “How many,” she said.
[236 Body Text] “Two hundred and nine out of two hundred and nine,” said Miss Pike. “Every single one I’ve looked at. All of them dipped, in the same six hours, on the fourteenth of March. Fourteen of them tunnelled out and resolved somewhere impossible. The other hundred and ninety-five didn’t go anywhere.”
[237 Body Text] “Then what did they do?”
[238 Body Text] “They came back,” said Miss Pike. “They dipped, and the barrier came back up, and they’re sitting where they were, unresolved, patient, apparently fine.” A pause, and then the thing she had been ringing to say. “It isn’t that fourteen of them were unlucky. It’s that all of them were offered. Two hundred and nine for two hundred and nine. The other ninety-nine are still on the old Annex loggers, which take one reading a day, at noon, so the window fell between two readings, and for those we will never know. I’m not going to guess. Sorry. Whatever came through on the fourteenth of March didn’t select anybody. It went past, and for six hours every unresolved thing in its path had a door in it, and almost everybody happened not to walk through.”
[239 Body Text] Lolly looked at the sky over Cheltenham, which was doing nothing at all, in the ordinary way.
[240 Body Text] “Adaeze,” she said. “Is there anything in the traces about the shape of the window?”
[241 Body Text] “How do you mean.”
[242 Body Text] “Six hours. Did it come on all at once and go off all at once, or did it—”
[243 Body Text] “It ramped,” said Miss Pike. “Up over about ninety minutes, flat for three hours, down over ninety. Why?”
[244 Body Text] Fainrose answered before she could.
[245 Body Text] “Because that is what a thing looks like when it passes,” she said, “rather than when it happens. And because it means we can compute a speed, and if we can compute a speed we can compute when it will be back, if it is the sort of thing that comes back.”
[246 Body Text] She got into the car.
[247 Body Text] “Marsham Street,” she said. “And then I want Beatrix. Not for a court. For a treaty.”
[248 Body Text] Lolly, getting in, said, “A treaty with whom?”
[249 Body Text] “I don’t know yet,” said Stephanie Fainrose. “But somebody has been lowering the price of nothing, and I should like to establish very early on whether they know we are here.”
[250 Body Text] ∗
[251 First Paragraph] At eight, it being Thursday, Lolly took her phone into the stairwell between the second and third floors and rang Larchfield House.
[252 Body Text] She had rung at eight on Thursdays for more than four years. In that time a Ministry had been taken apart, a department had been invented around her, and she had learned to read a coherence trace the way her father read a circuit diagram, by looking for the place where somebody had been in a hurry. None of it had moved the call. The call was a fixed point. She had come to suspect it was the only one she owned outright.
[253 Body Text] “Larchfield, Sandra speaking.”
[254 Body Text] “It’s Lolly.”
[255 Body Text] “Course it is, it’s eight.” Keys, a chair, the small grunt of a woman sitting down for the first time since lunch. “He’s had his tea. He’s had his tea and he’s had Tomasz’s tea, because Tomasz left it on the trolley, and your dad says wasting a hot drink is a crime against the working man, which I’ll be taking up with Tomasz.” A pause, and the voice changed gear into the one Sandra kept for paperwork. “Before I fetch him. Has anybody written to you about the reconfiguration?”
[256 Body Text] “The what?”
[257 Body Text] “I’ll take that as a no. Council letter. Came Monday, to the home, copies to families to follow, which means never. The residential floor closes at the end of January. Everyone downstairs gets a review in the autumn. Best interests. There’ll be a form with two boxes on it, and a meeting, and a social worker who’s lovely and has sixty cases.” Another pause. “I’m telling you now so you hear it from a person.”
[258 Body Text] Lolly stood on the cold landing and looked at nothing. Two floors down, through 1971 concrete, she could hear the bench room: a radio, and Gideon arguing with it.
[259 Body Text] “What are the two boxes?”
[260 Body Text] “Nursing wing upstairs, the secure side. Or a place they’ve found in Croydon, Elmhurst Court, which is very nice, it’s got a sensory garden.” Sandra said sensory garden the way Lolly’s father said decoration. “Neither one’s got a window onto the bus stop, if that’s what you’re asking.”
[261 Body Text] It was what she was asking. She had not known it until it was answered.
[262 Body Text] “There’s something else, and it’s probably nothing. I wrote it in the night book, because I write everything in the night book, because the one time I didn’t was the one time anybody asked.” Sandra breathed out. “Saturday he had a funny night. Agency girl found him in the lounge at three in the morning with his coat on and the kettle filled. He said your mum had come home and he’d better put a pot on. Ever so cheerful. Not the seaside, not a parents’ evening. Home. First time I’ve ever heard him say it.”
[263 Body Text] “When did he settle?”
[264 Body Text] “That’s the funny bit. Half seven, eight. He sat by the window the whole time like he was waiting for the door to go. Then he got up, quite calm, and said, ‘She’s not, is she,’ and had his porridge. Right as rain since. I only mention it.”
[265 Body Text] Saturday had been the fourteenth of March.
[266 Body Text] Lolly found she had sat down on the step. It was concrete, and cold, and had been poured for some purpose long forgotten, and it held her up perfectly well. Two in the morning until eight. A ramp, a plateau, a ramp. Every one of the two hundred and nine, Miss Pike had said, and the rest unknowable. Several billion others, Fainrose had said, and no instrument anywhere watching a single one of them.
[267 Body Text] Nobody had been watching her father. There was no trace. There was a night book in a care home off Pelham Road with a tired woman’s handwriting in it, and that was the whole of the record.
[268 Body Text] She caught herself writing him down as a ket, home plus not home, a little column of amplitudes with her father’s name on it, and stopped. He was not a ket. He was a man who had once rewired her entire bedroom on a Sunday so that she could reach the light switch from the bed, and then charged her a Mars bar for labour.
[269 Body Text] “Lol?” His voice arrived too loud, the way it always did, as though telephones were a new invention he was being careful not to break. “Sandra says I had a funny turn.”
[270 Body Text] “Hello, Dad. Do you remember it?”
[271 Body Text] “I remember the kettle. Somebody’d moved the kettle.” A pause, the honest kind. “I heard the door. You know how you hear a door. Like a fuse going in another room. You don’t hear it go, you hear the quiet after. So I thought, that’s June, she’s back early, the fathers can’t have argued much.” He laughed, short and pleased, the repairman’s laugh. “And then she wasn’t, and I knew she wasn’t, and that was that. I’m not daft. I’ve just got a lot of rooms and some of them have the lights off.”
[272 Body Text] “I know.”
[273 Body Text] “Big lad’s coming Sunday. We’re doing a Roberts. Some fool’s put the battery in it backwards and then blamed the radio. Fault’s never where they point.” The voice dropped to the one from the shop on Pelham Parade, the one he had used on customers who had tried to fix their own sets with a fork. “You’ve found something, haven’t you. You’ve got your found-something voice.”
[274 Body Text] “Something’s been taking the floors out from under people, Dad.”
[275 Body Text] There was a silence on the line, long enough for a trolley to go past somewhere behind him.
[276 Body Text] “Then find where it’s earthed,” said Dennis Wren. “Everything’s earthed somewhere. That’s not engineering, Lol. That’s manners.”
[277 Body Text] She went back up the stairs at twenty past eight with a page in the notebook that was not physics, and she did not show it to anyone.
[278 Body Text] Larchfield. 14 March, 03:00 to 08:00. Coat on. Kettle filled. “She’s not, is she.” No trace. Nobody watching.
[279 Body Text] And underneath, smaller:
[280 Body Text] Two boxes. Neither has the window.
[281 Heading 2] Chapter Three
[282 Heading 3] No Dice
[283 First Paragraph] He could not come in.
[284 Body Text] That was the first thing, and it took Lolly Wren rather longer to understand than it should have. At half past nine on the Thursday evening the front desk rang up to the second floor and said there was a gentleman in reception who would not state his business and would not sit down, and Lolly went down expecting a journalist.
[285 Body Text] Albrecht Eilstein was standing in the middle of the lobby, on the far side of the barrier line, with his coat over his arm.
[286 Body Text] He had got thinner, which Lolly had not thought possible, since the man had not been made of anything in particular to begin with. The great white nimbus of hair was the same. The face was the same. But something had gone out of it — not vitality exactly; more the settled comfort of a creature that has never once had to wonder where the next thing was coming from.
[287 Body Text] “Ms Wren,” he said. “I cannot get past your desk.”
[288 Body Text] “It’s a card reader.”
[289 Body Text] “It is not a card reader,” said Eilstein. “Or rather, it is, and that is not why. Your Act of the year before last, schedule four, paragraph nine. No unresolved state within the civil estate shall be subject to enquiry except upon the initiative of the person to whom it belongs.” He smiled faintly. “You wrote the notebook they took that from. It is the finest piece of legislation anyone has drafted against me in eleven thousand years, and I congratulate you, and it means I have been standing in this lobby for forty minutes.”
[290 Body Text] Lolly looked at him for a while. The first time they met he had told her Ulm, 1879, and he had not been lying: the man had been born then. What he carried had not. It was eleven thousand years old, it had come to him whole, memories and all, the way a house comes with its cellar, and he spoke from it as if he had been there, because in every sense that mattered to a ledger he had.
[291 Body Text] “You want to be invited.”
[292 Body Text] “I want to be asked,” said Eilstein. “There is a difference and you of all people know it. An invitation is hospitality. A question is a measurement. I am not here to be entertained, Ms Wren, I am here to be interrogated, and I cannot begin until somebody in this building wants to know something.”
[293 Body Text] “Why?”
[294 Body Text] “Because I have not eaten since the fourteenth of March,” said Albrecht Eilstein, “and I should like somebody competent to tell me why.”
[295 Body Text] Jago Flint was on the public side of the barrier with the evening post under one arm, a narrow dark coat, and the air of a man the card reader had agreed to tolerate until further notice.
[296 Body Text] “He won’t come up unless you ask him,” Jago said. The sentence arrived the way his sentences always did, cheerful, and carrying slightly more than the stamp covered. “I tried him in a stairwell last spring. He told me the pallor was the fluorescents and the Bloody Marys were only a preference. I have never believed a word of it. There is also a letter for you, from a garden in Woking. I wasn’t meant to read it. I read the first line. He says he signed a page he didn’t read.”
[297 Body Text] Lolly took the letter. She did not open it. She put it in the notebook, between two pages that were not ready for it. Eilstein inclined his head to the courier, with a courtesy so complete it was faintly alarming, and did not correct the story. Jago, on his way back out through the barrier, left word at the front desk for Miss Pike: could the eleven pounds forty wait till Friday? It had been waiting till Friday since the third of March.
[298 Body Text] Lolly said, “Come up.”
[299 Body Text] The barrier let him through at once, with a clunk that sounded relieved. Mr Hale on the night desk did not. Mr Hale had a procedure, and the procedure had a camera. Visitors were photographed, printed, laminated and clipped, and Mr Hale had done it to two Secretaries of State and the man who came about the lifts, and he was not going to stop for a gentleman with a coat over his arm.
[300 Body Text] Eilstein stood in front of the little lens with the patience he had inherited from a disappointing sitting for Holbein. The printer whirred. The badge came out with VISITOR: A. EILSTEIN across the top, the date, the escort’s name, and underneath, where the face should have been, a neat grey rectangle of lobby wall with the fire-exit sign in it.
[301 Body Text] Mr Hale looked at the badge. He looked at Eilstein. He took a second picture and got the wall again, slightly more in focus.
[302 Body Text] “Camera’s playing up,” he said.
[303 Body Text] “It is not,” said Eilstein. “It is the most honest camera in Westminster.”
[304 Body Text] Mr Hale laminated it anyway, because the procedure said laminate. Under PURPOSE OF VISIT Eilstein wrote, in a hand shaped by memories older than printing, To be asked, and under TIME OUT he wrote That depends on Ms Wren. Mr Hale read both, said, “Fair enough,” and clipped the badge to the lapel himself. It was the only photograph of Albrecht Eilstein the Ministry ever held. For the rest of the year it was pinned to the corkboard in the briefing room, a portrait of a fire exit, under a sticky note in Miss Pike’s capitals: SUBJECT DECLINED TO APPEAR.
[305 Body Text] They took him to the third-floor briefing room, where the traces were still pinned up. Fainrose came from a dinner she did not explain; Miss Pike came from the desk she had occupied since seven that morning. Beatrix Sloan arrived from chambers in Gray’s Inn at ten past ten, looking as if she had cancelled something she had been looking forward to.
[306 Body Text] Eilstein looked at the four of them with something close to affection and considerably closer to hunger.
[307 Body Text] “Ms Sloan. You have gone to the Bar.”
[308 Body Text] “I have gone to the Bar,” said Beatrix, “because I established in the year before last that being right inside an institution is a great deal slower than being expensive outside one.”
[309 Body Text] “And you, Miss Pike.” He inclined his head. “The column. I have thought about you a good deal. You were the only person in that room who lost something by being honest, and you did it in under four minutes, and I have eaten the discarded version of you in which you did not, and it was the bitterest thing I have tasted in a century.”
[310 Body Text] Miss Pike went slightly grey and said nothing.
[311 Body Text] “You said you hadn’t eaten,” said Lolly.
[312 Body Text] “I said I have gone without since the fourteenth of March,” said Eilstein, “which is a precise statement and not a complaint about portions.” He sat down without being asked, which he was entitled to now, and laced his old spotted hands. “Before we go further: yes. The vessel is mine. The charter is mine. The holding company is mine and there are a dozen others and Ms Sloan will find them all by Tuesday and I have instructed my people to assist her, which will annoy her more than obstruction would.”
[313 Body Text] “Considerably,” said Beatrix.
[314 Body Text] “I chartered a marine survey out of Peterhead in January because I wished to measure something on the floor of the North Sea and I did not wish anybody to know I was measuring it. I did not select the crew. The agent selected the crew. I have never met Ellen Prosper and until this week I did not know her name.” He looked round at them. “That is the whole of my involvement in the thing you have come to think of as my involvement, and it is a coincidence, and I am aware that it is precisely the sort of thing a person says.”
[315 Body Text] Fainrose said, “What were you measuring.”
[316 Body Text] “The price of nothing,” said Eilstein. “Same as your engineer. I began in January because in December I noticed I was getting less from ordinary business than I had been getting in the autumn, and by January I was certain, and by February I was frightened, and on the fourteenth of March at approximately two in the morning it stopped entirely for six hours, and I have been living on scraps ever since.” He turned his hands over. “Look at them. I carry eleven thousand years, and nothing that carried them has been thin since the Bronze Age.”
[317 Body Text] Lolly sat down opposite him.
[318 Body Text] “Explain what you eat,” she said. “Properly. Not the poetry.”
[319 Body Text] Eilstein’s eyebrows went up.
[320 Body Text] “You have got harder.”
[321 Body Text] “I have a department.”
[322 Body Text] “Ah. Yes.” He nodded, and for a moment looked genuinely pleased. “Very well. The poetry is that I drink the road not taken. The mechanism is this: when a system in more than one possible condition is obliged to become one, the conditions that do not obtain are not simply lost. There is a transition, it has a cost, and what is paid is what I take. I am not a predator, Ms Wren. I am a scavenger of an extremely refined kind.” He spread his hands. “Wherever there is chance, there is waste. And wherever there is waste, there is dinner.”
[323 Body Text] “Until the fourteenth of March.”
[324 Body Text] “Until the fourteenth of March,” said Albrecht Eilstein, “on which day two hundred and nine unresolved states in this country — and I would guess a very great number elsewhere, but I have no instruments outside these islands — were offered a door, and fourteen of them went through it, and there was nothing to eat.”
[325 Body Text] The room went quiet.
[326 Body Text] Fainrose said, slowly, “Nothing at all?”
[327 Body Text] “Not a crumb. Fourteen systems that had been in more than one condition and were now in exactly one, and no cost paid anywhere.” He leaned forward. “Dr Fainrose, I know what a resolution tastes like the way your Ms Wren knows what a failing capacitor smells like. On the fourteenth of March fourteen things resolved in front of me and left no leavings, and I have spent every hour since trying to work out what process does that, and yesterday I concluded that I am not clever enough and that the only competent person in the field is a woman who thinks I am Undead.”
[328 Body Text] “You are Undead,” said Fainrose.
[329 Body Text] “I am an informed Undead, and I am currently your instrument,” said Eilstein. “I am the only detector on this planet that responds to the thing you cannot measure. Mr van Casteel’s balance sees the vacuum. Miss Pike’s traces see the barrier. Nobody sees the waste, because nobody has ever needed to, because waste has always simply happened. I see it. And on the fourteenth of March there was none.”
[330 Body Text] Lolly had been writing. She stopped.
[331 Body Text] “Say what that means,” she said. “You’ve worked it out. You’ve had a week. Say it.”
[332 Body Text] And Albrecht Eilstein said:
[333 Body Text] “It means God did not roll the dice for those fourteen.”
[334 Body Text] Nobody spoke.
[335 Body Text] “That is a very old phrase,” he said, “and I did not invent it and the man who made it famous was wrong about a great deal. But he was not wrong about the method, and the method is the only thing I have brought you tonight, so let me say it carefully.”
[336 Body Text] He sat back.
[337 Body Text] “Your Miss Pike told you — I have read the file, I have a copy, do not ask how — that two hundred and nine doors opened and fourteen people happened to walk through. Happened. Miss Pike, that was the word, was it not.”
[338 Body Text] Miss Pike said, “It was.”
[339 Body Text] “It is a beautiful word and it is doing an enormous amount of work.” Eilstein’s voice was mild. “It says: the door opened for everyone, a few went through, and there is nothing more to explain, because that is what probability is. Six point seven per cent, a rate, a fact about nature. You will build a model with a transmission coefficient in it, and it will fit, and it will be correct, and you will have learned nothing whatever about the fourteen.”
[340 Body Text] Beatrix said, “Because?”
[341 Body Text] “Because a probability is a confession,” said Eilstein. “It is what one writes down in place of a reason. Sometimes that is all there is — better men than I have quarrelled about it for a century and the quarrel is not settled. But sometimes, Miss Pike, it is a pattern one has not found yet, and there is a way to tell the difference, and I have it.”
[342 Body Text] Fainrose said, “The waste.”
[343 Body Text] “The waste.” He nodded once. “This is the whole of my educated opinion, and it is worth what you paid for it, which is a chair and a hard question. If those fourteen had been chosen by chance, there would have been leftovers. Chance is expensive. It sheds discarded branches by the hundred — the version where Ellen Prosper stayed, the version where she went to Newcastle — all of it paid for, all of it mine.” He held up one thin hand. “There was nothing. Not one branch. So those fourteen were not selected. They were determined. No dice were thrown at any point, and that is why I starved.”
[344 Body Text] Lolly was on her feet.
[345 Body Text] “Then there’s a selection principle.”
[346 Body Text] “There is not a selection principle,” said Eilstein. “There is a reason. A selection principle is what you call a reason when you have found the correlation and not the mechanism. Determinism is not subtle, Miss Pike. If those fourteen were bound to go and the rest bound to stay, the fourteen have something in common that your register already records and nobody has looked at, because everybody has been counting rather than reading.”
[347 Body Text] Miss Pike said, “They were the last fourteen recovered from the horizon.”
[348 Body Text] “Yes, and that is a fact about your procedure, not about them,” said Eilstein. “It may matter. It probably matters. But it is not a reason, it is a filing detail, and you knew that at nine o’clock this morning which is why you did not like it.”
[349 Body Text] She looked at him.
[350 Body Text] Four minutes later she was back, opening a lever-arch file on the briefing table.
[351 Body Text] “Right,” she said. “Then let’s read them.”
[352 Body Text] They read them until twenty past one in the morning.
[353 Body Text] The fourteen were: a postgraduate place or a shop. Two marriages. An apprenticeship. A house purchase. A change of name. A reconciliation with a brother. A decision about a dog. A tenancy. A partnership dissolution. An operation, elective. A move to Canada. A career left unnamed in the file. And a girl of nineteen who had been deciding whether to make a complaint.
[354 Body Text] Beatrix found it first, which surprised nobody afterwards. She had spent nine years reading files for the one sentence somebody had hoped would go unread.
[355 Body Text] “Lolly,” she said. “Get the other hundred and ninety-five.”
[356 Body Text] “What have you got.”
[357 Body Text] “Get them first. I want to be wrong.”
[358 Body Text] They got them. It took an hour. And at the end of the hour Beatrix Sloan stood up at the end of the briefing table with her hands flat on a stack of paper and said:
[359 Body Text] “In every one of the fourteen, both permitted options had become impossible before the fourteenth of March. Not undesirable. Impossible.”
[360 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Show me.”
[361 Body Text] “Ellen Prosper. Postgraduate place or the mother’s shop.” Beatrix turned the sheet round. “The place was withdrawn in January of last year — the course was discontinued, it’s in the register as a note nobody actioned. And the shop’s lease was not renewed in the September. Both options were gone by the autumn. She had two doors and both of them had been bricked up and she was still, on the register, holding an unresolved state between them.”
[362 Body Text] She moved to the next.
[363 Body Text] “The apprenticeship: firm went into administration. The house purchase: the house burned down in the October, it is in the file, there is a photograph. The reconciliation with the brother: the brother died in February. The operation: the surgeon retired and the second option was a treatment that was withdrawn from licensing.” She went down the row, quickly, not enjoying it. “The dog died. The tenancy was demolished. The marriage — one of the two marriages — the man emigrated and the other option was a woman who had by then married somebody else. Both. In every case. Both.”
[364 Body Text] “And the hundred and ninety-five?” asked Lolly.
[365 Body Text] “At least one live option in every file I’ve checked,” said Beatrix. “I’ve done a hundred and forty so far. Every single one has somewhere it could still actually go.”
[366 Body Text] Albrecht Eilstein sat back in his chair and closed his eyes.
[367 Body Text] “There,” he said. “There it is. And no dice anywhere.”
[368 Body Text] Fainrose stood at the blackboard with the chalk unused. Lolly had learned to leave her alone at that stage.
[369 Body Text] After a while she said, “It’s not a selection principle. He’s right. It’s a barrier height.”
[370 Body Text] “Go on,” said Lolly.
[371 Body Text] “A pending state sits in a well, and the walls of the well are the permitted options, and the walls are only walls because the options are reachable.” She turned round. “Take away the reachability and you haven’t removed the walls. You’ve removed the floor. The register still says it must be one of two things, and nobody has updated the file, but physically there is nothing under it at all.”
[372 Body Text] Miss Pike said, “So the barrier—”
[373 Body Text] “The barrier for those fourteen was already almost nothing,” said Fainrose. “Not because they were remarkable. Because a register that we run had kept them in a state whose options no longer existed, for between five and fifteen months.” She turned back to the wall of traces. “Then on the fourteenth of March somebody lowered the price of nothing by eleven parts in ten to the twenty-second, for six hours, across the whole country, and every unresolved state was offered a door. For a hundred and ninety-five of them the door was a formality, because they still had somewhere real to be. And for fourteen of them—”
[374 Body Text] “—it was the only place left,” said Lolly.
[375 Body Text] “And so they went, and there was nothing discarded, because there was nothing else they could have done.” Fainrose’s voice was perfectly level and Lolly had never once heard her sound worse. “It was not chance. It was not selection. It was drainage.”
[376 Body Text] Eilstein opened his eyes.
[377 Body Text] “Dr Fainrose,” he said quietly. “Whose register was it.”
[378 Body Text] “Mine.”
[379 Body Text] “Yes.”
[380 Body Text] “We recovered three hundred and eight pending states from a burning horizon in under three months and we gave them back and we wrote them into a schedule of an Act, and we monitored them monthly, and we never once checked whether the options were still there.” Fainrose put the chalk down. “We froze them a second time. Not with a grid. With a filing system. And we have been publishing coherence traces for sixteen months describing how beautifully stable they were.”
[381 Body Text] Beatrix said, “Stephanie.”
[382 Body Text] “Don’t.”
[383 Body Text] “You did not lower the vacuum.”
[384 Body Text] “No,” said Fainrose. “Somebody else did that, and I intend to find out who, and I will be dealing with them shortly. But they only opened the doors. We built the rooms with no floors.”
[385 Body Text] It was nearly three. Somebody’s telephone was ringing in an office two doors down, and it rang out unanswered.
[386 Body Text] Eilstein got up, slowly, and put his coat over his arm.
[387 Body Text] “I shall go,” he said. “You have work and I am now merely furniture. But since I have been asked a question tonight and answered it, I am permitted one in return, and I shall use it badly, because I am old and sentimental about very few things.”
[388 Body Text] He looked at Miss Pike.
[389 Body Text] “The girl of nineteen,” he said. “The one who was deciding whether to make a complaint. Both her options impossible — I have not read that file, tell me why.”
[390 Body Text] Miss Pike found it.
[391 Body Text] “The person she would have complained about died in November,” she said. “And the body that would have received the complaint was abolished in a reorganisation in January and its functions were not transferred.”
[392 Body Text] “And where did she resolve to.”
[393 Body Text] Miss Pike checked, and stopped, and read it again.
[394 Body Text] “She’s a caseworker,” she said. “At a small charity in Dundee that helps people make complaints about institutions that no longer exist.”
[395 Body Text] Albrecht Eilstein stood in the doorway of the briefing room for a moment.
[396 Body Text] “You will want to know whether that is a comfort,” he said. “It is not. It is the same phenomenon. Water does not choose the drain, Miss Pike; the drain is simply where there is nothing holding it up. That young woman had one place in the whole of the state space that was not solid rock, and she is now in it, and she may be happy there, and she was never once consulted.”
[397 Body Text] He put on his hat.
[398 Body Text] “Find the ones with no floor,” he said. “There will be a great many outside your register and nobody is watching a single one of them. And find out who is lowering the price of nothing, because whoever it is has now demonstrated that they can do it for six hours across a planet, and the interesting question is not what they were trying to move.” He glanced back. “It is that they left almost everybody standing where they were. Which is either extraordinary restraint, or it means they were not aiming at us at all, and we were merely in the shadow of something.”
[399 Body Text] He went out past the front desk and Lolly, watching from the landing, did not see him leave the lobby, in the manner of a thing that had not, strictly, been present.
[400 Body Text] On the stair afterwards Gideon was waiting with two teas and an apology he had rehearsed on the landing and did not, in the end, need.
[401 Body Text] “You went the colour of an unsigned form,” he said. “I don’t know what he eats. I know the face you make when a number is wrong and it was said by someone you were using as a fixed point.”
[402 Body Text] “He isn’t wrong.”
[403 Body Text] “Then the fixed point moved.” He held the mug out until she took it. Neither of them made a speech of it. Downstairs a telephone rang, and did not matter yet.
[404 Body Text] Lolly opened the notebook on the third-floor landing and wrote, in the poor handwriting of someone who had been awake for twenty hours:
[405 Body Text] Chance leaves waste. 
There was no waste. 
Therefore there was no chance.
[406 Body Text] And beneath it, underlined:
[407 Body Text] Fourteen rooms with no floor. 
We built them. Monthly. For sixteen months. With a form.
[408 Body Text] Gideon had driven back to Cheltenham before light and cleaned the earth, as he had said he would. In the morning the six hours were still in the traces. He had been right about both halves of the sentence, and he had not required her to say so.
[409 Heading 2] Chapter Four
[410 Heading 3] The Same Holiday, Described Twice
[411 First Paragraph] The letter had been in the department’s general correspondence tray for five weeks, because it was handwritten, and because it began Dear Sir or Madam, I am a travel agent, and because the graduate on general correspondence had a system for handwritten letters: they went to the bottom.
[412 Body Text] Lolly found it only by reading the general tray herself on Sunday mornings, having concluded in March that the previous administration’s chief failure had not been malice but sorting.
[413 Body Text] The letter said:
[414 Body Text] Dear Sir or Madam,
[415 Body Text] I am a travel agent. I have been a travel agent for twenty-two years, at Kind & Farrow of Croydon, which was my father’s before it was mine.
[416 Body Text] I am writing about your Statement of Coherence for the period ending 31 March, which I obtained from the Stationery Office, and about paragraph 14, in which you state that the fourteen anomalous resolutions “moved to a state outside the permitted options”.
[417 Body Text] I do not think anybody moved.
[418 Body Text] I think somebody altered the description, and I think the description is the thing, and I think this is not a philosophical remark. I enclose fifteen pages. They are not written in the correct notation and I apologise for that. I did not go to university.
[419 Body Text] If the fifteen pages are wrong I should be grateful to be told why, as I have been unable to find anybody to tell me, and I have been carrying it about for four years and it has begun to affect my sleep.
[420 Body Text] Yours faithfully,
[421 Body Text] Susie Kind (Mrs)
[422 Body Text] The fifteen pages were in biro.
[423 Body Text] ∗
[424 First Paragraph] Gideon Wigglesworth went to Larchfield House at three that Sunday with a biscuit tin of capacitors under one arm. He was not there to be kind. He was there to mend things, and to be shouted at for mending them wrong, and he had come to understand that this was the more useful of the two.
[425 Body Text] Tomasz signed him in. Dennis Wren was in the chair nearest the radiator with a Roberts radio on the card table in front of him, its back off, and a council envelope beside it, unopened, with a tea ring on it.
[426 Body Text] “You’re late,” said Dennis.
[427 Body Text] “It’s three.”
[428 Body Text] “I know what time it is. I’ve been sat here since two looking at this.” He pushed the radio an inch towards Gideon. “Battery in backwards. Woman in room nine. Then she tried a different battery, also backwards, which takes commitment. Now it hisses. Go on, tell me what it is, and then I’ll tell you why you’re wrong.”
[429 Body Text] Gideon sat down on the low chair, which put his knees somewhere near his ears, and looked. He looked for a long time, because Dennis liked to be kept waiting the way some men like to be kept warm.
[430 Body Text] “Reverse-polarity diode’s gone short,” he said eventually. “It did its job. It died for the radio.”
[431 Body Text] “Ha.” Dennis was pleased. “Good lad. Customer kills the diode and comes in saying the set’s dead. Never once has he pointed at the right bit.” He handed over the side-cutters as though passing the sacrament. “Snip it. Let’s hear her first.”
[432 Body Text] Gideon snipped it. The radio, given a fresh battery the right way round, found Radio 4 halfway through a man explaining compost.
[433 Body Text] “There,” said Dennis. “Lovely.” He listened to the compost with deep satisfaction for a while. Then, without looking away from the dial: “Read us that, would you. My eyes are all right. It’s my patience.”
[434 Body Text] Gideon opened the envelope. RESIDENTIAL CARE: SERVICE RECONFIGURATION. A date at the end of January. A review in the autumn. A paragraph about best interests, and another about choice, and a table with two rows: Elmhurst Court, Croydon (nursing); Larchfield, first floor (secure nursing).
[435 Body Text] He read it out exactly as written, because he did not know how else to read things.
[436 Body Text] Dennis listened to the end. “Which one’s got the window?”
[437 Body Text] “Neither.”
[438 Body Text] “No.” He nodded, as if a meter had confirmed what his ear had already told him. “I’m not daft, son. I know what a form’s for. It’s for when they’ve decided already and they want you to do the writing.” He reached out and turned the compost down. “Does Lol know?”
[439 Body Text] “Sandra told her. In March.”
[440 Body Text] “Then she’ll be taking it apart.” He said it with pride and without comfort. “She takes things apart to see what they are. Always did. My good multimeter at nine; I came in and she’d labelled every part with a bit of masking tape and a question. Never could put herself back on the bench after, though. She thinks if she knows how a thing works she’s done something about it.” He looked at Gideon sideways. “You courting her?”
[441 Body Text] Gideon considered the question with the care he gave any question with a live chassis in it.
[442 Body Text] “No,” he said. “I’m the one who stays at the bench.”
[443 Body Text] “Good,” said Dennis. “She needs one of them more than the other.” And then, more quietly, in the voice that came and went: “In March. I had a funny night. Did she tell you?”
[444 Body Text] “She told me you heard a door.”
[445 Body Text] “I did hear a door.” Dennis frowned at the radio. “I know there wasn’t one. I knew it at eight. But I heard it, Gideon, the way you hear a fuse go in another room. Not the bang. The quiet. Like the house had more room in it all of a sudden.” He tapped the card table. “I’ve been in this chair four years and I know every noise this building makes, and that one wasn’t on the list.”
[446 Body Text] Gideon wrote nothing down. He would remember it the way he remembered a reading that should not have been there.
[447 Body Text] At five, when the compost had become the news, Tomasz came over with a kettle held at arm’s length, as one carries something that has bitten.
[448 Body Text] “Lounge kettle,” he said. “Trips the circuit. Has done since the night your Mr Wren made tea at three o’clock.”
[449 Body Text] “Give it here,” said Dennis, before Gideon could. “No. Give it him. He’s got the van.” He looked at Gideon with an expression of terrible authority. “Earth leakage. I’ll bet you a pound. Bring it back working on Sunday or don’t come.”
[450 Body Text] Gideon took the kettle to the car park, put it on the passenger seat, and secured it with the seat belt, because it seemed only fair. Then he drove to Lolly’s, where the pages from Croydon were already all over the kitchen table, and sat at the other end of it rewiring the kettle. He put the tea where her hand would find it and did not mention the door. She had a face on that said she was already holding one thing too many.
[451 Body Text] Lolly read them once at the kitchen table and then again standing up, and at a quarter to eight the next morning she telephoned Fainrose.
[452 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Is somebody dead.”
[453 Body Text] “How much information can you fit in a room.”
[454 Body Text] There was a pause.
[455 Body Text] “Who’s asking.”
[456 Body Text] “A travel agent in Croydon.”
[457 Body Text] “Read me a sentence.”
[458 Body Text] Lolly read her the fourth page.
[459 Body Text] A holiday is not a place. It is a set of relations — what you see from the window, what time the light comes, how long it takes to reach the water, who is next to you at dinner. I have sold the identical holiday in two different hotels in two different countries and had both parties come home and describe the same fortnight. This used to embarrass me. I now think it is the most important thing I know. If two arrangements produce the same set of relations, they are not two arrangements. There is no further fact about which one you were in.
[460 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Get her in.”
[461 Body Text] ∗
[462 First Paragraph] Stephanie Fainrose went home that evening to the Institute for Unhelpful Clarity, which employed one person, and read her sixteen monthly Statements of Coherence the way she would have read a stranger’s, looking for the paragraph in which the author first knew something and decided not to say it.
[463 Body Text] There was no such paragraph. That was the worst of it. She had not known. Every month for sixteen months the department had measured, beautifully, whether three hundred and eight states were stable, and nobody had asked whether a state can be stable because it has nowhere left to go. A stone at the bottom of a well is very stable. She had put that sentence in a lecture once, to undergraduates, as a joke.
[464 Body Text] She issued herself a written warning, in green ink, for negligence in the matter of floors, and filed it in the folder marked PERSONNEL, which until that evening had contained only her own contract and a note from her dentist. It was the Institute’s first disciplinary action in four years. Its staff took it badly.
[465 Body Text] Then, because self-reproach is a luxury and she had always distrusted luxuries, she wrote to Mrs Susie Kind of Kind & Farrow, Croydon, asking her to come to Marsham Street on Wednesday at ten. She began a second letter, to Mrs Elspeth Chain, got as far as Dear Mrs Chain, I am writing to, and left it under the stack, unsigned.
[466 Body Text] ∗
[467 First Paragraph] On the Tuesday, before Mrs Kind could be got in, Mrs Elspeth Chain came up without an appointment and sat in the visitors’ chair from ten. Nobody moved her.
[468 Body Text] “Paragraph fourteen,” she said. The voice was low and dry and did not need the corridor to be quieter. “Fourteen people, moved to a state outside the permitted options. I had a sister moved outside a description. This building did not call that a resolution. I have come about the word.”
[469 Body Text] Lolly brought the file down. She did not touch the trace. She crossed out resolved on the cover, where someone in March had written it, and clipped a card where the word had been: Not resolved. Speak to her. Not to the trace. Three floors up, the Chain file still said Pending. Not lost. Not finished. across its cover, in the same hand.
[470 Body Text] “That will do until it doesn’t,” said Mrs Chain. “Send me the page if the word comes back.” She took the umbrella and went down to the street. She did not stay for the theory. She had stayed for one of those already.
[471 Body Text] Mrs Susie Kind arrived on the Wednesday at ten, in a good coat, with a wheeled document case and the air of a woman braced for the kindly, hurried explanation of why she had wasted her time. She was fifty-eight. She apologised twice for the case.
[472 Body Text] “I want to say at the start that I know what I sound like,” she said. “I have a brochure business and a strong opinion about the universe. I am aware of the type.”
[473 Body Text] “Mrs Kind,” said Fainrose, “I have read your fifteen pages four times. Two of your steps are wrong and one of them is wrong in an interesting way. Sit down. What made you start?”
[474 Body Text] Mrs Kind put the case on the table and did not open it.
[475 Body Text] “Baggage,” she said.
[476 Body Text] “Baggage.”
[477 Body Text] “In nineteen ninety-nine I began keeping a note of the complaints, because complaints are the only honest data a travel agent gets. Nobody writes in to say the week was exactly as advertised.” She folded her hands. “And after some years I noticed that I could predict the complaint from the booking form. From the form. From how much of the form the client had filled in and which boxes they had left as no preference.
[478 Body Text] “A client who specifies everything will complain about the one thing she forgot. A client who specifies nothing will come home content, and will not be able to tell you the name of the town.” She looked up. “And I thought: the holiday I am selling is not in Spain. It is on that form. The form has an area and everything the client will ever be able to say about the fortnight has to fit in those boxes. If it isn’t on the form it doesn’t happen to them. It happens near them.”
[479 Body Text] Miss Pike, who had come in halfway through with coffee for four and had not sat down, said, “That’s a bound.”
[480 Body Text] “I beg your pardon?”
[481 Body Text] “You’ve described a bound. The amount that can be true inside the holiday is limited by the size of the surface you wrote it on.”
[482 Body Text] Fainrose did the blackboard, as Fainrose always did, but she did it slowly and she kept turning round.
[483 Body Text] “Here is what Mrs Kind has arrived at from lost luggage,” she said, “and what a small number of physicists arrived at from black holes, and I want the department to notice that she got there with a biro.”
[484 Body Text] She drew a box.
[485 Body Text] “The instinct is that the amount of stuff you can put in a region goes as its volume. Twice the shelving, twice the books. It is the most obvious thing in the world and it is false.” She wrote:
[486 Source Code] Sₘₐₓ = k_B c³A / (4Għ)
[487 Body Text] “The maximum information in a region of space is set by the area of its boundary, not by the volume inside. One unit of entropy for every four Planck areas, which comes to one bit for every two point eight. Not approximately area. Area!” She tapped the box. “Which means that everything that can be true inside this room is already written on the walls, and there is no room on the walls for a second, independent story about the interior. The interior is not an extra thing. It is a way of reading the walls.”
[488 Body Text] Lolly said, “Wittenberg.”
[489 Body Text] “Wittenberg told us this in a reading room in Coldharrow and we wrote it down as a nice remark about the ledger.” Fainrose laid the chalk in the tray. “The ledger is on the horizon. It is the architecture. Four C’s surface was the contents, and the interior was the same information described in a way that lets you walk about in it.”
[490 Body Text] “Two descriptions,” said Mrs Kind. “One of them has three dimensions and gravity and takes a week. The other one is flat, and has no gravity in it at all, and is just as complete.”
[491 Body Text] “Yes.”
[492 Body Text] “Then may I say the thing I have come to say,” said Mrs Kind, “because I have been sitting on it since the letter and I would rather be wrong out loud.”
[493 Body Text] “Please.”
[494 Body Text] Mrs Susie Kind opened the document case.
[495 Body Text] “Your fourteen did not travel,” she said. “There was nowhere to travel to. Your register says a pending state must resolve to A or B, and you searched the interior for a third room and concluded it was outside the description. It was outside your reading of the description. Somebody rewrote the surface, and the interior came out differently, and from inside that looks like fourteen people going somewhere they could not have gone. There is no other fact.”
[496 Body Text] “Prove it,” said Fainrose, not unkindly. “You have understood something. Now make it cost you a prediction.”
[497 Body Text] “I have one.” Mrs Kind’s hands were shaking slightly and her voice was not. “You measured that on the fourteenth of March nothing became cheaper by eleven parts in ten to the twenty-second, and you have been asking who was pushing.”
[498 Body Text] “We have.”
[499 Body Text] “Nobody was pushing. There was less written on the wall.” She looked round the table. “A fully subscribed surface is what the ordinary vacuum looks like from inside. Clear a little of the surface and, for as long as it stays under-subscribed, the interior is slack. Nothing is supplying energy. It is an absence of bookkeeping.”
[500 Body Text] The room was extremely quiet.
[501 Body Text] Lolly said, “Say the number.”
[502 Body Text] “I cannot do the number. I have done the shape.” She turned a page round. “Ramp up, plateau, ramp down. Your Statement has it. That is not a wave passing. That is a deletion and a rewrite. I have been in the back office for four booking-system migrations, Ms Wren. Ninety minutes to release the records, three very strange hours, ninety minutes of matching. Same shape, same operation. Somebody was doing an overnight run on the surface of this country.”
[503 Body Text] Fainrose worked for nine hours with Miss Pike and a numerical analyst on secondment and came back at nine that evening with a single sheet.
[504 Body Text] “Eleven parts in ten to the twenty-second,” she said, “over the volume the four instruments were sensitive to, corresponds to a surface deficit of two point one times ten to the fifty-eight Planck areas, plus or minus a factor of two, and I want to be clear that the factor of two is doing a great deal of work.”
[505 Body Text] Beatrix said, “In English.”
[506 Body Text] “Something under ten to the fifty-eight bits. Which is a large number and a useless one until you ask what it is the size of.” Fainrose put the sheet down. “It is, to within the factor of two, the surface information content of the fourteen.”
[507 Body Text] “Of the fourteen people?” asked Lolly.
[508 Body Text] “Of the fourteen pending states as they stood on the thirteenth of March,” said Fainrose. “Which is a much smaller thing than fourteen people, and that is the horror of it. A pending state with two live options carries both options, and the relations between them, and the person’s correlations with everybody waiting on the answer. It is a fat record. A pending state whose options are both gone carries almost nothing: two dead branches and a name.” She looked at the traces on the plasterboard. “Those fourteen were the thinnest records on the surface of this country. We had spent sixteen months making them thin.”
[509 Body Text] Mrs Kind said, quietly, “They were overwritten.”
[510 Body Text] “They were reassigned,” said Fainrose. “They are not gone. Ellen Prosper is on a survey vessel out of Peterhead and is by all accounts extremely well. But the version of her that was pending — the one with the postgraduate place and the shop and the sixteen months of not deciding — that was not resolved. It was the space that was used.”
[511 Body Text] “And they resolved to something outside the permitted options,” said Lolly slowly, “because when you rewrite a surface you do not care what the previous tenant’s options were.”
[512 Body Text] “No. You care about the area.”
[513 Body Text] It was Mrs Kind who asked the question that put the matter in the file, and she asked it apologetically, at twenty to ten, having twice refused to go home.
[514 Body Text] “Dr Fainrose,” she said, “who made the booking?”
[515 Body Text] Fainrose said, “I’m sorry?”
[516 Body Text] “You keep saying somebody cleared the surface, and somebody wrote. In my trade that is two people, never one.” Mrs Kind picked up the case. “There is the airline, which has the aircraft and does not care who is on it. And there is somebody like me, who knows which boxes on the form the airline actually reads. A thing that can clear two times ten to the fifty-eight Planck areas of the Earth’s surface is an airline. It does not know what Croydon is. It did not pick those fourteen out of tenderness for the thinnest records.”
[517 Body Text] “Then who did,” said Lolly.
