[518 Body Text] “Somebody who could read the register,” said Mrs Susie Kind. “Somebody who knew which fourteen records in this country had been reduced to a name and two dead branches, because somebody had been publishing that monthly for sixteen months.” She did up her coat. “I am not accusing anyone in this room. But I have been in a great many meetings where the disaster was arranged by a person who was only providing information, and I should like to say out loud, before I get my train, that your Statement of Coherence is a picking list.”
[519 Body Text] She got to the door and stopped.
[520 Body Text] “You will want the other thing,” she said. “You will have worked out from the shape when it will come round again.”
[521 Body Text] “The ninth of February,” Miss Pike said.
[522 Body Text] “Then you have until the ninth of February to make everybody fat,” said Mrs Kind. “It is the only defence I can see. A record with live options in it is expensive to clear. Give them somewhere real they could still go. Even one. Especially the ones nobody is counting.” She glanced at Lolly. “We abolished choices for sixteen months because it made the monthly return easier to compile.”
[523 Body Text] Lolly said, “Mrs Kind. Would you come and work here.”
[524 Body Text] She thought about it with complete seriousness, for about as long as it took the kettle to click.
[525 Body Text] “No,” she said. “But I will come on Thursdays. You have nobody who reads forms for a living and it shows.” Thursday was early closing at Kind & Farrow, and had been since her father’s day; she had spent twenty-two years of Thursday afternoons, she said, waiting for something worth closing for.
[526 Body Text] Albrecht Eilstein telephoned at half past eleven, which he had never done, and would not say how he had the number.
[527 Body Text] “They have told you about the wall,” he said.
[528 Body Text] “A travel agent told us about the wall.”
[529 Body Text] “Of course she did.” A sound that might have been satisfaction. “I am still thin, Ms Wren, so I will say one thing. Tengelman will be in the newspapers within a fortnight explaining that, since on this account nothing is ever destroyed, there is no wrong to remedy. And he will be correct, and it will be the most successful lie of the decade. I eat the difference, and the fourteenth of March did not taste like destruction. It tasted like nothing at all — and that is worse, because a thing that leaves no leavings has not thrown anything away. It has taken it with it.”
[530 Body Text] Lolly wrote, at midnight:
[531 Body Text] They took the thinnest records because they were the cheapest to clear. 
We made them thin. On purpose. With a form. Monthly.
[532 Body Text] And last:
[533 Body Text] 9 February. 
Make everybody fat. 
Somewhere real for each of them to go.
[534 Body Text] ∗
[535 First Paragraph] Gideon walked her to the bus. It was late, and he had the look of a man carrying something he had not been asked to carry.
[536 Body Text] “Fat records,” he said, at the stop.
[537 Body Text] “I know.”
[538 Body Text] “Live options. Somewhere real they could still go.” He was looking at the timetable, not at her, which was how he said difficult things. “Sunday he asked me which room had the window. Twice. He’s frightened of the upstairs, Lol. He doesn’t say it. He says the upstairs has bad light for soldering.”
[539 Body Text] She had kept that knowledge out of the notebook. Written down, it would require something of her.
[540 Body Text] “The form’s got two boxes,” she said. “Neither of them has the window.”
[541 Body Text] “Then it’s a thin record.”
[542 Body Text] “Don’t.”
[543 Body Text] “I’m not being clever. I’m saying what she said. You spent all evening working out how to keep three hundred and eight strangers fat. I’m saying your dad needs the same.”
[544 Body Text] The bus came. She did not get on it. It went.
[545 Body Text] “I’m frightened of the wrong thing,” she said, to the empty road. “I’m frightened of the form. I should be frightened of the morning he doesn’t ask about the window any more.”
[546 Body Text] Gideon did not touch her shoulder. He stood a careful foot away, the way he stood at the bench when someone else’s hands were in the chassis, and when the next bus came he said, “That one’s yours,” and she got on it, and he stayed at the stop until it turned the corner, which she saw, because she was looking back.
[547 Body Text] She took the notebook out on the top deck and, under Somewhere real for each of them to go, wrote: Dad. The window. Start there.
[548 Heading 2] Chapter Five
[549 Heading 3] Mrs Kind Reads the Footnotes
[550 First Paragraph] The Galactic Counsel is not called that because of any galaxy. It is called that because in 1961 a clerk in the Cabinet Office was handed a treaty instrument in a language nobody could read and asked to file it, and he filed it under Counsel, meaning advice, and the misspelling entered the schedule, and the schedule entered the Act, and by the time anybody noticed there had been eleven cross-references and it was easier to leave it.
[551 Body Text] Lolly learned this from Mrs Kind, on a Thursday, because Mrs Kind read footnotes.
[552 Body Text] “It’s a body of advisers, not a council of worlds,” she said. “That’s the whole difference and everyone gets it wrong including them. It has no army and no territory and no money of its own. What it has is the subscription schedule, and forty-one signatory instruments, and a budget line.”
[553 Body Text] “And how do you know that?”
[554 Body Text] “Because it is a travel document,” said Mrs Kind. “It is the only interstellar instrument in the Stationery Office and it costs eleven pounds forty and nobody has ever bought a copy but me.”
[555 Body Text] ∗
[556 First Paragraph] On the Friday, Mrs Susie Kind opened the shop at nine, as she had every weekday since her father’s stroke in 2003, and turned the sign, and put the kettle on, and took the treaty instrument out of her handbag and laid it on the desk beside the brochures, between Lanzarote and the Lakes.
[557 Body Text] Kind & Farrow occupied a narrow unit between a nail bar and a shop that had been about to open for two years. Farrow had left in 1986 with the Tenerife deposits and a woman from the bank, and had been on the sign ever since, because repainting cost more than forgiveness and her father had never been a man to pay over the odds for either.
[558 Body Text] The instrument was a hundred and four pages long and smelled of the Stationery Office, which is to say of nothing, very expensively. She read it the way she read a tour operator’s terms: from the back. The back was where people kept the things they hoped you would be too tired to reach. She had found Article 2 in the first week, and the subscription schedule in the second, and in the third she had found the clause about records and lodgement and had sat with it for an afternoon, not because she understood what it meant for stars, but because she knew exactly what it meant for bookings. When a company is wound up, the aircraft go to auction. The passenger list goes to whoever bought the company.
[559 Body Text] At ten to ten the woman came in.
[560 Body Text] She came in most Fridays now. Her name was Delia Price and her sister was being buried in Inverness the following Friday, and she had a hundred and sixty pounds, and a hip, and a son who had said he would sort it and had not. The computer wanted three hundred and twelve pounds for the cheapest return that did not involve a night on a bench in Perth.
[561 Body Text] “I’m sorry,” said Mrs Price, as she always did, before she had sat down.
[562 Body Text] “Don’t be sorry. Be specific.” Mrs Kind turned the screen so they could both see it. “What time do you have to be in the church?”
[563 Body Text] “Eleven.”
[564 Body Text] “And when do you want to be home?”
[565 Body Text] Mrs Price looked at her as if no one had asked her that in several years. “I’d like to stay the night with my niece. If I could. If it’s not silly.”
[566 Body Text] “It isn’t silly. It’s a preference. People always leave out the preference, and then they wonder why the trip’s a misery.” Mrs Kind was already typing. “The machine has two answers for you, and both of them are wrong, because the machine was built by somebody who thinks you’re going to Inverness to look at Inverness. You’re not. You’re going to stand next to your niece at eleven next Friday and then sleep in her spare room. That’s a different holiday.”
[567 Body Text] It took her forty minutes. A coach to Glasgow on the Thursday, booked direct with a firm in Paisley that did not appear on any system because its owner disapproved of systems. A rail fare from Glasgow on a ticket that existed only if you asked for it by name. A return on the Saturday, after the spare room, on an advance fare released at midnight that the machine had not yet noticed. A hundred and fifty-eight pounds. Mrs Kind charged nothing for the booking and two pounds for the stamp, because a person who is given something entirely free assumes it will not work.
[568 Body Text] When Mrs Price had gone, Mrs Kind sat for a while with her hands flat on the desk.
[569 Body Text] The machine had offered two options, and both of them had been impossible for the woman in front of it, and the machine had not known. Nobody had told it anything except a destination and a price. The woman had come in holding both of them, unable to choose, the way the fourteen had held their two dead doors on the department’s register. And if Mrs Kind had not been there to ask what time she wanted to be home, she would have sat on that, Mrs Kind thought, and sat on it, and sat on it, until something cheap enough came past.
[570 Body Text] That was why she would not go and work in Marsham Street. It was not modesty. Somebody had to be on the Tuesday side of the counter, where the problem was still a person and had not yet become a record.
[571 Body Text] She picked up the treaty instrument again and turned to the last page, where the misfiling of 1961 had been preserved in amber by its cross-references. Counsel. Advice. A body of advisers, whose only asset was a passenger list of every thin place in the galaxy.
[572 Body Text] “Who made the booking,” she said aloud, to the empty shop, and wrote it in pencil in the margin, and underlined it, and went to put the kettle on again.
[573 Body Text] ∗
[574 First Paragraph] The papers came through Eilstein, who had observer status, which he described as “the status of a creature too old to exclude and too disreputable to consult.”
[575 Body Text] There were three of them.
[576 Body Text] The first was an agenda for the two hundred and fourth ordinary session, item 9(c).
[577 Body Text] The second was a briefing note, four pages, from something called the Office of the President.
[578 Body Text] The third was a costed proposal for the discontinuation of the Galactic Health Support Programme with effect from the end of the current subscription year.
[579 Body Text] Beatrix Sloan read all three on the Tube from Gray’s Inn and arrived at Marsham Street at twenty past eight in the morning without her coat, which she had left on the Circle line.
[580 Body Text] “Lolly,” she said. “What is the Galactic Health Support Programme.”
[581 Body Text] “I have no idea. Until this morning I did not know there was a galactic anything.”
[582 Body Text] “It is forty-one signatories paying into a common fund,” said Beatrix, “and the fund does exactly one thing, and it has done it since before the treaty was in English, and it is described in the founding instrument in a phrase I have now read nineteen times.” She put the paper down and read it out. “The maintenance of full subscription upon local screens within the signatory volume.”
[583 Body Text] Fainrose, who had come in behind her and stopped in the doorway, said: “Say that again.”
[584 Body Text] Beatrix read it again, slowly, with a finger under each word.
[585 Body Text] Fainrose sat down on the edge of the table.
[586 Body Text] “They keep the walls written on,” she said.
[587 Body Text] It took four days and Mrs Kind’s document case to establish what the Programme actually did. The Programme did not describe itself in physics. It described itself in accountancy, which was worse, and then in an annex, which was fatal.
[588 Body Text] The mechanism was this.
[589 Body Text] A screen — a horizon, a boundary, any surface across which the interior is encoded — has a capacity, and it has a load. The vacuum inside is what a fully subscribed screen looks like from within. When a screen falls below full subscription, the interior goes slack: the price of nothing drops, barriers thin, and everything that proceeds by tunnelling proceeds faster.
[590 Body Text] Screens do not stay full by themselves. Information gets consolidated, records get thinned, tidy administrations reduce fat descriptions to names and dead branches, and slowly, across a volume of space, the walls develop gaps.
[591 Body Text] And so, since a date the instrument gave only as the first arrangement, forty-one signatories had paid a small annual sum into a common fund whose entire purpose was to keep the walls of the local galaxy written on. Not to add anything. Not to build. To maintain subscription. To go round the volume topping up screens that had gone slack, in the way that a very old and very unglamorous water company spends its whole existence keeping the pressure up in pipes nobody thinks about.
[592 Body Text] Annex C listed the consequences of under-subscription in ascending order of expense. There were nine. Lolly read them at her desk with the door shut.
[593 Body Text] Item four was anomalous resolution of pending states within the biological estate.
[594 Body Text] Item seven was premature ignition of stellar cores.
[595 Body Text] They got Priya Ramanathan down from Cheltenham on the Sunday and she brought a graduate student and four boxes and did not sleep.
[596 Body Text] “Right,” she said on Monday morning. “Item seven. I want to be careful, because I have been up for thirty hours and I would rather be slow than wrong.”
[597 Body Text] “Be slow,” said Fainrose.
[598 Body Text] “A star does not burn because it is hot enough. It is nowhere near hot enough.” Ramanathan put a plot up. “Two protons in a stellar core repel each other, and classically they never touch, not at fifteen million kelvin. Fusion happens only by tunnelling, and the tunnelling probability is exponential in the barrier, which means it is exponential in everything.” She looked up. “Stars are the most sensitive amplifiers of vacuum conditions in nature, and nobody ever needed to know that, because the vacuum had never changed.”
[599 Body Text] Lolly said, “Give me the number.”
[600 Body Text] “Eleven parts in ten to the twenty-second, off the floor.” Ramanathan’s mouth did something unpleasant. “In the biological estate, over six hours, that produced fourteen anomalies out of two hundred and nine, and it took Miss Pike two days of trace work to see it. In a stellar core, over six hours, with ten to the fifty-six participating nuclei and a Gamow factor in the exponent, that is not a small effect. That is a core running roughly two per cent fast for six hours.”
[601 Body Text] “Which does what?”
[602 Body Text] “To a young star, nothing at all. It rings for a century and settles.” Ramanathan turned the page. “To a core within a few thousand years of its own limit — a core already into its last burning stages and running out of fuel — two per cent for six hours is enough to take it over. Not enough to cause a supernova. Enough to advance one. I should be honest about the size. On a stellar clock, six hours of burning slightly faster is a rounding error, and a rounding error would not matter. What matters is that a core that late in its life is a stack of thresholds, each stage lighting the next, and a push at the wrong moment tips it over one early, and everything after inherits the head start. I can give you the sign with confidence. The size comes from my models, and a model is a model. The distinction between causing and advancing will be the entire argument at the session and I want the department to be very clear about it before we go, because the President’s brief has already found it.”
[603 Body Text] Beatrix said, “Show me the President’s brief.”
[604 Body Text] The brief from the Office of the President ran to four pages and Lolly came to think of it, afterwards, as the most accomplished document she had ever read.
[605 Body Text] It made three arguments and it did not raise its voice once.
[606 Body Text] The first was that the Programme could not demonstrate a benefit. Subscription maintenance is invisible when it works; there is no counterfactual galaxy to compare with; forty-one signatories had been paying since before writing for an absence of events. The Programme’s own performance indicator is that nothing occurred. The Office notes that nothing has also occurred in volumes where no subscription was maintained. This was untrue, but it was untrue in a way that required nine months of work to demonstrate, and the session was in three weeks.
[607 Body Text] The second was that the Programme’s costs fell disproportionately on the paying signatories while its benefits accrued to the unsubscribed volume. The Office does not consider it equitable that the responsible parties should indefinitely underwrite the untidiness of others.
[608 Body Text] The third argument was the one that made Fainrose put the paper down and go and stand at the window.
[609 Body Text] The Office draws the session’s attention to the settled position that information within the signatory volume is conserved. The Office is advised that no process occurring at any screen destroys information, and that all apparent loss is a change of description rather than a change of content. The Office therefore finds it difficult to identify the harm which the Programme exists to prevent. In a unitary volume there is no such thing as damage. There is only re-encoding.
[610 Body Text] And in a footnote:
[611 Body Text] The Office is grateful to Professor M. Tengelman for his assistance in the preparation of this paragraph.
[612 Body Text] “He’s not lying,” said Fainrose. She had been at the window for twenty minutes. “That is the thing I want everybody to sit with before we go to Vienna. Tengelman has not lied. Information is conserved. The surface is complete. Everything on that page is true and it is being used to abolish the only thing standing between this galaxy and a great many advanced supernovae, and the reason it works is that we spent nineteen months teaching everybody that nothing is ever really lost because it made our own conduct look better.”
[613 Body Text] Lolly said, “Then we need an argument that a conserved thing can still be a harm.”
[614 Body Text] “Yes.”
[615 Body Text] “Have we got one?”
[616 Body Text] “Beatrix has got one,” said Fainrose, “and it is the best legal argument I have ever seen and it is going to require us to stand up in front of forty-one signatories and say that our own defence from the last case was rubbish.”
[617 Body Text] Crispin Vale came up from Woking on the train he could still get a seat on, in the very good suit, and waited in the corridor until Gideon went out to see who it was. Gideon knew him at once. He had last seen that suit on the Annex stairs at twenty to six in the morning, going down.
[618 Body Text] Vale sat. He was greyer than he had been at the Annex and thinner in the face, and he still had the stillness of a man who has signed something. Lolly had been carrying his letter since the night at the barrier and had not yet had a day that belonged to it. This was the day.
[619 Body Text] “I signed the second page without reading it,” he said. “You have that in a finding already. I then spent eleven days saying so, and they let me go home, which was decent and was not the same as finished. Paragraph fourteen is the same method. Two options on a form, and anything else called a breach. I will not sign a paper that says those fourteen were never anyone’s loss. I came to ask you to minute that I was asked, and that I refused.”
[620 Body Text] “You weren’t asked,” said Lolly.
[621 Body Text] “Then minute that I came anyway,” said Vale. “The second version is harder to misfile.”
[622 Body Text] She wrote it on the minute while he watched, and she read it back. She did not offer forgiveness. He had not asked for any. He made the train.
[623 Body Text] ∗
[624 First Paragraph] Crispin Vale got the 6:52 back to Woking and, for the first time since the Annex, did not take out the crossword.
[625 Body Text] He sat by the window with his hands on his knees and watched south London go past in the dusk, back gardens and lit kitchens and a man on a ladder fixing a gutter by torchlight, and thought about the second page.
[626 Body Text] He had told the story so many times now — to the inquiry, to his wife, to the vicar, to a very young journalist who had cried — that it had worn smooth, like a step. I put my name to the second page and did not read it. It was true. It was also, he had begun to suspect, a way of not saying the other thing, which was that he had not read it because he had not wanted to know what was on it. A man who does not read the page is not innocent of the page. He is only innocent of reading.
[627 Body Text] Today he had read paragraph fourteen. He had read it on the train up, twice, and it had been the same method, and he had known it was the same method by the second line, and he had felt something he had not felt since the Annex, which was the wish to be useful rather than forgiven.
[628 Body Text] Ms Wren had not forgiven him. He had watched her not do it, and had been grateful. She had written down that he came. He had given her the reason without planning to, and it had been the best sentence he had spoken since the Annex.
[629 Body Text] At Surbiton a woman got on with a pushchair and could not get it up the step, and Crispin Vale, who had once had a driver and a department and a signature that moved districts, got up and lifted the front wheels for her without being asked, and sat down again, and found that his hands were shaking slightly, and did not mind.
[630 Body Text] At home his wife asked how it had gone.
[631 Body Text] “I refused something,” he said. “In writing. It’s on the minute.”
[632 Body Text] She looked at him over her glasses, the way she had not looked at him in a while.
[633 Body Text] “Good,” she said, and put the kettle on.
[634 Heading 2] Chapter Six
[635 Heading 3] The Motion Was Carried
[636 First Paragraph] On the way to the session she found a page in the notebook she had not written. Gideon’s pencil, blunt, and slightly behind the thought: If they say nothing was lost, ask what the screen is made of. I can build a small one. I cannot build a galaxy. Don’t let them talk you into pretending those are the same job.
[637 Body Text] She read it twice. The second time she was checking that he was still speaking to her in the voice he used when he was frightened for her and would rather be useful. She put the page back. It was where she kept the things she was not ready to call by a larger name.
[638 Body Text] The two hundred and fourth ordinary session was held in a building off the Ringstrasse that had been a bank, and the Counsel sat at a horseshoe table under a ceiling that had been painted for something else entirely.
[639 Body Text] It smelled of a bank still, faintly, under the coffee and the recycled air — vaults, and money that used to be kept somewhere solid — and Lolly thought it suited the day’s business better than anyone had probably intended.
[640 Body Text] The President of the Galactic Counsel, Aldous Wexley, was in his seat before anyone else, with a single folder squared to the edge of the table.
[641 Body Text] Lolly had prepared herself for a villain, and found the reality much more upsetting: the President was not a villain. He was a career administrator who had chaired the Counsel’s finance committee for longer than most of the delegates had been delegates, and who had learned, somewhere in all those years, that a room will nearly always give way to whoever sounds most reasonable, and that sounding reasonable is a skill a man can practise, like scales.
[642 Body Text] He did not interrupt. He waited for the chair to open item 9(c), thanked her, and read three sentences from the Office’s brief in a voice that suggested he regretted every one of them:
[643 Body Text] “The Programme’s own indicator is that nothing occurred. The Office is advised that nothing would have occurred without it. I do not say this gladly. I say it because the signatories have asked me to account for the common fund, and I cannot account for an absence.”
[644 Body Text] The delegate for a signatory Lolly could not identify — the interpretation gave it as the Fourth Concordance — asked whether the Office had modelled the consequences of discontinuation.
[645 Body Text] “The Office took advice,” said the President. “I would not presume to put it in my own words. Professor Tengelman?”
[646 Body Text] Tengelman, whom Lolly had last seen at a Board of Inquiry being cheerful about the multiverse, and whom the Counsel now kept on a retainer through the Collegium at Brask, was seated at the end of the horseshoe with a folder. He explained that he had been asked a narrow question about unitarity and had answered it accurately, and that he would prefer not to be characterised as endorsing the wider proposal.
[647 Body Text] “Thank you,” said the President. “So the advice is that nothing is destroyed.”
[648 Body Text] “That is not what I—” said Tengelman. “I answered a question about unitarity. I have not endorsed—”
[649 Body Text] “Nobody suggests you have,” said the President pleasantly, and turned a page. “The professor has answered the question.”
[650 Body Text] And Lolly understood, then, something about the mechanism, which she wrote down that night: that the President did not require Tengelman’s agreement, only a form of words he could stand next to; that ‘the professor has answered the question’ was true and did all the work of an endorsement without being one; and that correcting it in public would look like arguing with the physics, not with him.
[651 Body Text] Beatrix Sloan was called at four twenty, as counsel for a non-signatory observer with leave to be heard, and she had thirteen minutes.
[652 Body Text] She used three of them on the facts and ten on the law.
[653 Body Text] “The Office’s third argument,” she said, “is that in a unitary volume there is no damage, only re-encoding. The Office’s authority for that is complementarity, and I want to state that doctrine fairly, because it is correct and because I intend to rely on it myself.
[654 Body Text] “Complementarity holds that a screen has two accounts. The party who stays outside sees the interior information on the surface and nothing lost. The party who goes in sees a place and no surface at all. Both accounts are consistent, and — this is what the Office has built on — no observer can hold both, so they can never be compared, so there is no contradiction, so nothing has gone wrong.
[655 Body Text] “The Office concludes that since no one may compare the two accounts, no one has standing to allege a loss. My client’s fourteen citizens are living — Ellen Prosper in Peterhead, and thirteen others elsewhere — and are also data on a rewritten screen, and the Office invites the session to find there is therefore nothing to remedy.
[656 Body Text] “I say the Office is thirty years out of date.”
[657 Body Text] She turned a page.
[658 Body Text] “Because that protection has a condition, and the condition failed. To keep both accounts, a late outgoing quantum must be fully entangled with the early radiation and with its partner inside, at once. And it is a theorem — this session adopted it in the schedule to the instrument of the year before last — that this is impossible. Entanglement is monogamous. The Office’s two accounts need the same correlation spent twice. They are not two consistent descriptions of one event. They are two claims, and at most one of them is true.
[659 Body Text] “Which means the comparison the Office says is forbidden is not forbidden. It is decisive. And where a rewrite of a screen requires the same correlation to be spent twice, the deficit is not an artefact of description. It is a physical loss, at a definite place, of a definite size, and it can be measured, and my client has measured it.
[660 Body Text] “S was two point four one. The ceiling for a settled record is two. The prediction for an intact one is two point eight. My client published that number in a Statement of Coherence in the year before last and was widely criticised for it, largely by me.
[661 Body Text] “The Office says there is no such thing as damage in a unitary volume. I say that unitarity guarantees that the information still exists. It does not guarantee that it exists here, and it does not guarantee that anyone in this room can get at it, and a conservation law is not a defence to a charge of taking. The Office has confused the accounts balance with nothing has happened, and every institution I have ever audited has made that error, and it has never once been an accident.”
[662 Body Text] She sat down at four thirty-three.
[663 Body Text] There was a silence of some length.
[664 Body Text] Then the President thanked Ms Sloan, with every appearance of sincerity, for the most interesting submission the session had heard in years.
[665 Body Text] “The signatories will weigh it,” he said. “They must also weigh the bill.” And he asked the chair to call the vote.
[666 Body Text] The vote on item 9(c) was taken at five past five.
[667 Body Text] Fourteen in favour of discontinuation. Nine against. Sixteen abstentions, and Beatrix afterwards said that the sixteen abstentions were the whole of the day’s information, because an abstention is what a delegate files when he has been persuaded and cannot say so.
[668 Body Text] Discontinuation of the Galactic Health Support Programme with effect from the end of the subscription year. Assets to be realised. Field maintenance to cease on the ninth of February. Records to be lodged with the Office of the President.
[669 Body Text] The chair asked whether there was any other business under the item.
[670 Body Text] The delegate for the Fourth Concordance said that she wished to record, for the minutes, that the Concordance had maintained subscription across its own volume for four hundred years before accession and had ceased on accession because the common fund made it redundant, and that it no longer possessed the capability to resume, and that neither did anybody else, and that this had been the point of a common fund.
[671 Body Text] The chair thanked the delegate and said the observation would be recorded.
[672 Body Text] It was recorded. Lolly obtained the minutes ten weeks later and read the paragraph. It was two lines long, and it did not name the consequence, and the word supernova appeared nowhere in the document at any point.
[673 Body Text] Eilstein was in the corridor afterwards, by the cloakroom, thinner than in London.
[674 Body Text] “You did not win,” he said.
[675 Body Text] “No,” said Beatrix.
[676 Body Text] “You will want to know why, because you were right.” He took his hat from the girl. “It is not because they did not follow you, Ms Sloan. Nine of them followed you exactly and I watched their faces. It is that you offered them a difficult truth with a bill attached, and he offered them a true sentence with a refund attached, and the sentence was true.” He put the hat on. “That is the innovation. There is nothing to fact-check. He has found a way to be entirely accurate and completely wrong, and what I carry has been about since the ice went back and has not seen it done at this scale before, and I want you all to understand that I am not being witty. I am frightened, and I am the only person in this building who eats.”
[677 Body Text] Lolly said, “What happens on the ninth of February?”
[678 Body Text] “Nothing happens on the ninth of February,” said Albrecht Eilstein. “That is what makes it so difficult to legislate against. The pressure comes off the pipes and for a while everything is as it was, and then one day a core that had four thousand years left has forty, and nobody in this room will be blamed, because by then the paragraph will be eleven cross-references deep and spelled wrong.”
[679 Body Text] He went out through the revolving door and did not appear in the glass.
[680 Body Text] ∗
[681 First Paragraph] Professor Tengelman took the tram.
[682 Body Text] The Office had offered a car. The Office always offered a car, in the same tone in which it offered a retainer, a footnote, and the use of his name, and in six years he had always accepted, because refusing a car is the sort of gesture that is remembered and he had not wished to be remembered for anything except accuracy. Tonight he had said that he preferred to walk, and had then not walked, and was now sitting on the Number 2 going round the Ring with his folder on his knees, which was the most he had defied anybody since his doctorate.
[683 Body Text] He replayed the afternoon in the order in which it had happened, because he did not trust any other order.
[684 Body Text] He had been asked a question. The question had been whether rewriting a screen is unitary. He had answered that it is, and it is. He had added, in writing, three qualifications. He had added, aloud, that nobody should take him for a supporter of the wider proposal. And the President had said: Then nothing is lost, and that is the advice. And Tengelman had said That is not what I, and the President had said, pleasantly, Nobody suggests you have, and the sentence had been taken out of his mouth and carried to the vote like a coat on a hanger, with nobody inside it.
[685 Body Text] He could have stood up. He had considered it. And he had understood, in the considering, that if he stood up and said the question I answered was narrow, the room would hear a physicist retreating from his own physics, and the President would thank him, and the vote would go exactly as it went. He had sat down inside his own sentence and let it be used. That was the whole of what he had done. He was a careful man, and he wished to be careful about the size of it.
[686 Body Text] The tram went past the Opera. A woman got on with a cello and stood, because cellists do not sit.
[687 Body Text] And then there was the other thing, which he had been not thinking about since half past four, when the English barrister had said my client’s fourteen citizens.
[688 Body Text] At the hotel he did not go to the bar. He went up to his room and opened the briefcase and took out the folder he had carried to every session for six years, and found the letter he had been not thinking about.
[689 Body Text] Office of the President, 9 March. Professor M. Tengelman, Collegium at Brask. The Office intends a limited pilot realisation of fourteen records on the fourteenth instant, in order to demonstrate to signatories, ahead of the two hundred and fourth session, that discontinuation entails no loss of information. The Office would be grateful for the Professor’s confirmation that a rewrite of a screen is unitary.
[690 Body Text] And his own reply, by return, the tenth of March: I confirm that, on the standard account, a rewrite of a screen is a unitary process and destroys no information. I note that it does not follow that the information remains accessible to any particular party.
[691 Body Text] He had been proud of the second sentence. He had thought it a qualification. He read it now as what it was: a man noticing the trapdoor, and describing its hinges, and stepping round it.
[692 Body Text] Fourteen records. The fourteenth instant. Fourteen citizens.
[693 Body Text] He did not know that they were the same fourteen. He wished to be precise about that. He knew only that he had not asked what the records were, and that he had not asked because the question had not been put to him, and that this was precisely the defence the English barrister had described as never once having been an accident.
[694 Body Text] He took a sheet of hotel paper and wrote Dear Ms Sloan, and then I write in connection with item 9(c), and then nothing for a long time.
[695 Body Text] At one in the morning he folded the sheet along the middle and put it in the folder behind the letter of the ninth of March. A man folds a letter along its own crease when he is not yet ready to make a new one. He knew that about himself. He did not like it, and he put the light out anyway, and lay in the dark being accurate about it until he slept.
[696 Body Text] ∗
[697 First Paragraph] Ramanathan telephoned from Cheltenham at eleven that night, and Lolly took it in the hotel corridor.
[698 Body Text] “I’ve finished the first core,” she said. “Twelve degrees off the plane, about nine thousand light-years out. I’ve been sitting on it for two days because I wanted the systematics right.”
[699 Body Text] “Go on.”
[700 Body Text] “It’s a red supergiant already into carbon burning, a few thousand years from core collapse. Ordinary in every respect. I ran the progenitor models with the barrier two per cent lower for six hours, which is what the field did here, and which the Programme’s own logs say it did everywhere the Programme maintains.” A pause, and paper. “In my models it brings the collapse forward by between three and four thousand years. Which means it may already have happened. And if it has, we won’t see it for nine thousand years, because that is how long the light takes to get here. Nobody alive will ever see what the fourteenth of March did to that star. That is the part I can’t get past.”
[701 Body Text] Lolly stood in the corridor with the telephone.
[702 Body Text] “Priya. It was six hours.”
[703 Body Text] “It was six hours here,” said Priya Ramanathan. “No one has ever suggested it was six hours only here. And I would like to say, on the record, before somebody in a good suit says it first, that a dip does not make a star explode. It was always going to explode. It goes four thousand years early, which is nothing, cosmically. Absolutely nothing.” Her voice was perfectly steady. “There are two hundred and six cores in this arm within four thousand years of their limit, and I have the list, and the Programme has eight months left.”
[704 Body Text] ∗
[705 First Paragraph] Priya Ramanathan put the telephone down and sat for a while in the dark of the instrument room at Cheltenham with the list on her knee.
[706 Body Text] Two hundred and six cores. She had typed them herself. She did not trust anyone else to get the catalogue numbers right, and typing a thing is a way of reading it slowly. Each one was a line: a designation, a distance, a mass, an estimate of how long the core had left. Most of the designations were letters and numbers. A few of the brighter ones had names, old names, Arabic mostly, given by people who had looked up at them from deserts without telescopes and wanted to be able to say that one.
[707 Body Text] She had grown up in Leicester above a pharmacy, and her father had taken her out to the edge of Bradgate Park on clear nights with a pair of binoculars that had belonged to his father, and shown her the ones he knew. He had known about six. He had pronounced them all slightly wrong, with total confidence, and she had loved him for it with an intensity that had made her, at fourteen, decide to know all of them properly, and correctly, and for ever.
[708 Body Text] Number forty-one on her list was one of his six.
[709 Body Text] She had not told Lolly that. She had not told anybody. It was not data. It had no bearing on the progenitor models, which she had run three times, at the lowered barrier, and which had come out the same each time: a few thousand years taken off a core that had a few thousand years left. Nothing, cosmically. She had said so on the telephone, steadily, and it was true.
[710 Body Text] The night technician, a boy called Owen who was doing a sandwich year and wore headphones he believed were invisible, put his head round the door.
[711 Body Text] “You all right, Dr R? You’ve been sat in the dark.”
[712 Body Text] “I’m thinking.”
[713 Body Text] “Right.” He considered this. “Do you want the light on for it?”
[714 Body Text] “No,” said Ramanathan. “Thank you, Owen. It goes better in the dark.”
[715 Body Text] He went. She heard the headphones go back on, and a faint, tinny, cheerful beat.
[716 Body Text] She took a pencil and, beside line forty-one, very small, in a hand no one but her would read, wrote her father’s pronunciation of its name. It was wrong. It had always been wrong. She wrote it anyway. It was a record of something true, and she had spent the months since March among people who believed that what you wrote down was a way of keeping it, and she found, at one in the morning, with a star on her knee that might already be gone, that she believed it too.
[717 Body Text] Then she turned the light on and ran the models a fourth time, because that was also a way of keeping things.
[718 Body Text] ∗
[719 First Paragraph] Lolly wrote, in a hotel in Vienna:
[720 Body Text] He did not lie. Not once. That is the method. 
A true sentence, cut away from the person who said it, cannot be corrected. 
Conservation is not a defence to taking.
[721 Body Text] And last:
[722 Body Text] 9 February.
206 cores. 
The word does not appear in the minutes.
[723 Heading 2] Chapter Seven
[724 Heading 3] An Eternal Golden Braid
[725 First Paragraph] The letter came in on the general tray, which by June had become the most productive intelligence source in the department, and it was forty-one pages long, and twelve of them were music.
[726 Body Text] Dear Department,
[727 Body Text] My name is Dougbart Stadthof and I restore organs. Cathedral, parish, and one dreadful thing in a hotel in Harrogate. I also translate, badly, from three languages, for pleasure, and I have been told by two separate people whose opinion I respect that I should either publish or stop talking.
[728 Body Text] I am writing because of the Statement of Coherence you published at the end of March, and specifically because of the sentence in paragraph 22 in which you say that the Counsel’s founding instrument “does not admit of an interpretation under which the Office may be compelled”.
[729 Body Text] I think it admits of exactly one, and I think it is the interpretation the instrument cannot survive, and I have enclosed it as Appendix D.
[730 Body Text] Appendices A, B and C are a unified theory of everything and you should probably not read them. I have called it the Eternal Golden Braid. My wife has asked me not to use that title in correspondence with government departments and I have agreed and then done it anyway, which is, if you think about it, the whole subject.
[731 Body Text] Yours,
[732 Body Text] D. Stadthof
[733 Body Text] Gideon read the first page over her shoulder, found no plug on it, and went back downstairs. Through the summer’s paper he kept the benches. He came up when a voice changed, or when she had been too long on a step, and he did not sit in a meeting in order to be counted in it.
[734 Body Text] Lolly read Appendix D on the Monday and telephoned him on the Monday.
[735 Body Text] Dougbart Stadthof arrived on the Thursday, Mrs Kind’s day, and the two of them got on so immediately and so loudly that Miss Pike had to move them out of the open plan.
[736 Body Text] He was sixty-three, bearded, in a jacket with a pencil in the breast pocket and rosin on the cuff, and he talked the way an organ sounds when the blower is on and nobody is playing yet — a constant low readiness that occasionally became music.
[737 Body Text] “Before anything,” he said, “I want to be clear about what I am and am not. I am not a physicist. I am a man who tunes reed stops and translates poetry for fun, and everything I know about your subject I know because in nineteen seventy-six I was regulating a Father Smith in a Fenland church and I noticed something about a fugue and I have not recovered.”
[738 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Noticed what?”
[739 Body Text] “That a fugue is a machine that contains a description of itself, and can operate on that description, and that this is not a metaphor.” He put a score on the table. “Bach. The canon that modulates up a tone, and again, and after six times you are home, having risen the whole way. You cannot point to where it turns round, because it does not. There is a print by a Dutchman of two hands drawing one another, which is the same object. There is a theorem by an Austrian, which is the same object. I have spent forty years being told that noticing this is not a discipline.”
[740 Body Text] “Is it finished?”
[741 Body Text] “No,” said Stadthof cheerfully. “It is not even wrong yet. Appendices A to C are three hundred pages of a man in love. What I have brought that is usable is nine pages, and I would rather we spent the day on those.”
[742 Body Text] The theorem was the Austrian’s, and Stadthof explained it in the briefing room with chalk and, at one point, by singing.
[743 Body Text] “Take any system of reasoning strong enough to do arithmetic,” he said. “Axioms, rules, and — this is essential — mechanical: you can check a proof without needing taste. The Austrian’s trick is to make the system talk about itself. Give every statement a number, and statements about numbers become statements about statements. Then you can build the sentence that ruins everything.”
[744 Body Text] He wrote it on the board in plain English:
[745 Body Text] This statement cannot be proved in this system.
[746 Body Text] “Now,” said Stadthof. “If the system proves it, the system has proved something false and is broken. So if the system is sound, it cannot prove it. But then the statement is true — that is exactly what it says.” He put the chalk down. “Every consistent system of that kind has one. Add the sentence as a new axiom and the new system grows a fresh one. It is not a bug in particular rules. It is a property of having rules at all.
[747 Body Text] “And there is a second part, which is the part your Ms Sloan wants. No such system can prove its own consistency. A system that proves it is consistent has, by that very proof, shown that it is not. The only bodies that can certify themselves are the broken ones.”
[748 Body Text] Beatrix Sloan, who had not spoken for twenty minutes, said: “Say that again, slowly, and let me write it down in the form I will have to say it in Vienna.”
[749 Body Text] Appendix D was four pages and it concerned Article 2 of the founding instrument.
[750 Body Text] “In nineteen forty-seven,” said Stadthof, “the Austrian went to his citizenship hearing in New Jersey, having read the constitution of the country he was applying to join and found in it, he told his friends, a lawful route to a dictatorship. They begged him not to raise it. He nearly did. The judge changed the subject.
[751 Body Text] “Now.” He turned the page round. “Article 2 of your instrument. The Office shall determine all questions as to the scope and application of this instrument, including questions as to the extent of the Office’s own competence to so determine.”
[752 Body Text] Fainrose said, “That’s a self-certification clause.”
[753 Body Text] “It is more than that, it is the sentence,” said Stadthof. “That clause makes the Counsel’s instrument a formal system that contains a description of itself and the authority to reason about it. It has all the Austrian’s diseases and none of his humility. Somewhere in it there is a motion the Counsel can neither pass nor reject. I have written it out. It is on page four. I wrote it as a joke in April.”
[754 Body Text] Beatrix read page four.
[755 Body Text] Then she read it again.
[756 Body Text] She left for fifty minutes. When she returned, her sleeves were up.
[757 Body Text] The motion, as eventually laid before the two hundred and sixth session, ran to a single sentence, and Lolly came to think of it as the second most accomplished document she had ever read.
[758 Body Text] That the Office lacks competence to determine any question upon which the Office’s own competence is the question at issue; and that this motion is such a question.
[759 Body Text] “Look at what it does,” said Beatrix, at half past nine that night, with the whole department round the table and Stadthof visibly delighted with himself. “If the Office rules the motion out of order, it has determined a question upon which its own competence was at issue — which is the very thing the motion says it may not do. So its ruling has no standing, and the motion survives.
[760 Body Text] “If the Office rules the motion in order and the session rejects it, then the session has affirmed that the Office does have that competence, in a vote whose validity depends on the Office having had competence to put it to them. The rejection assumes what it decides. It is circular, and it is void on its face, and I will bring that in any forum in the galaxy.
[761 Body Text] “And if the Office does nothing at all, the motion is pending. And pending, under Article 9, suspends the operation of any decision of the session which depends on the disputed competence.”
[762 Body Text] Lolly said, quietly, “Item nine C.”
[763 Body Text] “Item nine C,” said Beatrix. “The discontinuation of the Galactic Health Support Programme was a decision of the session taken on the Office’s ruling as to scope. It depends on the disputed competence. It cannot take effect while the question is pending.” She sat back. “And the question cannot be decided. Not by them. Not ever. The Austrian’s point is that there is no move available inside the system.”
[764 Body Text] “So we’ve stopped it,” said Miss Pike.
[765 Body Text] “We have frozen it,” said Beatrix. “Which is not the same thing and I want that on the record. I have not saved the Programme. I have put it in a state that cannot be resolved by the body with authority over it, which — Lolly, you will forgive me — is precisely the crime we spent the last case prosecuting.”
[766 Body Text] There was a rather long silence.
[767 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Yes. It is.”
[768 Body Text] “I know.”
[769 Body Text] “Do it anyway.”
[770 Body Text] “I intend to,” said Beatrix Sloan. “I want it minuted that I know what it is, that is all. Somebody in this room ought to be in the habit.”
[771 Body Text] Mrs Kind had spent the afternoon on the winding-up schedule, and she raised her hand at ten to ten in the manner of a person who has read the annex nobody else has reached.
[772 Body Text] “Before everyone celebrates,” she said. “Assets.”
[773 Body Text] “Sorry?”
[774 Body Text] “The motion in Vienna provided that on discontinuation the Programme’s assets be realised and its records lodged with the Office of the President.” She had it flagged. “I have been trying since Tuesday to work out what a subscription-maintenance body owns. It has no vessels. It has leased premises and some instruments and a pension liability. Its realisable value is essentially nil.”
[775 Body Text] “Then what’s the concern?”
[776 Body Text] “The records,” said Mrs Kind. “Ms Wren, what does a body that spends eleven thousand years topping up slack screens necessarily possess?”
[777 Body Text] Lolly understood it about a second before she said it, and had to sit down.
[778 Body Text] “A map,” she managed.
[779 Body Text] “A complete, current, galaxy-wide register of every under-subscribed screen in the signatory volume,” said Mrs Kind. “Every thin place. Every wall with a gap in it. Sorted, one presumes, by how slack, because that is how you would sequence the maintenance rounds.” She closed the folder. “It is the only document of its kind in existence, it cannot be reconstructed by anybody in under a millennium, and clause 14 lodges it with the Office of the President in the event of a discontinuation which, thanks to Ms Sloan, will now never technically occur.”
[780 Body Text] Beatrix finished, “Then the lodgement is suspended too.”
[781 Body Text] “Is it?” asked Mrs Kind. “Clause 14 isn’t a decision of the session. It’s a standing provision of the instrument, triggered by the resolution’s passage, not its coming into effect. I have booked two hundred and seventy group tours on contracts drafted like that. The cancellation is suspended. The handover isn’t.”
[782 Body Text] Nobody spoke.
[783 Body Text] “When,” said Fainrose.
[784 Body Text] “The papers went across on the eleventh of June,” said Mrs Kind.
[785 Body Text] Priya Ramanathan came down from Cheltenham on the Friday with the list, and it was the list that ended the good mood entirely.
[786 Body Text] “Two hundred and six cores within four thousand years of limit in this arm,” she said. “I’ve been classifying them by whether a six-hour vacuum excursion of the observed depth would advance ignition. Yes, no, or insufficient data.”
[787 Body Text] “And?”
[788 Body Text] “A hundred and ninety-seven classified. Yes for thirty-one, no for a hundred and sixty-six.” She put the sheet down. “Nine unclassified. And I want to be careful here, because I spent sixteen days assuming the nine were a data problem and they are not a data problem.”
[789 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Explain.”
[790 Body Text] “For those nine, whether the core ignites early depends on the spectral gap — the gap between the ground state and the first excited state of the coupled system.” Ramanathan looked up. “And it is a proper result, not a curiosity, that the spectral gap problem is undecidable. You can write down the Hamiltonian exactly and there is no algorithm that will tell you whether the system is gapped. It is the halting problem in a lab coat. So when I say nine are unclassified, I do not mean I need a better telescope. I mean these nine sit in the class where no general algorithm can settle the question, and for any one of them the answer may not be provable at all. I could hold the complete state of each star on a card and be no better off.”
[791 Body Text] Stadthof said, into the silence, “Ah.”
[792 Body Text] “Mr Stadthof, this is not a good moment for delight.”
[793 Body Text] “It is not delight, Dr Ramanathan, it is recognition, and I am sorry that it looks the same on my face.” He had gone quite serious. “That is the Austrian’s theorem in a star. That is the third strand and I have been claiming for forty years that there was a third strand and I would give a great deal to have been wrong.”
[794 Body Text] It was Miss Pike who, at some point after eleven, said the thing that made Lolly write the afternoon up as an argument rather than a minute.
[795 Body Text] “Mavis,” she said.
[796 Body Text] The support unit in the corner, which had been in the corner since the reorganisation and had been used, in nineteen months, principally as a place to put coats, said: “Present.”
[797 Body Text] “Mavis-9, are you consistent?”
[798 Body Text] A pause of about three seconds, which for Mavis-9 was geological.
[799 Body Text] “I can confirm that no inconsistency has been detected within my own operation,” said Mavis-9.
[800 Body Text] “That isn’t what I asked.”
[801 Body Text] “No,” said Mavis-9. “It is what I am able to answer. Miss Pike, if I were to certify my own consistency, my certification would be worthless in exactly the degree that it was confident. I am able to tell you that I have found nothing. I am not able to tell you that there is nothing. The distinction is in my documentation and nobody has ever read it.”
[802 Body Text] Stadthof leaned across the table, palms down.
[803 Body Text] “Mrs Kind,” he said, “I have waited my whole life to hear a machine say that in a government building, and I want to note that it did not say it until somebody asked.”
[804 Body Text] “That’s the invitation clause again,” Lolly realised.
[805 Body Text] “It is the invitation clause everywhere,” said Dougbart Stadthof. “That is Appendix A, that is the whole of the Braid, and I shall spare you the three hundred pages and give you the one sentence, and then I shall go and catch my train.
[806 Body Text] “Every system rich enough to describe itself has a place it cannot reach from inside. The fugue cannot say where it turned round. The Austrian’s arithmetic cannot certify its own soundness. Your Counsel cannot decide its own competence. Dr Ramanathan’s nine stars cannot be settled by any calculation. And your machine in the corner cannot vouch for itself, and knows it, and said so the moment it was asked and not one moment before.
[807 Body Text] “That is not a defect. That is what having an inside is. And the only thing anybody can do about it — the only move, in every one of those cases — is that somebody outside has to look, and somebody inside has to be asked. Your Programme was a hundred centuries of somebody outside looking. Your President has abolished it on the grounds that nothing had gone wrong, which is precisely the certificate that no system is entitled to issue about itself.
[808 Body Text] “I have called that the Eternal Golden Braid and it is a ridiculous name for it and I am too old to change it now.”
[809 Body Text] Lolly said, “Then the minute will not say Gödel refuted them. Gödel did not. A statute is not an arithmetic. The minute will say we declined to let a body certify its own competence. We know that declining is the thing the year before last called a crime. We are doing it with our eyes open. That is the wording. Not a theorem.”
[810 Body Text] Stadthof had been agreed with, and declined to notice. He did not correct the minute.
[811 Body Text] He got his coat.
[812 Body Text] “You will want the fourth strand,” he said, from the door.
[813 Body Text] Lolly said, “There’s a fourth strand?”
[814 Body Text] “There is always a fourth strand, Ms Wren, that is the trouble with braids.” Stadthof smiled. “The Austrian starved to death. He would not eat unless his wife tasted the food, and she went into hospital, and he stopped, and the man who proved that no system can vouch for itself died because he could not obtain external verification of his dinner.” He put his hat on. “I mention it because you have a hungry immortal on your files who will not enter a building without being asked, and I read the whole of your Statement of Coherence including the schedules, and I do not think anybody in this department has yet noticed that he is the same shape as the theorem.”
[815 Body Text] Lolly put one line in the notebook before she went home: Mavis said it the moment she was asked. Nineteen months. Nobody asked.
[816 Body Text] ∗
[817 First Paragraph] Albrecht Eilstein spent that night, as he spent most nights, in a lobby.
[818 Body Text] It was the lobby of a hotel in Bloomsbury that had been grand in 1911 and was now a place where conferences happened to people. The night porter knew him. The night porter did not know that he knew him; he knew only that there was an elderly gentleman in a good coat who sometimes sat in the second armchair from the revolving door between one and five in the morning, and who never asked for a room, and whom it had somehow never seemed necessary to move on.
[819 Body Text] He was hungry. He was always hungry now. Since March the world had offered him nothing but crumbs, and he had grown used to the crumbs the way a man in a long siege grows used to the sound of the guns. There was a little, here, if he attended to it. A guest in room 214 who could not decide whether to ring her sister. A cleaner who had been offered a job in Leeds and had until Friday. Small branches, thin and sour. He did not take them. He had decided that evening, when Ms Wren telephoned to tell him about the Austrian, that he would not take anything this week that he had not been asked for, and he had found to his considerable surprise that he meant it.
[820 Body Text] Across the lobby there was a long mirror behind the reception desk, gilt, foxed at the edges. It showed the revolving door, the armchairs, the porter with his crossword, the clock at twenty past three. It showed the second armchair from the door, empty, with the cushion slightly compressed.
[821 Body Text] He had stopped minding about the mirror a lifetime ago, and what he carried had stopped long before that. He found tonight that he minded again, a little, and could not at first work out why. Then he understood. A man he had never met had told a roomful of civil servants that he was the same shape as the theorem, and Ms Wren had repeated it to him on the telephone, carefully, as though handing over something breakable, and for the first time in a long while somebody had described him accurately, and it had felt, absurdly, like being seen.
[822 Body Text] He was a system that could not vouch for itself. He had always known that. What he had not known, until a stranger said it in front of a minute-taker, was that this was not a peculiarity of vampires. It was the condition of everything with an inside.
[823 Body Text] “Can I get you anything, sir?” said the porter, looking up from the crossword. “Tea? It’s a cold one.”
[824 Body Text] Eilstein looked at him. It was a long time since anybody had asked him that.
[825 Body Text] “Thank you,” he said. “No. But it was very kind of you to ask.” And then, because it seemed churlish not to: “Seven down is palimpsest.”
[826 Body Text] The porter counted the letters, and wrote it in, and said, “Well, I’ll be damned,” and Eilstein, who had been, inclined his head and went back to watching the empty chair in the mirror until it was light.
[827 Body Text] ∗
[828 First Paragraph] On the Saturday Lolly went to Larchfield. The social worker could only do Saturdays, and a letter on council paper had asked her to attend a preliminary options conversation, which is what a meeting is called when it has already taken place.
[829 Body Text] Ms Hollis Breen was waiting in the conservatory with a laptop, a lanyard, a folder, and the expression of a woman who had sixty cases and liked all of them and could not save one. She had a cold. She apologised for the cold before she apologised for anything else, which Lolly respected.
[830 Body Text] “It’s only to talk through the options,” she said. “Nothing’s decided.”
[831 Body Text] She put a sheet on the wicker table. BEST INTERESTS: OPTIONS APPRAISAL (FORM BI-2). A row for each option, with columns for benefits, risks and cost per week. Option A, Elmhurst Court, Croydon. Option B, Larchfield First Floor (secure). At the bottom, a box headed Person’s own views (where these can be ascertained). It was empty. It was a very clean box.
[832 Body Text] “Has anyone asked him?” said Lolly.
[833 Body Text] “We did try.” Ms Breen did not look away, which Lolly respected too. “It was one of his difficult days. He thought I was from the electricity. And under the Act, for a decision like this, he hasn’t got capacity, so the decision falls to you as attorney, with us advising on what the council will fund. These are the two we fund. I’m sorry. I know how it looks.”
[834 Body Text] “How does it look?”
[835 Body Text] “Like a form with two boxes,” said Ms Breen, and blew her nose.
[836 Body Text] Her father came in on Tomasz’s arm at half past eleven, in his good cardigan, and was delighted to see her and not entirely sure what year it was. It was a shop day. Lolly could tell from the way he looked round the conservatory, as if checking the stock.
[837 Body Text] “Lol! Is your mother with you? She said she’d pop in with the fish.”
[838 Body Text] “Not today, Dad.”
[839 Body Text] “No, well. Fridays are murder.” He sat, and saw Ms Breen, and his face arranged itself into the courtesy he had kept for customers since 1962. “Hello, love. Is it the telly? Don’t tell me. Picture rolling.”
[840 Body Text] “I’m Hollis, Mr Wren. From the council. We met before.”
[841 Body Text] “Course we did.” He did not remember, and decided at once that it did not matter, which was the kindest thing about him and always had been. “What can I do you for?”
[842 Body Text] Ms Breen looked at Lolly. Lolly nodded. It was, after all, his name on the form.
[843 Body Text] “We’re thinking about where you might be most comfortable living, Mr Wren. Next year. There are some changes here.”
[844 Body Text] “Over the shop,” said Dennis Wren, immediately, as though she had asked him the way to the station. “Where else? Pelham Parade. There’s the flat upstairs, two rooms and a kitchen, June’s got it lovely. You can see the buses from the front room. I like to see the buses.”
[845 Body Text] There was a short silence in the conservatory.
[846 Body Text] Wren’s Radio & Television had closed nine years ago. The shop itself now sold vapes and phone cases, and had a neon sign in the window in the shape of a cloud.
[847 Body Text] Ms Breen wrote, carefully, in the clean box: Expressed wish to return to former home (no longer available). Then she looked at the line for a while, as though it might do something.
[848 Body Text] Lolly looked at it too. Two permitted options in printed rows, and a third in pencil underneath, the only one he actually wanted, and that one gone. Not withdrawn by anybody. Not taken. It had simply stopped being a place, years ago, while he went on holding it.
[849 Body Text] “I’m not signing anything today,” she said.
[850 Body Text] “Nobody’s asking you to,” said Ms Breen. “Not till the autumn.”
[851 Body Text] “There’s always a rush till the autumn.”
[852 Body Text] Ms Breen almost smiled, and sneezed instead, and put the form back in the folder.
[853 Body Text] Her father, who had been following this with the polite attention of a man at somebody else’s wedding, leaned over and patted Lolly’s knee.
[854 Body Text] “Don’t let them rush you, Lol. Forms are like valves. You put the wrong one in in a hurry and the whole set sulks for a week.” He sat back, satisfied. “Is your mother coming? She said she’d pop in.”
[855 Body Text] On the train home Lolly opened the notebook at a fresh page and wrote at the top Person’s own views, and underneath it, in her best handwriting, what he had said about the buses. She wrote it exactly, with his words in his order, the way she would have written a reading she did not yet understand and did not intend to correct.
[856 Body Text] It was, she realised somewhere after Clapham Junction, the first time anybody had written it down.
[857 Heading 2] Chapter Eight
[858 Heading 3] The Game with No Optimal Play
[859 First Paragraph] Jack Nashville came in on a Tuesday, by appointment, alone, and would not be met at the station.
[860 Body Text] He was ninety-six. He wore a mackintosh over a cardigan and carried nothing at all, no case, no papers, which Lolly found more intimidating than the wheeled document case had been. He shook hands, sat down, looked at the traces on the plasterboard for about a minute and a half without speaking, and then said:
[861 Body Text] “You have a commons problem and you think you have a physics problem. You have both. They are the same problem. Nobody has told you that because the people who do the physics do not read the other literature and the people who do the other literature are not asked about stars.”
[862 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Mr Nashville—”
[863 Body Text] “Jack is fine. I have been Professor and it did not improve anything.”
[864 Body Text] “Jack. Before you start. I asked you here because of a paper of yours from nineteen fifty-one which I read as a graduate student, and because Mrs Kind found your name in the Programme’s leasehold schedule, and I would like to know which of those two things you came about.”
[865 Body Text] “The second,” said Jack Nashville. “The first was a small thing I did quickly at twenty-one. It is what I am known for and it is not what I am. I was a geometer. I proved that any curved space, however strangely curved, can be laid inside a flat one of enough dimensions without stretching or tearing.” He glanced at the board, where Fainrose’s box from the holiday argument was still faintly visible. “I understand you have recently become interested in that. Nobody ever asks me about it. They ask me about the twenty-seven pages.”
[866 Body Text] Miss Pike asked, “Why did you come about the leases?”
[867 Body Text] “Because I know what is in them,” said Nashville, “and I have known since nineteen fifty-nine, and for a long while after that I was not in a condition to be believed. I spent some years unable to tell a signal from a meaning. It is a specific illness, and it teaches you permanently the difference between a pattern that is there and a pattern you have supplied.” He folded his hands. “So I waited sixty-seven years for somebody in a government building to publish a number I recognised. In March your Miss Pike published two hundred and nine out of two hundred and nine. I bought the Statement from the Stationery Office, and here I am.”
[868 Body Text] He did the commons first, because he said it was the easy half and people should be allowed one easy half.
[869 Body Text] “Forty-one signatories,” he began. “A common fund. Each pays a small sum annually. The benefit is diffuse, the cost is specific, and no member’s own contribution is individually decisive. What is each member’s best move, taking the others’ moves as fixed?”
[870 Body Text] “To stop paying,” said Lolly.
[871 Body Text] “To stop paying. Always, whatever the others do.” Nashville counted it on one hand. “If everyone else pays, you get the maintained galaxy free. If nobody else pays, your payment is wasted. That is not greed and it needs no villain. It is the structure. The only stable point of that game is that nobody pays and the walls go slack.”
[872 Body Text] “Then how did it run for so long?” asked Fainrose.
[873 Body Text] “That,” said Jack Nashville, “is the only interesting question about item nine C, and nobody at the session asked it. On the plain analysis it should have collapsed in the first century. The usual rescue is the future: play repeatedly, watch for cheats, punish them. But your Counsel has no army, no territory, and until June no address. It cannot punish anybody. Something else was doing the work.” He nodded at Mrs Kind. “Which is why I came about the leases.”
[874 Body Text] Mrs Kind had the leasehold schedule out and flagged in three colours. She had been on it since Thursday.
[875 Body Text] “Eleven leased premises,” she said. “I could not make them make sense. A body that maintains screens across a galaxy does not need a dozen rooms, and if it did need rooms it would need them near the work, and these are not near anything. Two in the Atacama, one in a Cumbrian mineshaft — which I will come back to because that is van Casteel’s mineshaft and the lease predates him by six hundred years — one in Svalbard, one under a Swiss hillside, four at sites the schedule gives only by coordinate, and two in Vienna.”
[876 Body Text] “And the rent?” asked Beatrix.
[877 Body Text] “Nominal. A peppercorn in nine of them. But the service charge is enormous and it is all one line item, and the line item is described as—” she found it — “maintenance of held correlations, replenishment as required.”
[878 Body Text] Nashville said, “There it is.”
[879 Body Text] “There what is?” asked Lolly.
[880 Body Text] “Your enforcement mechanism,” said Nashville. “Those rooms are not offices. They are where the shared correlations are kept. And once you know that, everything about your instrument makes sense, including the parts your lawyers have been calling badly drafted.”
[881 Body Text] He turned to Beatrix.
[882 Body Text] “Ms Sloan. When you drafted your motion in Vienna you were trying to bind forty-one parties by words. Words require a forum, and a forum requires a bailiff, and there is no bailiff. The founding parties knew that in the first century. So they did not build a legal instrument. They built a game they could not cheat at, and they parked the equipment in leased rooms, and they charged it to the service charge, and they told nobody, and it worked for longer than there have been cities.”
[883 Body Text] The rest of the morning was the hard half, and Nashville did it slowly, like someone who had explained it before to people who had not wanted to hear it. Mrs Kind, who had once explained to a coach party why Lourdes is not on the way to Benidorm, recognised the tone and sat up straighter.
[884 Body Text] “Consider a game between two players who cannot communicate,” he said. “They are separated. Each receives a question — a bit, say, chosen at random by a referee — and each must answer. They win or lose according to a rule that depends on both questions and both answers. They may agree any strategy in advance. They may carry any notes, any tables, any shared random numbers. They simply may not talk once the questions are asked.
[885 Body Text] “There is a best possible classical strategy, and you can compute it, and for the simplest such game it wins seventy-five per cent of the time. That is a theorem. It is not a matter of cleverness. Seventy-five per cent is the ceiling for any pair of players who share only ordinary information, however much of it, however cunningly arranged.
[886 Body Text] “Now give them a pair of correlated particles beforehand. Nothing else changes. They still cannot communicate — and I want to be exact, because this is where everybody goes wrong: they cannot signal, not one bit, the correlation transmits nothing, you could not send a message with it if your life depended on it.” He paused. “And they win eighty-five per cent of the time.”
[887 Body Text] Stadthof, who had come in on his Thursday and stayed for a Tuesday, said, “Which is Bellboy’s number.”
[888 Body Text] “It is Bellboy’s number wearing different clothes,” said Fainrose. “Two point eight against a ceiling of two.”
[889 Body Text] “Yes. Same content, different clothes, and the clothes matter.” Nashville leaned forward. “Because as a game you can see what it is for. It lets separated parties who cannot communicate, and do not trust one another, coordinate better than any agreement could arrange. And in some games the gap is total. There is a little three-by-three grid with parity rules where the best classical play wins eight times in nine, and shared correlation wins every time. With certainty. No arrangement of notes and dice achieves it.”
[890 Body Text] No one said anything for a while. Miss Pike wrote eight times in nine on her blotter and drew a box round it, the way she boxed figures she meant to check herself.
[891 Body Text] Lolly said, slowly, “You can build compliance out of that.”
[892 Body Text] “You can build verified compliance out of that,” said Nashville. “The design is not mine. It is older than agriculture and I merely recognised it. Each signatory holds correlated partners with the common store, and compliance is demonstrated by playing. A member who has kept up his subscription wins at the quantum rate. A member who has quietly stopped, and whose correlations have decayed, drops to seventy-five per cent and cannot fake his way up, because the ceiling is a theorem and not a standard.
[893 Body Text] “No inspectors. No army. No address. Defection is not punished, it is simply visible, and it is visible without anybody having to be trusted, and that is why forty-one parties who have never met and do not like each other paid a subscription since the founding.” He sat back. “They were not being good. They were playing a game in which the honest move was the only one that scored.”
[894 Body Text] Fainrose had gone still.
[895 Body Text] “And in June,” she said, “the equipment was transferred to the Office of the President.”
[896 Body Text] “The equipment, and the map, and the leases,” Mrs Kind added.
[897 Body Text] “And the service charge,” said Nashville. “Which pays for replenishment as required. Correlations decay, Dr Fainrose; they are not furniture. A body that stops replenishing them is not economising. It is dismantling the only enforcement mechanism in the galaxy, one leased room at a time, without a single vote.”
[898 Body Text] At some point after lunch Beatrix Sloan said, “Then I stop drafting.”
[899 Body Text] “I beg your pardon?” asked Lolly.
[900 Body Text] “I have been drafting a treaty since March, on Stephanie’s instruction, in the only way I know: words, a forum, a remedy.” She had her hands flat on the table again. “It cannot work. There is no bailiff. Jack was right in nineteen fifty-nine and nobody listened, and I have spent four months building an instrument that depends on a body Mr Stadthof has just proved cannot decide anything.”
[901 Body Text] “So what do you draft instead?”
[902 Body Text] “A protocol,” said Beatrix. “Not a treaty. Nobody promises anything, because promises need enforcing. Parties simply hold correlated partners and play, at intervals, and the score is the compliance record, and there is nothing to argue about, and nothing to interpret, and no Office determines the scope of anything.” She looked at Nashville. “Tell me the catch. There is always a catch and you have known it for sixty-seven years.”
[903 Body Text] “There are two,” said Jack Nashville. “The first is monogamy. You cannot hold maximal correlation with two parties at once — your own schedule to the Act says so. So a protocol like this has a membership limit, and it is arithmetic, not drafting. The leased rooms hold what they hold. Spread the store across forty-one members and you get forty-one weak correlations, none of which clears the classical bound reliably, and the whole thing collapses into a system of assertions. Which is to say, into a treaty.”
[904 Body Text] Beatrix said, “How many can it carry?”
[905 Body Text] “With what is in those rooms, at present decay, and assuming no replenishment after February?” Nashville did the sum in his head. “Nine. Possibly ten if you are willing to accept a scoring interval of a century.”
[906 Body Text] “Nine,” said Fainrose. “Out of forty-one.”
[907 Body Text] “Nine,” said Nashville. “And you will have to choose them, and every one you leave out will correctly perceive that you have built a small trusted club out of the wreckage of a universal fund, and they will not be wrong, and I have no advice about that at all. I do arithmetic.”
[908 Body Text] Lolly thought of Gideon, in Marsham Street, with a burnt wrist and no brief. If she told him the only bailiff in the galaxy was a leased room with a service charge, he would ask to see the plug. She wanted, with a sharpness that surprised her, to be in the room where that was the next sentence.
[909 Body Text] Lolly asked, “And the second catch?”
[910 Body Text] And Jack Nashville, who had not raised his voice all day, said:
[911 Body Text] “The second catch is that these games have no optimal play.”
[912 Body Text] He explained it standing up, at the window, with his back to them, in the manner of a man who does not like the sentence.
[913 Body Text] “For fifty years everybody assumed such a game has a best achievable score you could compute, or at least squeeze from above and below. Two traditions defined that score in two reasonable ways, and everybody assumed they agreed. They do not. Approximating the best score of a game like this is exactly as hard as the halting problem. Mr Stadthof’s Austrian again, at the bottom of it. A page of paper — questions, rules, scoring, all finite — and no algorithm that will tell you how well it can be played.”
