[914 Body Text] Stadthof said quietly, “Oh, that is beautiful and I hate it.”
[915 Body Text] “It gets worse,” said Nashville, turning round. “For some games the score keeps improving as you allow the players bigger shared systems, and never settles, and you cannot even bound how much you are missing.” He came back to the table. “For your protocol, one consequence, and not a good one. A game like the grid is fine: the score is one, certain, checkable. But if any party may propose the game, a patient adversary can put forward one whose score no one can compute, demand that the protocol resolve it, and it will hang. For ever.”
[916 Body Text] Beatrix closed her notebook.
[917 Body Text] “He will do that,” she said. “Within a year.”
[918 Body Text] “He will do it in October,” said Nashville, “and he will not have understood any of it, and somebody in his office will have read your protocol and mine and worked out that a body which cannot decide is a body which cannot decide against him, and it is the same weapon you used in Vienna, Ms Sloan, and you handed him the drawings when you filed it.”
[919 Body Text] Priya Ramanathan came in at four with the 4C radiation curve, having been promised ten minutes and waited three hours for them.
[920 Body Text] “Briefly,” she said. “I’ve dated the crossover on Four C. For the first part of its evaporation the radiation carried essentially nothing — the correlations were all still inside. Then it turned over, and after that every quantum leaving carried structure, and the outside began to hold more of the record than the inside did. It rises and then it falls, and the peak is the moment the account changes hands.”
[921 Body Text] “When?” asked Fainrose.
[922 Body Text] “Four C crossed over in nineteen sixty-one,” said Ramanathan, without looking up from the curve.
[923 Body Text] Mrs Kind looked up from the leasehold schedule.
[924 Body Text] “Nineteen sixty-one,” she said, “is the year the clerk filed the treaty instrument under the wrong word.”
[925 Body Text] No one was quite prepared to say anything about that, then or later, and Lolly noted it in the margin and drew a box round it and did not write a conclusion.
[926 Body Text] Miss Pike had been doing arithmetic since Ramanathan said nineteen sixty-one, and when the room had stopped not saying anything about the clerk, she put her pencil down.
[927 Body Text] “I know where to get correlation to refill the leased rooms,” she said. She had worked it out in the margin of the leasehold schedule, and it was the least comfortable place available.
[928 Body Text] “The crossover in nineteen sixty-one. Priya’s curve. Every quantum it has let go since then carries structure. Which means Four C has been emitting usable correlation for sixty-five years, and we have been treating the entire volume as a crime scene and a mausoleum and we have never once put a collector on it.”
[929 Body Text] Fainrose said, “You want to harvest the horizon.”
[930 Body Text] “I want to harvest the horizon of the object we recovered three hundred and eight people from,” said Miss Pike, “and I have written down all four of the reasons that is monstrous, and I have also written down what happens to a hundred and ninety-five unresolved records in this country alone if the walls go slack in February, and I would like the department to decide rather than me.”
[931 Body Text] They put Nashville in a taxi at half past six and Eilstein was in the lobby, waiting to be asked.
[932 Body Text] Lolly asked him up, and then, on the stairs, asked him the other thing.
[933 Body Text] “Mr Stadthof said the Austrian starved because he wouldn’t eat unless his wife tasted it,” she said. “And that you’re the same shape. Who tasted yours?”
[934 Body Text] Eilstein stopped on the landing.
[935 Body Text] “That is the first question anybody in this building has asked me that I did not want,” he said.
[936 Body Text] “You said you wanted to be interrogated.”
[937 Body Text] “I did, and I am a man of my word, which is the only virtue I have and I keep it in excellent condition.” He looked at the wall for a while. “There is a woman in what I carry. Ravenna, the winter of thirteen thirty-eight. I never met her. The one who carried this before me did, and I have his memory of her the way you might have a grandmother’s recipes: complete, and not mine. Not a servant. A notary’s widow who understood what he was inside a fortnight and did not scream and did not run and did not want anything from him, which in my experience is the rarest of the three. She would sit with him and tell him whether what he had taken was a thing anybody had needed. She was the only external check this thing has ever had, and she had it for nine years, and then she died in the ordinary way at sixty-one, and nobody has been permitted to do it since. I inherited six hundred and seventy-nine years of decisions made without a single person to say whether they were good ones, and I have added my own.”
[938 Body Text] Lolly said, “That’s why you keep asking to be interrogated.”
[939 Body Text] “Ms Wren,” said Albrecht Eilstein, “I am a system rich enough to describe myself, and I have been issuing my own certificate of consistency since the fourteenth century, and your Mr Stadthof has just informed a room full of civil servants that the certificate is worthless in exactly the degree that I am confident about it, and he is correct, and I have known he was correct for six hundred years, and it is the reason I stand in lobbies.”
[940 Body Text] ∗
[941 First Paragraph] Van Casteel came up from Cumbria on the Thursday with a photocopy from a county record office and put it on the table without sitting down.
[942 Body Text] “My mineshaft,” he said. “I have worked in it for nineteen years and my employer leased it in nineteen sixty-three from a company in Kendal. That company took an underlease in eighteen ninety. The head lease is dated fourteen hundred and twelve and the lessee is not named, and the covenant — the one covenant on the entire instrument, in Latin, in a hand I had to pay a man in Carlisle to read — is that the demised chamber shall be kept still, and dark, and undisturbed, and its contents shall not be counted.”
[943 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Shall not be counted.”
[944 Body Text] “Shall not be counted,” said Hendrik van Casteel. “I have spent nineteen years in that chamber measuring the force between two plates more finely than anyone else has bothered to, and I would like somebody in this building to tell me whether I have been the tenant or the breach.”
[945 Body Text] The Vienna premises turned up the same afternoon. Mrs Kind found them at ten past six and made everybody wait while she read the lease to the end.
[946 Body Text] “Two Vienna leases,” she said. “The first is the registry. Rates, cleaner, a strong room, all completely ordinary, and the map went into it on the eleventh of June.
[947 Body Text] “The second is a single room on the fourth floor of a building off the Ringstrasse and it has been leased continuously since the founding, and it has no telephone, no instruments, no strong room, and no fixtures beyond a table and two chairs, and the service charge is nil, and the purpose clause reads: for the accommodation of an unaffiliated observer.”
[948 Body Text] Lolly said, “Meaning what?”
[949 Body Text] “Meaning it is deliberately empty,” said Mrs Kind. “Forty-one signatories, since the founding, and a room maintained at the founders’ expense for somebody who belongs to none of them, to come and look, and to say whether what the Counsel had done was a thing anybody needed.”
[950 Body Text] Stadthof put down his tea.
[951 Body Text] “Somebody outside has to look,” he said, “and somebody inside has to be asked.”
[952 Body Text] “How many times has it been used?” asked Beatrix.
[953 Body Text] Mrs Kind checked the occupancy schedule: one page, covering the whole life of the Counsel, and nearly blank.
[954 Body Text] “Twice,” she said.
[955 Body Text] Lolly wrote again, at midnight:
[956 Body Text] Ravenna. 1338. Nine years. He stands in lobbies because he knows what his own certificate is worth. So should we.
Vienna, fourth floor. A table and two chairs. Nil service charge.
Unaffiliated observers only.
Used twice.
[957 Heading 2] Chapter Nine
[958 Heading 3] The Manufacture of an Agreed World
[959 First Paragraph] Three items had been left on the table since the Monday, and Fainrose called them, on the Thursday morning, “the backlog,” which was the first time Lolly had heard her use a word that small for something that could end with either a black hole undressed in public or a lighting engineer evicted from his own mineshaft for counting.
[960 Body Text] “Two hundred and fifteen days,” said Miss Pike, who had started opening every backlog meeting with the number since no one else would. “Item one.”
[961 Body Text] Item one was Miss Pike’s own proposal, which meant item one was Miss Pike, standing at the board before anyone had sat down, with Ramanathan beside her holding the curve.
[962 Body Text] “I want to withdraw half of what I said,” Miss Pike said. “Not the four reasons. The verdict.”
[963 Body Text] “Go on,” said Fainrose.
[964 Body Text] “I said harvesting Four C’s horizon was monstrous, and I still think it would have been, then. I asked Priya to check whether ‘then’ still applies.” She nodded at Ramanathan. “It doesn’t.”
[965 Body Text] Ramanathan put the curve on the board and drew a vertical line through it, well past the peak. “The Page time was nineteen sixty-one. We are sixty-five years past it — Miss Pike’s own figure still holds. Whatever went in has been circulating inside that horizon and coming back out, mixed with everything else that fell in before and since, for six and a half decades of scrambling.”
[966 Body Text] “What does that buy us?” said Lolly.
[967 Body Text] “It buys us this: information leaving a horizon that long after the crossover isn’t a message from anybody in particular. It’s noise with the right statistics, featureless one photon at a time. You cannot open an envelope that has been shredded and reshredded for sixty-five years and pull one person’s name out of it. Not because the information isn’t there in principle, but because none of the radiation is any longer correlated with one contributor more than another. It’s public the way a very old, very average day is public. Nobody owns it.”
[968 Body Text] Miss Pike said, carefully, “That’s not the same as saying it’s safe.”
[969 Body Text] “No. It’s saying it’s already happened.” Ramanathan looked at her, not unkindly. “We are not proposing to go looking for Ellen Prosper in the static. No one could. We are proposing to point a receiver at static and log its statistics. You were frightened of the right thing. It just isn’t the thing that’s available any more.”
[970 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Show me the redundancy.”
[971 Body Text] “That’s the part I came to show you.” Ramanathan slid the next plot across. “One photon tells you almost nothing. But the hole’s coarse facts — its mass, its temperature — are smeared across an enormous number of photons, none of them privileged. Ask any large enough fraction of the radiation and you get the same answer from a hundred fractions that never compared notes. That is the opposite of a leak. A leak is one copy in the wrong hands. This is the same answer arriving independently everywhere, which is what a fact looks like once it has stopped being anybody’s secret.”
[972 Body Text] Beatrix, who had come in during the curve, said, “Put that in a form I can defend to the nine.”
[973 Body Text] “You can harvest structural correlation from Four C’s current emission for the Vienna stores,” said Ramanathan, “because by the redundancy test itself, nothing you would recover could be traced to a contributor, which is the same test that would forbid you to recover it if it could. The test isn’t a loophole. It’s the same rule protecting the fourteen that lets you touch anything at all.”
[974 Body Text] Fainrose looked at the curve for a while.
[975 Body Text] “Minute it exactly like that,” she said. “And underline the word public.”
[976 Body Text] Before item two there was the correspondence, which Miss Pike read out. It had reached the department through three others, each of which had stamped it NOT US in a slightly different red.
[977 Body Text] “A ward petition from Preston,” she said. “Three hundred and eighty-six signatures. They say that when the Ministry closed the footbridge over the railway, nine winters ago, the notice on the gate promised it would reopen by Whitsun. All of them remember the notice. Forty of them remember it word for word, in the same words. They would like Whitsun honoured.”
[978 Body Text] “And the file?” said Fainrose.
[979 Body Text] “The file has the notice in it.” Miss Pike held it up: one typed sheet, foxed at a corner. “It says the bridge is closed pending a decision. It promises nothing. That is the house style.”
[980 Body Text] Beatrix said, “Three hundred and eighty-six witnesses against one sheet of paper. In most rooms that is a verdict.”
[981 Body Text] “It’s one witness,” said Lolly. It came out already shaped like a minute. “They read the gate for a week. Then they remembered it from each other for nine years: the pub, the parish letters page, whoever stands up at every residents’ meeting and says it again. A memory isn’t a copy kept in a drawer. It gets rebuilt every time it’s taken out, from whatever is in the room, and in Preston the room was full of neighbours. Three hundred and eighty-six people with one cause are one record with three hundred and eighty-six signatures.”
[982 Body Text] Ramanathan looked up from the trace, for once. “The opposite of my photons,” she said. “Mine never compared notes. Theirs have done nothing else.”
[983 Body Text] “Write back,” said Fainrose. “Tell them the ward and the file disagree, that the file is older than the agreement, and that the Ministry regrets the bridge. Do not tell them they are wrong. Tell them they are agreed. It is a different finding.”
[984 Body Text] “And the bridge?” said Miss Pike.
[985 Body Text] “Is closed,” said Fainrose. “That part, everyone remembers correctly.”
[986 Body Text] Item two came up from Cumbria on the sleeper again, unshaven again, and put a single sheet on the table before he had taken his coat off.
[987 Body Text] “I have not stopped thinking about the question I asked last time,” said Hendrik van Casteel, “and I would like it answered before I go back down that shaft, because I have started leaving the plates further apart than the experiment wants, and that is not science, that is a man being careful of a covenant he does not understand, and it is costing me a decimal place.”
[988 Body Text] “We’ve been thinking about it too,” said Beatrix, already writing it down.
[989 Body Text] “Then tell me. Tenant, or breach.”
[990 Body Text] It was Fainrose who answered, and she did it the way she did the board — slowly, and without softening any of it.
[991 Body Text] “Neither,” she said, “and I’ll give you the reasoning rather than the relief, because relief wears off the first time somebody asks you to justify it. The covenant tells the caretaker to keep the chamber still, dark and unvisited, and to leave its contents uncounted. As an instruction to a caretaker, fine. As a description of a state of the vacuum, it asks for something nature does not stock. A field mode cannot have exactly zero amplitude and exactly zero rate of change at the same time. It’s forbidden the way a triangle is forbidden four sides.”
[992 Body Text] “I don’t build triangles,” said van Casteel. “I build benches.”
[993 Body Text] “So whoever wrote the lease in fourteen twelve wanted a vacuum with nothing in it and nothing happening, and there is no such vacuum. The room has never for one instant kept its own covenant, with or without you in it. What you’ve spent nineteen years measuring is the one thing every such room has regardless of anyone’s care: the floor under ‘nothing’, which was never yours to disturb because it was never anybody’s to still.”
[994 Body Text] “And the counting?”
[995 Body Text] “You have counted a force. You have never counted a thing — an occupant, a fact that could have been otherwise. You measured the one quantity in that chamber that could not have come out any other way. That isn’t a breach.” She paused. “It’s proof the covenant was never answerable, and I’d be careful whom you tell, because a great many arrangements in this Programme are old promises about a stillness nobody checked.”
[996 Body Text] Van Casteel sat with that for a while, the way he had sat, that first day in Cheltenham, with one arm still in his coat.
[997 Body Text] “So the chamber was never once at rest.”
[998 Body Text] “Not for a picosecond since fourteen twelve.”
[999 Body Text] “Then I have not been breaking a silence,” he said slowly. “I have been the first person willing to write down how loud it always was.” He put the sheet back in his case. “I withdraw the decimal place. I’ll close the gap again on Thursday.”
[1000 Body Text] Item three, Mrs Kind had saved for last on purpose, and she said so.
[1001 Body Text] “Because it’s the one where I don’t know the answer and I want everybody awake for it,” she said. “The room in Vienna. Fourth floor, two chairs, nil service charge, used twice since the founding. It is kept for an unaffiliated observer. I want to send somebody, and before anybody says a name, I want to know what the word means, because I have been in the travel trade twenty-two years and I have never once sent a client somewhere without a definition of who’s allowed off the plane.”
[1002 Body Text] Beatrix asked, “Unaffiliated from what?”
[1003 Body Text] “From us. From the Counsel. From the nine, from Mr Eilstein, from the Office, from anybody whose word we’d already have reason to trust or distrust.” Mrs Kind folded her hands. “It can’t just mean a stranger. Strangers are cheap; I could hire one this afternoon.”
[1004 Body Text] Lolly said, “It’s the same question Ramanathan just answered.”
[1005 Body Text] The room looked at her.
[1006 Body Text] “Not the same words. The same shape.” She pulled the curve back towards her. “A fact stops being anybody’s secret when it turns up the same way in many places that never coordinated. An observer is unaffiliated for the same reason: not because no one has heard of them, but because their record of events was never coordinated with ours. Not a witness we trained. Not a witness who trained us.”
[1007 Body Text] Fainrose said, “That’s testable.”
[1008 Body Text] “It’s testable,” said Lolly. “Ask: does this person’s account of us share a common cause with our account of ourselves? If yes, they’re a colleague, however distant. If no — if they got here by their own road, for their own reasons, and never once needed our permission to keep thinking — they’re the thing Mrs Kind is trying to book a ticket for.”
[1009 Body Text] Beatrix was already writing.
[1010 Body Text] “There’s a candidate,” she said. “He saw two hundred and nine out of two hundred and nine in a Statement he bought from the Stationery Office, knew what was in the leases before anybody in this building did, and has been telling this department things it didn’t know about itself ever since, without once asking to be paid, thanked, or believed. Nobody trained him. Nobody vetted him. He arrived at every conclusion he’s given us along a road that never once ran through this building.”
[1011 Body Text] “Jack,” said Mrs Kind, and something in her voice suggested she’d known the name before Beatrix said it and had wanted somebody else to say it first.
[1012 Body Text] “I’ll write to him today,” said Beatrix. “And I intend to reuse the definition. Nashville told me the protocol can only trust nine of forty-one, and whoever we leave out will be right to resent it. I would rather choose the nine by the rule Lolly just stated than by who answers the telephone fastest. It won’t make the exclusion kinder. It will make it defensible, which in this department has not always been the same thing as kind.”
[1013 Body Text] There was nothing to add to that, and the meeting ended the way the good ones increasingly did: with everybody reaching for their own coat before anybody suggested it.
[1014 Body Text] Detective Inspector Albert Priddy was on the landing with the umbrella he counted as equipment, and a notebook whose corners had gone as soft as hers. Wet wool. Boot polish. He waited until Fainrose said his name, and then he spoke as if the statement had already been typed.
[1015 Body Text] “Fourteen missing persons,” he said. “Thirteen accounts that will not occupy the same page. Ellen Prosper’s neighbours watched her leave for the shop. The shop watched her come home. The interval is not a period I can put anybody in a cell for. I am closing the criminal file. I am not closing her.”
[1016 Body Text] He offered a carbon. Lolly did not lift Mrs Chain’s card to make room. She clipped the carbon beside it.
[1017 Body Text] “If the word comes back as an offence, ring the station,” he said. “If it comes back as a fact, spare the sergeant. I have been wrong in that second way often enough to recognise it, and not often enough to like it.”
[1018 Body Text] He went down the stair without shaking the umbrella out, and on the landing, to no one in particular, said the one thing he could not put on a charge sheet: “Somebody always holds the pen.”
[1019 Body Text] ∗
[1020 First Paragraph] Gideon was in the corridor with the coat she had left on the wrong hook. He had been holding it long enough for the holding to become its own decision, and he had not made it a reproach.
[1021 Body Text] “Nine,” she said, because the number was still in her mouth.
[1022 Body Text] “Nine what.”
[1023 Body Text] “People the protocol can keep. Out of forty-one.”
[1024 Body Text] He looked at the coat as if it might have an opinion. “Then you’ll want the plug checked by someone who doesn’t get a vote. I’ll do it in the morning. Come and eat something that isn’t a minute.”
[1025 Body Text] She went with him. The arithmetic would be there when she came back, and so, she had learned, would he.
[1026 Body Text] Lolly wrote, that night:
[1027 Body Text] 215 days. Ramanathan says the sixty-five years is the whole of the argument. I keep turning that over: something can stop being a secret without anybody deciding to forgive it.
[1028 Body Text] Gideon, this morning, on a scrap he left under the mug: The plug is honest. Same filth as Cheltenham. Not why the number is nine.
[1029 Body Text] ∗
[1030 First Paragraph] Mrs Elspeth Chain went to Larchfield House on the Tuesday, as she had most weeks since the spring before last, and found Ivy Danks asleep in room four with her mouth open and the crossword finished in ink.
[1031 Body Text] She did not wake her. A woman who has finished the crossword in ink has earned her sleep. She sat for a quarter of an hour by the bed with the umbrella across her knees and then, because a quarter of an hour of watching somebody sleep is company and more than that is surveillance, she went along to the lounge.
[1032 Body Text] Dennis Wren was in his usual chair by the radiator. He had a radio on the card table with its back off and a small screwdriver in his hand, and he was not using the screwdriver. He was looking out of the window at the bus stop, where no one was waiting, with a patience learned over nine weeks of waiting for a valve from Holland.
[1033 Body Text] “The umbrella lady,” he said, without turning round. “Sit down. Ivy’s asleep, I expect. She did the crossword before breakfast to show off.”
[1034 Body Text] “She did it in ink.”
[1035 Body Text] “She would.” He turned then, and looked at her properly, and she saw that it was one of his clear days, the ones Lolly spoke about in the voice people use for weather they cannot trust. “You’ve come about the form.”
[1036 Body Text] “I have come to visit Mrs Danks,” said Mrs Chain, “who is asleep. I am not here about anything.”
[1037 Body Text] “Everybody’s here about the form. Tomasz is here about the form. The tea lady’s here about the form.” He put the screwdriver down. “They’re going to ask me where I want to be. They’re not, though, are they. They’re going to ask Lol, and Lol’s going to say she wants what’s best for me, and best is a word. I sold tellies for forty-one years. I know what a word’s for.”
[1038 Body Text] Mrs Chain sat down in the chair opposite, lower than she would have liked, and arranged the umbrella.
[1039 Body Text] “What is it for?”
[1040 Body Text] “It’s for when the man’s already decided which set you’re having.” He almost smiled. “I used to say to the reps, don’t tell me it’s the best, tell me what’s wrong with it. They never would. The honest ones went pale and the other ones went into sales.”
[1041 Body Text] They watched the bus stop together for a while. A 412 came, and stopped for nobody, and went.
[1042 Body Text] “June used to get that one,” said Dennis. “To the school. She taught maths, did Lol tell you? Year Ten. Terrifying woman. Boys used to come in the shop on a Saturday and hide behind the aerials if she was on the counter.” He was quiet for a moment. “She’s gone. I know she is. Some days I know it and some days I don’t, and Lol thinks the days I don’t are the illness and the days I do are me.” He looked at Mrs Chain with sudden, shrewd curiosity. “Which do you think?”
[1043 Body Text] Mrs Chain thought about it. She did not believe in answering that kind of question quickly, because it was the kind people remembered.
[1044 Body Text] “I had a sister,” she said. “Margaret. She took her tea with too much milk and she laughed at the wrong jokes, and there was a matter between us, about our mother’s house, that I had not finished being angry about. Then the Ministry filed her somewhere I could not follow, and I was told, very kindly, by a very clever young woman, that she would not be coming back.” She folded her gloves, left over right. “Some mornings I put out two cups. I know perfectly well that she is not coming. I am not confused. I am simply not finished. I do not think the second cup is the illness, Mr Wren, and I have not got one.”
[1045 Body Text] Dennis nodded slowly. “Not finished,” he said. “That’s it. That’s a good word. That’s not a best sort of word.”
[1046 Body Text] “No.”
[1047 Body Text] “They’ll want me finished,” he said. “That’s what the form’s for. Secure side or Croydon. Tick, sign, done. And I’ll go upstairs where there’s no window, or down to Croydon where there’s a garden that smells of lavender on purpose, and I’ll be very well looked after, and nobody’ll bring me anything broken, and in a year I’ll be a man in a chair.” He said it without self-pity, as a diagnosis. “I’m not daft. I can feel the rooms going dark. I’d just like to keep the ones with the lights on as long as they’ll stay on. And the light’s here.” He nodded at the window, the bus stop, the 412 timetable fading in its frame. “And Sundays. The big lad comes Sundays and brings me rubbish people have broken, and I tell him he’s doing it wrong. A man’s got to have somebody to tell they’re doing it wrong, Mrs Chain, or he stops being a man and starts being a resident.”
[1048 Body Text] Mrs Chain considered him. Then she took the small notebook out of her handbag, the one she used for the gas and the doctor and things the Ministry had said to her in corridors, and uncapped her pen.
[1049 Body Text] “Say it again,” she said. “Slowly. In the words you want.”
[1050 Body Text] He said it again. She wrote it down: the window, and the buses, and Sundays, and somebody to tell they were doing it wrong. When she read it back he corrected one word. Rubbish was to stand. Broken was to be broken by fools.
[1051 Body Text] “You’ll give it to Lol,” he said.
[1052 Body Text] “I shall say it to her once, and plainly, and in front of whoever she likes,” said Mrs Chain. “I do not pass notes. I am too old, and notes get lost, and then somebody says the person must have changed their mind.” She put the notebook away. “And I shall say that you said it on a Tuesday, in July, sober, with the screwdriver down. Those things matter. People think the facts are the words. Half the facts are the circumstances.”
[1053 Body Text] “That’s what I used to tell customers,” said Dennis Wren. “Never mind what it’s doing. What were you doing when it started doing it?”
[1054 Body Text] In room four, Ivy Danks woke up and called for whoever was there. Mrs Chain went to her. On her way out of the lounge she looked back once. He had picked up the screwdriver again and was working, unhurried, on the radio’s back, with the window full of flat July light beside him and the 412 coming round the corner, lit up, empty, exactly on time.
[1055 Heading 2] Chapter Ten
[1056 Heading 3] Which Way the Galaxy Is Running
[1057 First Paragraph] Viktor Eugenius did not write in. He telephoned, from Cambridge, and asked for Beatrix Sloan by name, and said one sentence.
[1058 Body Text] “You are relying on a theorem. I should like to object.”
[1059 Body Text] He was ninety-three, Hungarian by birth and by vowel, and he arrived on the Wednesday in a suit that had been very good in 1974, and he began before he sat down, which Lolly had by then learned was the mark of everybody worth having in the building.
[1060 Body Text] “I am not a sceptic by temperament,” he said. “I am not here to tell you that mathematics is a swindle.”
[1061 Body Text] “Then what are you here to tell us?” asked Fainrose.
[1062 Body Text] “That your protocol contains a category error,” said Viktor Eugenius, “and that it is the most respectable category error in the history of science, and that everybody in this building has made it, including me, for sixty years, and that it stops being harmless in about two hundred and ten days.”
[1063 Body Text] He warmed up by demolishing his oldest friend’s most famous essay, the one on the unreasonable effectiveness of mathematics, in under four minutes and standing, the way other men crack their knuckles. It was not a miracle, he said. Mathematics got into physics wherever it proved useful, and we called those places physics and the rest history. “If you fire ten thousand arrows and seven hit the target, and you frame the seven, you will develop a wonderful theory of your own marksmanship.”
[1064 Body Text] Stadthof said, “You’re taking away my Braid.”
[1065 Body Text] “I am taking away your miracle, Mr Stadthof, which is not the same thing, and you will feel better in a fortnight.”
[1066 Body Text] “And now the part that concerns my two hundred and nine days,” said Fainrose, and heard it as she said it. “Two hundred and nine. The same as Miss Pike’s recorders. I did not arrange that; the calendar did, and I should like it minuted that I noticed before anybody could make something of it.” Beatrix minuted it. “You did not come from Cambridge to review a sixty-year-old essay.”
[1067 Body Text] “No.” Eugenius put a single sheet on the table. “I came because of paragraph 9 of Ms Sloan’s draft protocol, which reads: a party which has not maintained held correlations cannot attain a score above the classical ceiling, the ceiling being a theorem and not a standard.”
[1068 Body Text] “That’s Nashville’s point and it’s correct.”
[1069 Body Text] “It is correct within the formalism,” said Viktor Eugenius. “A theorem constrains a model. It constrains a world only to the extent that the world is faithfully described by the framework in which the theorem was proved, and that is not a theorem. It is an empirical claim, with a domain of validity, and it has never been tested in the regime you are now in.”
[1070 Body Text] Beatrix said, “Say the regime.”
[1071 Body Text] “Every experiment that has ever confirmed your ceiling — and there are thousands, and they are excellent, and I believe every one of them — was performed against a fixed background. The vacuum stayed where it was. Nobody had lowered the price of nothing by eleven parts in ten to the twenty-second while the apparatus was running.” He tapped the sheet. “You have discovered that the background is an editable quantity. And you have then gone on to build an enforcement mechanism whose entire security rests on a bound derived by assuming it is not.”
[1072 Body Text] “Give me the number,” said Fainrose. “You have one.”
[1073 Body Text] “I have three.” Eugenius held up a hand. “Your classical ceiling is two. Your quantum ceiling — the one Ms Sloan’s protocol relies upon — is two point eight two eight, and everybody in this room thinks of that as the ceiling imposed by the universe.
[1074 Body Text] “It is not. The ceiling imposed by relativity alone — by the requirement that no correlation can be used to send a signal, which is the only thing we know from principle rather than from formalism — is four.”
[1075 Body Text] No one spoke. Stadthof, who had been promised there would be no more mathematics after the Braid, looked betrayed.
[1076 Body Text] “There is a perfectly consistent hypothetical device, in the literature since nineteen ninety-four, which scores four. It sends no signal. Relativity has no objection to it. Quantum theory forbids it, on an axiom I believe and cannot prove survives in a volume where somebody is rewriting screens.” He sat back. “And Ms Sloan has drafted a protocol which reads a high score as proof of good standing.”
[1077 Body Text] Beatrix Sloan said, “Then a cheat scores above the quantum bound.”
[1078 Body Text] “A cheat with a vacuum budget and no held correlations at all,” said Eugenius, “scores three point four, and your protocol certifies him as the most compliant member of the nine, and every honest party sitting at two point eight is marked down as a laggard. You have not built a system that is impossible to fake. You have built one where the only way to fake it is to be the one entity in the galaxy who has been editing screens, and where the fake presents as excellence.”
[1079 Body Text] Beatrix did not look at the draft. She had sent it to the nine on Friday with a covering note that said, in her own words, this cannot be gamed. Lolly watched her take the pen out of her pocket, look at it, and put it back.
[1080 Body Text] It was Priya Ramanathan who came back at it, from a direction no one expected, and Lolly afterwards thought that this was the moment the department stopped receiving experts and started having an argument of its own.
[1081 Body Text] “Professor Eugenius,” she said. “I accept the whole of that and I want to hand you a bill for it.”
[1082 Body Text] “By all means.”
[1083 Body Text] “You’ve told us mathematics works only where we let it in, and that we call the places we let it in physics. Fine. But you haven’t asked the second question, which is why any place lets it in at all. Why is there a regime where three lines of algebra predict a planet for a thousand years? Selection explains which subjects we admitted to the club. It doesn’t explain why the club exists.”
[1084 Body Text] Eugenius put his hands together and said, “Go on. This is why I telephoned.”
[1085 Body Text] “Decoherence.” Ramanathan went to the board and did not write on it. “The environment watches everything, all the time. Every photon, every air molecule. Watching destroys the delicate combinations almost at once, and what survives is the small set of states the environment keeps copying: the simple, stable, local ones. The ones with short descriptions.”
[1086 Body Text] She turned round.
[1087 Body Text] “Mathematics is unreasonably effective because we are living downstream of a filter that removes everything mathematics could not have handled. There was never a miracle. There is a sieve, and the sieve is decoherence, and it runs on the environment, and the environment is—”
[1088 Body Text] She stopped.
[1089 Body Text] Fainrose said it.
[1090 Body Text] “—the screens.”
[1091 Body Text] “The screens,” said Ramanathan. “Full subscription is what continuous monitoring looks like from inside. That is what the Programme was buying, all that time. Not tidiness. Not safety. Classicality.”
[1092 Body Text] Viktor Eugenius sat for some time without saying anything at all.
[1093 Body Text] “I have been arguing that essay for sixty-five years,” he said eventually, “and I did not expect to be answered on a Wednesday in Marsham Street by a woman with a plot of a supernova in her hand.”
[1094 Body Text] “I’d rather I hadn’t been right,” said Ramanathan.
[1095 Body Text] “So would I, madam.” He looked round the table. “If the effectiveness of mathematics is a service, it has a supplier, and the supplier’s line item was cancelled in Vienna. The failure will not announce itself as physics breaking down. It will look like things becoming slightly less predictable than they used to be, wherever nobody keeps good records, for about a century. And then all at once.”
[1096 Body Text] ∗
[1097 First Paragraph] Szmulek Barbarian did not come to Marsham Street. He had not entered a government building since nineteen ninety-three, and said so on the telephone before anybody could use it against him. Then he said the other thing. “The Office of the President has written to me. Very courteously. They would like my view of your paragraph 11 in writing, and they have enclosed the stamp. The last collection from the box opposite my door is at half past five on Friday. If you would like to argue with me before then, you will have to do it in Kilburn.”
[1098 Body Text] So on the Friday Lolly, Beatrix, Ramanathan and Miss Pike took the Bakerloo line to Kilburn Park and climbed a staircase beside a launderette to a flat with two chairs, four thousand books and a window that rattled whenever the dryers downstairs changed cycle. On the table, beside a teapot in a knitted cosy, lay a long envelope addressed to the Office of the President, sealed and stamped. Across the road the post box said LAST COLLECTION 17.30. Beatrix, who did not sit in other people’s chairs, stood by the window where she could see it.
[1099 Body Text] “I translated Russian physics journals for a living for twenty-five years,” he said, before anyone had taken off a coat. “Four hundred physicists a year, at a rate per thousand words. You notice, eventually, that they are all using a word which none of them defines and which does no work in any of their equations.”
[1100 Body Text] Ramanathan said, “Time.”
[1101 Body Text] “Time.” He sat down in the better of the two chairs, because it was his. He was seventy-six, very neat, with the stillness of somebody who has spent thirty years thinking in a room without a telephone. “I am going to say something that will make everybody want to be polite to me, and I would rather you were rude. I do not think time exists. I think it is what a particular kind of correlation feels like from inside, and I have thought so for thirty-one years and been wrong about the date of the revolution every single time.”
[1102 Body Text] Lolly said, “Then let’s be rude. My department has two hundred and seven days left. What is that, if there’s no time?”
[1103 Body Text] “It is a number written in your files which appears in a certain relation to other numbers written in your files,” said Szmulek Barbarian. “And I am not being clever, Ms Wren, I am being literal, and I am about to tell you why it matters to your protocol, because it does, and nobody else will tell you.”
[1104 Body Text] He did the physics in three sentences, sitting still. The equations ran just as well backwards. The arrow came only from a beginning so improbable that physicists called it a boundary condition, which was what they said when they had agreed not to ask. And when you quantised gravity honestly the clock fell out of the equation altogether: the wavefunction of everything simply was, with no date on it.
[1105 Body Text] “My position, and here I am the crank and content to be, is that nothing has been left out. There is a vast, still space of possible arrangements of everything, none of them now. A few are very special. They contain records of one another. A rock with strata. A photograph. A green notebook with a date at the top.” He looked at Lolly. “I call those time capsules. The sense of having come from somewhere is what it is like to be one.”
[1106 Body Text] “Right,” said Lolly. “Now do the damage. You said on the telephone this matters to the protocol.”
[1107 Body Text] “It gutters the protocol,” said Barbarian, “and I am sorry, because I read the draft on Monday, next to the Office’s letter, and it is a beautiful piece of work.
[1108 Body Text] “Paragraph 11. A party shall demonstrate maintained subscription by achieving the quantum score at intervals not exceeding one century. Ms Sloan, that clause presupposes that there is a fact about what happened between the intervals. It does not measure maintenance. It measures a configuration, twice, and infers a history connecting them.
[1109 Body Text] “But if history is nothing more than records that agree with one another, then you are not testing a century of maintenance. You are testing whether a party can present records that look like one.” He let that sit. “And that can be met by a party who has maintained nothing at all and has instead built the records.”
[1110 Body Text] He put two fingers on the envelope. “That is what I have written to the Office of the President. Four pages. It is true, and they will quote it in Vienna as the considered view of an independent physicist, and they will be right to, because it is.” The window rattled. “It is twenty to one. I am perfectly willing to be talked out of it by twenty-five past five, and I warn you that nobody has managed it in thirty-one years.”
[1111 Body Text] Miss Pike said quietly, “Column B.”
[1112 Body Text] “Column B?” said Szmulek Barbarian.
[1113 Body Text] “There was a column,” said Miss Pike. “In the year before last. I fabricated it in four minutes because the directive required a certainty I did not have, and I confessed inside the hour, and it is in the schedule to the Act with my name on it.” She had gone slightly grey again. “Mr Barbarian, you have just described what I did, at the scale of a galaxy.”
[1114 Body Text] “Then you are the only person in this room who has already run the experiment,” said Szmulek Barbarian, “and I should very much like to know what it cost you, because that is the thing I have never been able to calculate.”
[1115 Body Text] It took Ramanathan and Miss Pike four and a half hours at Mr Barbarian’s kitchen table, between the dictionaries and a stack of Soviet proofs he let them write on the backs of, and it was the best thing the department produced that year.
[1116 Body Text] ∗
[1117 First Paragraph] In the second of those hours, at a kitchen table in Kilburn, Adaeze Pike found that she was answering Mr Barbarian’s question in her head instead of doing the arithmetic in front of her.
[1118 Body Text] What it had cost. Nobody had ever asked her that. People had asked her why, and how, and whether she was all right. The cost was in the schedule to the Act with her name on it, and everybody could read a schedule.
[1119 Body Text] Ramanathan was at the kitchen door, writing on the back of a Soviet proof with Mr Barbarian’s pencil. Beatrix was watching the post box as though it might leave early. Miss Pike had the calculator, the redundancy tables and a cheese sandwich Mr Barbarian had made her without asking, which she had not eaten.
[1120 Body Text] What it had cost was this. Before Column B she had believed that a record was a thing you made, and that if you made it well enough it would be true. After Column B she knew that a record was a thing you made, and that the world kept its own, in far more places than you could reach, and that the world’s copy always won. She had written one column of figures in four minutes. The world had written the true figures into everything it owned. It had taken under an hour for the two to meet, and she had been standing between them when they did.
[1121 Body Text] She had grown up in Peckham, in a house where the past was kept in shoeboxes labelled by year; her mother had once produced the receipt for a 1998 kettle at a county court, against a retailer who had been very confident until that minute. The morning after Column B, Miss Pike had bought a hardback ledger from a stationer in Victoria Street, and since then she had written everything down twice: once in the system, and once in a book the system could not edit. Every certified copy that left Marsham Street went into it in her own hand. She had never once had occasion to look anything up. It was for the morning somebody with a warrant asked whether she was sure, so that she could put her hand flat on a page and say yes.
[1122 Body Text] “Adaeze,” said Ramanathan, without turning round. “Column B. One copy, yours. How many copies did the world hold of the real figures by the time anybody looked?”
[1123 Body Text] She had it. She had worked it out at fourteen minutes past one, while she was thinking about shoeboxes.
[1124 Body Text] “About ten to the eleven,” she said. “Order of magnitude. Every reading, every log, every person who remembered their own bill. That’s the world’s copies. Mine was one.”
[1125 Body Text] Ramanathan wrote 1 : 10¹¹ across the back of the proof, and looked at it, and then looked at Miss Pike with an expression Miss Pike had not seen on her before and later identified as respect.
[1126 Body Text] “That’s the answer,” Ramanathan said. “You’ve just written the answer.”
[1127 Body Text] It was ten past four. For the first time since one o’clock Miss Pike ate the sandwich. Her mother, she thought, would have kept the receipt.
[1128 Body Text] ∗
[1129 First Paragraph] “He’s right about the requirement,” said Ramanathan, “and he’s wrong about the practicality, and the reason is the redundancy argument.
[1130 Body Text] “A forged past is not one record. It cannot be.” She turned the proof round so that Barbarian could read it. “To be believed, a history has to be redundant: written into many independent places that never coordinated. And copies cost surface. Every record written into the world uses area on a screen, and there is no discount for the record being false.”
[1131 Body Text] Beatrix said, slowly, “So forgery has a price.”
[1132 Body Text] “Forgery has a price and it is the same price as the truth, plus the coordination overhead of making the copies look uncoordinated, which is the expensive part.” Ramanathan almost smiled. “Mr Barbarian, that is what Column B cost. One copy against ten to the eleven is not a dispute. It is arithmetic.
[1133 Body Text] “To forge a century across nine signatories, you would have to write about as much onto the screens as honest maintenance would have written, and you cannot do that on a slack screen. The only entity that can forge the record is one with surface to spare, and the only way to get surface to spare is to clear some, and clearing is what our four instruments see.”
[1134 Body Text] Beatrix said, from the window, “Then we rewrite paragraph 11. Don’t test the score. Test the redundancy.”
[1135 Body Text] “Test the redundancy,” said Ramanathan, “with the copies lodged with parties who can be shown to hold none in common, which is Mrs Kind’s clause from Monday, and makes the Vienna room a working part of the protocol rather than a courtesy. And it kills Professor Eugenius’s cheat on the way past. A vacuum budget can push a score above the quantum bound. It cannot manufacture independent copies. The test stops being how high did you score and becomes who else can vouch for it.”
[1136 Body Text] Szmulek Barbarian sat for a while with his hands on the table.
[1137 Body Text] “Thirty-one years,” he said. “I have been telling people that history is nothing but the consistency of records, and every one of them has heard it as a licence, as though the past were cheap. And a civil servant’s analyst has just told me, with a number, at my own kitchen table, that it is exactly as expensive as it appears to be, and that this is why it is real.”
[1138 Body Text] It was twenty-two minutes past five. He picked up the envelope. He did not open it. He took a fountain pen from the jar on the table and wrote on the back of the sealed envelope, in a small upright hand that had copied four hundred physicists a year for twenty-five years:
[1139 Body Text] P.S. Paragraphs 3 to 9 of the enclosed are withdrawn. They have been answered with a number by an analyst of the Ministry, Miss A. Pike, whom I name with her permission. — He stopped and looked at Miss Pike.
[1140 Body Text] “May I?” he said. “It will go to the Office. They will know your name.”
[1141 Body Text] Miss Pike thought about the ledger in her desk drawer in Marsham Street, which no one in the Office knew existed, and about the morning somebody with a warrant would ask her whether she was sure.
[1142 Body Text] “Yes,” she said. “Spell it properly. Two e’s and a z.”
[1143 Body Text] He spelled it properly and initialled it. At five twenty-six he crossed the High Road against the lights, with forty years’ confidence, and posted the letter with the postscript facing up. The van came at half past. Lolly watched it go and understood that the Office of the President would read, on Monday, that paragraph 11 was being rewritten, and by whom. The department had paid for Mr Barbarian’s honesty by telling its opponent what it was about to do.
[1144 Body Text] “I would like it in writing as well, please,” said Szmulek Barbarian, on the pavement, with the launderette breathing warm air on all of them. “Mr van Casteel has a receipt. I should like a receipt.”
[1145 Body Text] Van Casteel’s twelve notebooks, meanwhile, had gone to a numerical analyst on secondment and to Miss Pike, and the result came back the following Friday, and Miss Pike would not present it standing up.
[1146 Body Text] “The store in the Cumbrian chamber has been decaying since at least two thousand and seven, which is when Hendrik started measuring,” she said. “The decay is not exponential. It is exponential with steps.”
[1147 Body Text] “Steps how?”
[1148 Body Text] “There are seven intervals in nineteen years — averaging about ten months each, irregularly spaced — during which the decay does not merely slow. It reverses.” She put the plot on the table. “Something was putting correlation back into that chamber. Seven times. Between two thousand and eight and two thousand and twenty-two. And there are no instruments in the leased rooms and no Programme field team has visited Cumbria since nineteen sixty-three, and the service charge for replenishment has not been drawn on the common fund since—” she checked, unnecessarily — “nineteen sixty-one.”
[1149 Body Text] Fainrose said, “Then who has been paying it?”
[1150 Body Text] Nobody wanted to say it, so Mrs Kind did. She had spent twenty-two years telling people things about their arrangements that they had not wished to hear.
[1151 Body Text] “Somebody who acquires correlation as a by-product of eating,” she said, “and who has told us to our faces that he wants the ledger preserved, and who has been standing in our lobby waiting to be asked, and who has gone from comfortable to visibly thin in the four months since something stopped him feeding.”
[1152 Body Text] “Seven top-ups,” said Lolly. “Fourteen years. Out of his own — what would you even call it.”
[1153 Body Text] “Dinner,” said Mrs Kind.
[1154 Body Text] Fainrose had to face the nine on the Thursday, by relay, and she had prepared sixteen minutes and used four.
[1155 Body Text] “You have asked me to certify direction,” she said, “and I am going to refuse, in public, now, rather than in a footnote in twenty years.
[1156 Body Text] “The protocol can certify that correlations are held. It can certify redundancy. It can certify that records are mutually consistent. What it cannot do — not from poor drafting, not from a lack of instruments, but in principle — is tell you which way the galaxy is running.
[1157 Body Text] “So here is what I will and will not sign. I will sign that if you hold your correlations and lodge your copies, your compliance is demonstrable. I will not sign that in a volume where screens go slack, your records will continue to distinguish the direction they point in. A weakening arrow does not look like time reversing. It looks like archives becoming ambiguous about which entry came first. It looks like two parties both sincerely remembering being the one who was owed. It looks like a hundred centuries of arrangements becoming, very slowly, matters of opinion.”
[1158 Body Text] The delegate for the Fourth Concordance said, after a pause, “Dr Fainrose, has that been observed?”
[1159 Body Text] “Ask me on the tenth of February,” said Fainrose.
[1160 Heading 2] Chapter Eleven
[1161 Heading 3] The Argument from Being Here
[1162 First Paragraph] Nashville went to Vienna on the Monday, with nothing, and would not be accompanied.
[1163 Body Text] He telephoned at twenty past nine on the Tuesday evening and Lolly took it standing in the corridor with a pencil and no paper and wrote on the back of her hand.
[1164 Body Text] “There are two chairs,” said Nashville, “and I have been sitting in one of them for six hours and he arrived at four.”
[1165 Body Text] “He came.”
[1166 Body Text] “He came at four in the afternoon and he stood in the corridor for forty minutes because nobody had asked him, and I went out and asked him, and he wept, Ms Wren, and I want you to be careful about how you record that, because he has eleven thousand years in him and he did not do it prettily and he would not want it in a schedule.”
[1167 Body Text] Lolly said, “Did he answer?”
[1168 Body Text] “I asked him the question the widow of Ravenna asked for nine years,” said Jack Nashville. “Whether what had been taken was a thing anybody needed. He said that between thirteen forty-seven and two thousand and eight the answer was no, on every occasion, without exception, his own years and the inherited ones alike, and that whoever was carrying it had known it at the time and had eaten anyway.
[1169 Body Text] “And then I asked him about the seven intervals, because your Miss Pike telegraphed me the plot, and he said — I wrote this down, and it is the only thing I wrote down all day —”
[1170 Body Text] “Mr Nashville, I did not do it out of remorse. I did it because I am a scavenger and a scavenger who eats the last of anything is a fool. I was maintaining my larder. I would like that understood, because I have been dishonest about a great many small matters and I have never once been dishonest about what I am.”
[1171 Body Text] “And your verdict?”
[1172 Body Text] “My verdict is that it does not matter in the slightest why he did it,” said Jack Nashville, “and that is the whole of what an unaffiliated observer is for. He put correlation into a sealed chamber in Cumbria seven times over fourteen years at his own cost, while the Counsel’s replenishment line had been dead since nineteen sixty-one. That is a fact, and I am the only person in the galaxy entitled to say so, and I will sign it in the morning.
[1173 Body Text] “And Ms Wren. He asked me a question in return and I could not answer it, so I am handing it to you.” A pause on the line. “He asked whether, once an outsider had verified him, he was still permitted to require an invitation. He has stood in lobbies for six hundred and eighty-eight years on the grounds that his own certificate is worthless. If somebody else’s certificate is now good, he does not know what he is any more. He did not ask it as philosophy.”
[1174 Body Text] Lolly wrote, at one in the morning:
[1175 Body Text] Nashville: it does not matter why he did it. That is what an outsider is for.
[1176 Body Text] His question, which I cannot answer: if
 an outsider’s certificate is good, may he still require an invitation? He
 meant it plainly.
[1177 Body Text] ∗
[1178 First Paragraph] The paper arrived from the Office of the President on a Tuesday, five weeks early, and Jack Nashville, who was still in Vienna in a room with two chairs, was told about it by telephone and said only: “Then he has somebody good. I had assumed he did not.”
[1179 Body Text] There were two parts to it, and the department spent four days on the first and a week and a half on the second, and got the order wrong, which Lolly afterwards recorded as her own error and not Beatrix’s.
[1180 Body Text] Part one was the game.
[1181 Body Text] It was set out with complete propriety on two pages: a certifying game, proposed by a party as the instrument permitted, with the referee’s question distribution specified, the winning predicate specified, the scoring interval specified. It was finite. It was explicit. It was, as far as anybody could see, entirely regular.
[1182 Body Text] Nashville had it read to him down the line, and then said:
[1183 Body Text] “That is a fine object. Whoever built that knew what he was doing. You cannot compute its value and you cannot prove that you cannot, not in the sense of producing a bound — approximating the best achievable score of that game is equivalent to deciding whether a machine halts, and the machine they have embedded in it is one no one has ever settled. It will run for ever and it will look, from the outside, like an audit in progress.”
[1184 Body Text] Beatrix said, “So we refuse it.”
[1185 Body Text] “On what ground? The instrument permits any party to propose a game, and the proposal complies in every particular. Refuse it and you have a body determining the scope of its own competence, which is your motion from Vienna aimed at your own head.”
[1186 Body Text] And then there was a pause on the line, and Beatrix Sloan said, in a voice Lolly had not heard from her before:
[1187 Body Text] “Jack. We don’t test the score any more.”
[1188 Body Text] “Say that again.”
[1189 Body Text] “We rewrote paragraph 11 five weeks ago, on Mr Barbarian’s objection. We don’t test whether a party can achieve a score. We test the redundancy of their records — how many independent, uncoordinated copies exist, and whether the holders can be shown not to hold copies in common.” She was already turning pages. “A proposed game is now evidentially irrelevant. He can propose a game whose value is undecidable and we will say: quite so, and here is the schedule of copies, and the undecidability of your game bears on nothing we rely upon.”
[1190 Body Text] The line was quiet for a while.
[1191 Body Text] “He was five weeks early,” said Nashville.
[1192 Body Text] “Five weeks,” said Beatrix. “Yes.”
[1193 Body Text] “Ms Sloan, I want you to write down somewhere that the only reason this department survived Tuesday is that a man who denies the existence of time made a pedantic objection to a subordinate clause in July.” A dry sound. “Now read me part two. If the game was the feint, part two is the blow.”
[1194 Body Text] Part two was four paragraphs and it did not contain a single number.
[1195 Body Text] The Office notes the amendment to paragraph 11 and does not object to it.
[1196 Body Text] “They had Mr Barbarian’s postscript on the Monday,” said Lolly. “We told them ourselves.” No one answered that. It had been the right thing to do, and it had cost exactly what it looked like.
[1197 Body Text] The Office observes that any volume in which observers are present is necessarily a volume in which conditions have been sufficient for the emergence and persistence of observers. Were subscription in this volume inadequate, the parties now complaining would not exist to complain. The presence of complainants is therefore itself evidence of adequacy, and the Office invites the session to have regard to it.
[1198 Body Text] The Office further observes that the parties’ own physics establishes that our volume’s conditions lie within an exceedingly narrow band, and that no explanation of this fact has ever been offered other than that observers necessarily find themselves in such a band. The Office adopts that reasoning. It does not originate with the Office. It is the settled view of the parties’ own literature and has been for fifty years.
[1199 Body Text] Professor M. Tengelman is thanked again, with less confidence, for paragraphs 2 and 3.
[1200 Body Text] Fainrose read it standing up and then sat down and said, “Get Eugenius.”
[1201 Body Text] Viktor Eugenius came on the Thursday and read the four paragraphs and put them down and said:
[1202 Body Text] “That is the cleverest wicked thing I have read in sixty years, and I want everyone to understand why it is wicked, because it is not wicked in the way you think. It does not contain a false statement. Again. That is now the third document from that office which is entirely accurate.”
[1203 Body Text] “Then take it apart,” said Lolly.
[1204 Body Text] “Very well. The physics first and the swindle second.”
[1205 Body Text] “The constants of nature,” he said. “Make the strong force a few per cent stronger or weaker and there is no chemistry worth the name. Make the energy of the vacuum a little larger and there are no galaxies. We sit in a narrow band, and the only explanation anybody has ever offered is that observers can only find themselves inside such a band. That is the weak anthropic principle. It is a statement about samples. It has made one famous prediction about carbon that came true. And it is not a theory of anything.”
[1206 Body Text] Beatrix said, “Then the Office’s paragraph three is sound?”
[1207 Body Text] “Paragraph three is sound and empty,” said Eugenius, “and here is the swindle, and it is in the joint between paragraph three and paragraph two, and it is done so smoothly that I read it four times before I saw the seam.
[1208 Body Text] “But paragraph two does something different and it is not physics at all. Paragraph two says: since you are here to complain, nothing has gone wrong.”
[1209 Body Text] He put his finger on the page.
[1210 Body Text] “Look at what that does. Anthropic reasoning is a selection effect on observation. It licenses exactly one conclusion: do not be surprised. The Office has turned it into a licence for anything survivable. Every harm short of extinction becomes self-refuting, because anyone left to complain is proof that the conditions permitted complainers.
[1211 Body Text] “And observe the direction. It is a bound on the worst the conditions could have been, not a certificate that they were good. It says the walls cannot have been so slack that nobody survived. Which we knew, madam, because we are all sitting here. It says nothing about two hundred and six cores, or fourteen tunnellings, or a hundred and three unrecovered records, and it will be reported as science anyway, because the sentence is true and the physicists who might contradict it cannot form a queue.”
[1212 Body Text] Ramanathan said, “So how do we answer it?”
[1213 Body Text] “You do not answer it,” said Viktor Eugenius. “You date it. The anthropic argument is about the conditions up to now. It has no forward content at all, because surviving to the present is consistent with any change beginning tomorrow. Paragraph two is a certificate about the past written in the tense of a guarantee, and if you can make forty-one signatories feel the difference between those tenses, you will have done more for this galaxy than any of my papers.”
[1214 Body Text] The Fourth Concordance was the blow, and it landed on the Friday, and it did not come from the Office at all. It came in a message from the Concordance itself, transmitted apologetically through the registry, and it was signed by the delegate who had twice spoken in the minutes.
[1215 Body Text] The Fourth Concordance had said as much in Vienna, about its own four hundred years of keeping its volume subscribed, and been thanked and minuted. Under the old paragraph 11 that had been a matter of assertion and standing. Under the new paragraph 11 it was a matter of lodged, independent, uncoordinated copies.
[1216 Body Text] And the Concordance had them. It had kept four hundred years of records with exemplary care, in the medium its people had used for such things since before their accession: correlation with a nearby stellar object — the records held not as marks on a surface but as correlations between their instruments and a star, replenished each generation, requiring the star’s continued state to decode.
[1217 Body Text] The star was at twelve degrees off the plane.
[1218 Body Text] “Say it plainly,” said Fainrose, when the room had been quiet for long enough.
[1219 Body Text] “Their archive was keyed to the progenitor,” Ramanathan answered. “It went core-collapse some four thousand years early, in a window consistent with the fourteenth of March. Everything they hold is now undecodable. Not lost. Unreadable. They have four hundred years of perfectly genuine, perfectly redundant compliance records and no key, and there is no key anywhere, because the key was a star and the star is a remnant.”
[1220 Body Text] Miss Pike started, “Then under our own clause—”
[1221 Body Text] “Under our own clause, the Fourth Concordance cannot demonstrate redundancy,” said Beatrix Sloan, “and is therefore not admissible among the nine, and the Office of the President has already filed a motion to that effect, dated Wednesday, which is two days before the Concordance told us.”
[1222 Body Text] “They knew first.”
[1223 Body Text] “They have the map,” said Mrs Kind.
[1224 Body Text] Lolly said, “Do we have a way out?”
[1225 Body Text] “There is one and it is disgusting,” said Beatrix. “The Concordance’s copies exist. They are simply not decodable by us. But redundancy, as Dr Ramanathan defines it, is a count of independent fragments carrying the record — and the definition does not require that we can read them. It requires that they be there and be independent.”
[1226 Body Text] “Then we amend the clause to accept unreadable copies.”
[1227 Body Text] “And the moment we do, every fabricated archive in the galaxy is admissible on the grounds that its copies are merely awaiting a key,” said Beatrix. “Which is the whole of the protection we bought from Mr Barbarian’s objection. The only test that defeats a forged past is one that requires the copies to be readable. And the only member with four hundred genuine years cannot produce a readable copy, because the entity we are trying to restrain destroyed the key four thousand years early, in an event which is item seven of Annex C, which is the thing the Programme existed to prevent.”
[1228 Body Text] Szmulek Barbarian, who had come to Marsham Street that morning on the 98 bus, his first government building since nineteen ninety-three, and had been in the corner all afternoon without speaking, said:
[1229 Body Text] “Then the Concordance is a rock whose strata are intact and whose light is gone.” He looked up. “I have been telling you for five weeks that a past is expensive and therefore real. Here is a party whose records are entirely consistent and entirely mute, and I do not know whether they have a past or not, and I am ashamed of how interesting I find that.”
[1230 Body Text] ∗
[1231 First Paragraph] Lolly stayed when the room emptied. They have the map, Mrs Kind had said, and in May she had said the other thing, the thing no one had minuted because it was rude: your Statement is a picking list. A map told you where the walls were thin. It did not tell you whose names were written on the thin parts. Somebody had needed both.
[1232 Body Text] She sat down at Mavis-9’s terminal in the corner, where the screen saver was the Ministry crest turning slowly round as if looking for the rest of its motto, and said, “Mavis. Certified copies of the Statement of Coherence with Schedule 4. All of them, since publication.”
[1233 Body Text] A pause of about four seconds, which for Mavis-9 was a sabbatical.
[1234 Body Text] “One certified copy has been issued,” said Mavis-9. “The date is withheld. The requester is withheld under Registry Rule 40, signatory confidentiality. The format is non-standard. Thank you for your enquiry. Your enquiry has been noted.”
[1235 Body Text] “Noted by whom?”
[1236 Body Text] “Non-standard means that the sort order differs from the default, which is by district. Further detail is withheld under Rule 40.” Another pause. “That question has also been noted.”
[1237 Body Text] “Mavis. Who is noting them?”
[1238 Body Text] “The requester asked to be informed of any enquiry about the request. Rule 40 permits this. Have a pleasant evening, Ms Wren.”
[1239 Body Text] Lolly sat back. Somewhere, in a building she had never seen, somebody was now reading that a minute-taker in Marsham Street had asked. It was the first thing all year that the other side had been made to read because she had written it.
[1240 Body Text] She wrote: One copy. Sorted differently. They know I asked. Then she filled in Form R/40, an application for a requester’s name to be released “where the public interest so requires”, which was the kind of phrase a registry puts in a rule so that it can say it has one, and took it to the post box on Horseferry Road herself, because the Programme had a hundred and sixty-five days left and she did not trust the internal mail with anything that had a deadline.
[1241 Body Text] In the morning she asked Miss Pike. Certified copies crossed Miss Pike’s desk. Miss Pike was at the kettle. She said that copies of the Statement went out every week and that she would look, and at lunchtime she came to Lolly’s desk and said, “The system says withheld,” which was true, and went back to the kettle. Lolly, who had a hundred and sixty-four days and a Concordance to save, wrote Pike: system says withheld, and did not notice for three months that it was the answer to a question she had not asked.
[1242 Body Text] ∗
[1243 First Paragraph] Eilstein’s question was answered on the Saturday, in the afternoon, in the third-floor briefing room. Beatrix had insisted on a room and not the lobby, and Lolly had insisted it be done by Beatrix and not by herself.
[1244 Body Text] He had asked whether, now that an unaffiliated observer had verified him, he might still insist on being invited.
[1245 Body Text] “You are not,” said Beatrix Sloan.
[1246 Body Text] Eilstein waited.
[1247 Body Text] “I want to give you the reasoning rather than the ruling, because it is going to be worse than the ruling,” she went on. “You have believed the invitation rule was a discipline you accepted because your own certificate is worthless. It was never about you. Schedule four, paragraph nine protects the person whose state it is. Your need to be asked is an incident of their right, not a property of your nature. You have organised your existence around a rule that was never addressed to you.”
[1248 Body Text] “I had worked that out,” said Eilstein. “Not on the Saturday. In about fourteen hundred and ten.”
[1249 Body Text] “Then here is the part you had not.” Beatrix did not soften it. “Mr Nashville’s certificate is good. It has been lodged and it will be relied upon, and its effect is that you now have standing. You are a party of record. A witness. And a witness, Mr Eilstein, may be summoned. You waited centuries in lobbies for that verdict. You got it in Vienna in six hours, and the price is that no lobby in this galaxy will ever again be a place where you are entitled to wait. You can be compelled now, lawfully, precisely because you were verified.”
[1250 Body Text] Albrecht Eilstein stood at the window for some time with his back to the room.
[1251 Body Text] “You know what I could say,” he said eventually.
[1252 Body Text] “Yes,” said Beatrix.
[1253 Body Text] “Say it for me, please. I should like to hear whether it sounds as cheap out loud.”
[1254 Body Text] “You could say that you are still here after all this time, and that if it had been intolerable the universe would have stopped you, and that your continued existence is therefore evidence that your conduct fell within permitted parameters.” Beatrix’s voice was perfectly level. “It is the Office’s paragraph two with a longer run of data. You have a better version of the argument than the President does, because your sample is a hundred centuries and his is four years.”
[1255 Body Text] “It sounds exactly as cheap as I thought,” said Eilstein. “And I shall not be using it, and I want that minuted, because I have used every other argument available to a creature in my position and this is the only one I have ever declined.” He turned round. “It is the argument from being here, Ms Sloan, and I have watched it made in nine languages by men standing on the results of a massacre, and it has never once been made by anybody who had a better one available.”
[1256 Body Text] He picked up his hat.
[1257 Body Text] “Summon me,” he said. “I have a hundred and sixty-four days of standing about and I would rather be used. And Ms Wren — the widow in Ravenna asked the same question in her ninth year, of the one who carried this before me, and he lied to her, and she died believing he was permitted. I inherited the lie with everything else. It has had six hundred and seventy-nine years to be considered, and my considered position is that I would have told it too, and that this is precisely why the rule cannot be left to me.”
[1258 Body Text] That night Lolly wrote:
[1259 Body Text] Verified means summonable. He went 688 years without an outside check, and the price of having one is the lobby. 
He would not use the argument from being here. He is the only one in this file who wouldn’t.
[1260 Body Text] That is the most honest sentence anybody has said to me since the Annex.
[1261 Body Text] ∗
[1262 First Paragraph] On the Sunday Gideon took a Hacker Herald to Larchfield in a carrier bag, because a woman in Lambeth had put it out with the bins with a note on it that said WORKS (DOESN’T), and he thought Dennis would enjoy the note.
[1263 Body Text] Dennis enjoyed the note. He read it out twice, once to Gideon and once to Tomasz, who was passing with a mop and stopped to hear it because on Sundays the lounge was where the entertainment was.
[1264 Body Text] “Works, doesn’t,” said Dennis. “That’s every set I ever had in the shop. Put it on the table, son. Let’s have a look at her.”
[1265 Body Text] They had a look at her. Gideon had the back off; Dennis watched his hands. He did not touch anything any more if he could help it, because his own hands had started doing things he had not told them to. He watched, and he talked, and he was right about one time in three, and on the other two occasions he was right about something else.
[1266 Body Text] The Herald hummed when Gideon powered it, a loud, steady, ugly hum, and played nothing.
[1267 Body Text] “Smoothing cap,” said Gideon.
[1268 Body Text] “Could be,” said Dennis, in the voice that meant it could not.
[1269 Body Text] Gideon checked the smoothing capacitor. It was fine. He checked the rectifier. Fine. He was reaching for the output stage when Dennis said, “Where’s she been?”
[1270 Body Text] “In a bin in Lambeth.”
[1271 Body Text] “Before that. Where’s she been plugged in? What’s she been sat next to?”
[1272 Body Text] Gideon did not know. He said so.
[1273 Body Text] “That’s your fault, then. Not hers.” Dennis leaned forward. “Hum’s not a fault, son. Hum’s a symptom. Half the hum I ever chased in forty-one years wasn’t in the set at all. It was what the set was earthed to. Customer swears blind it’s the set, you take it to the shop, sweet as a nut. Take it back, hum. Because she’s got her aerial earthed through the downpipe and the set earthed through the mains and the two earths are three volts apart because the bloke next door’s got a welder.” He sat back, pleased. “Earthed at both ends, son, and the earth’s not one place. It’s a lot of places that disagree, and current’ll go round any loop you give it.”
[1274 Body Text] “So you’d check the earth first.”
[1275 Body Text] “I’d check the earth first. Every time. Then I’d check the earth again, because I was in a hurry the first time.” Dennis nodded at the Herald. “That one’s not it, mind. That one’s the output transformer. I can hear it. I just wanted to tell you about the welder.”
[1276 Body Text] It was the output transformer. Gideon found it twenty minutes later, a shorted turn, and said so, and Dennis said, “Told you,” with enormous satisfaction, and then, after a moment, more quietly: “Don’t tell Lol I said welder. She’ll think I’m wandering. There was a welder. Mr Kaminski. Nineteen seventy-eight. He made gates.”
[1277 Body Text] “I won’t tell her,” said Gideon. And then, because it seemed to matter: “I’ll remember it, though.”
[1278 Body Text] “Good lad,” said Dennis Wren, and turned to the window to see if the bus was coming.
[1279 Heading 2] Chapter Twelve
[1280 Heading 3] Ludger Soccermann Is Filed
[1281 Body Text] The Vienna occupancy schedule was one page and it covered the whole life of the Counsel and had three entries, and no one had asked for the second until Mrs Kind did, on a Monday, with a hundred and fifty-five days left.
[1282 Body Text] It came back from the registry on Wednesday as a photostat of a photostat: a single sheet of hotel notepaper, a countersignature, and a date.
[1283 Body Text] 5 May 1906. Unaffiliated observer: L. Soccermann. Party attending upon enquiry: A. Eilstein.
[1284 Body Text] Attached: thirteen pages, in German, in a small forward-sloping hand, and a note from the registry apologising that the pages had never been translated because no request had been received in a hundred and twenty years.
[1285 Body Text] Mrs Kind read the German through, went out into the corridor and stood there for a while, and came back and said:
[1286 Body Text] “Lolly. He worked it out. All of it. In nineteen six. And it was filed.”
[1287 Body Text] Ludger Soccermann had been sixty-two in May of 1906.
[1288 Body Text] He had spent thirty years arguing that heat was the statistics of enormous numbers of invisible things, while eminent men told him his atoms were a bookkeeping fiction. He had defended himself well, in public, and it had cost him a great deal. He had held chairs in four cities and left most of them.
[1289 Body Text] He died in September of that year, at Duino, near Trieste, on holiday with his family, by his own hand, and within four years of his death the atoms were settled beyond argument by other men’s experiments.
[1290 Body Text] Lolly read the thirteen pages in Mrs Kind’s translation and went and sat in the stairwell. After a while Gideon came up from the benches and sat two steps below her, and did not ask, and when she was ready she read him the last page.
[1291 Body Text] The thirteen pages contained the answer to the question they had been failing to answer since the Concordance’s letter, and they contained it fifty-five years before anybody else wrote it down, and they were in a strong room in Vienna the entire time.
[1292 Body Text] Fainrose put the first four pages on the board on the Thursday. The department, she said, had been using the word entropy for two years without once earning it. She wrote one line and underlined it.
[1293 Source Code] S = k_B ln W
[1294 Body Text] “That is the whole of the man,” she said. “Entropy is a count of the arrangements that would look the same to you from where you stand. Nothing forbids the unlikely outcome. It is merely outnumbered. He spent his last ten years explaining to men with chairs that overwhelmingly probable is not the same word as certain, and they heard it as a confession.”
[1295 Body Text] It was pages five to eleven, about Eilstein, that made the department go quiet.
