[1296 Body Text] He had sat down opposite Albrecht Eilstein in a room off the Ringstrasse on the fifth of May 1906, as the first unaffiliated observer the room had ever held, and he had asked the question the clause required, which is whether what this party had taken was a thing anybody needed.
[1297 Body Text] And then, because he was a physicist and could not help himself, he had spent thirteen pages working out what Eilstein was.
[1298 Body Text] Mrs Kind read it out.
[1299 Body Text] The creature described to me feeds upon the alternatives which are discarded when a matter resolves. I take this to mean that it consumes precisely the difference between the many arrangements which were possible and the one which obtained — which is to say, it eats ln W.
[1300 Body Text] It then reports that it deposits some portion of what it takes into a sealed chamber in England, and regards this as prudence rather than virtue.
[1301 Body Text] I must therefore consider whether I am sitting opposite a mechanism of the sort Maxwell described in his letter: a small intelligence which sorts, and so would draw work from a single heat bath for ever. Such an engine is impossible. I am obliged either to find the error in what I have been told, or to find where the account is paid.
[1302 Body Text] The creature is not lying. I have tested it on matters where I could check.
[1303 Body Text] I therefore state where the account is paid, and I state it as a prediction so that it may be refuted by whoever reads this.
[1304 Body Text] Sorting requires knowing which to sort. Knowing requires a record. And a sorter which must sort again must first clear the record of the previous sorting, or it will fill and stop. The clearing is the cost. Not the looking; not the choosing; the forgetting. I cannot compute the amount and I believe it will prove to be a fixed quantity per distinction cleared, proportional to the temperature, and I regret that I have not the years to do it properly.
[1305 Body Text] And therefore, if the creature has taken ln W from the world for eleven thousand years and returned some of it, and is not thereby an impossible engine, there is only one place the account can have been kept.
[1306 Body Text] He does not forget anything.
[1307 Body Text] He named for me, without notes, and in order, and with the weather, the discarded alternatives of a woman in Ravenna in the winter of 1338, a woman he had never met and remembered anyway. He was not showing off. He was distressed, and could not stop, and apologised, and continued.
[1308 Body Text] That is the ledger. The entropy he removes from the world is not destroyed and is not paid by the chamber in England. It is held, in him, as a perfect and unerasable record, and it accumulates, and it cannot be discharged, because the only discharge available to a mechanism of this kind is to forget, and he is constitutionally unable to do so.
[1309 Body Text] He is not a violation of the second law. He is its collateral.
[1310 Body Text] As to the question I was required to answer: he told me that what he had taken had been needed. I do not believe him. I record that I asked him three times, and that on the third occasion he said “no”, and then asked me not to write it down, and I have written it down, because a room paid for by forty-one parties for eleven thousand years is not a room in which a man is entitled to be kind.
[1311 Body Text] L. Soccermann, 5 May 1906.
[1312 Body Text] Eilstein was summoned on the Friday, lawfully now, and he came without being asked and stood while Mrs Kind read him pages five to eleven, and did not sit down at any point.
[1313 Body Text] At the end he said, “He was correct in every particular.”
[1314 Body Text] “You lied to him,” said Lolly.
[1315 Body Text] “On the first two occasions. Then he asked a third time, and nobody had asked three times since Ravenna, which was before I was born — I have that winter only as the one who carried this before me left it to me, with the rest of what I carry — and I told him the truth, and asked him not to write it down, and he refused, and he was right to. And I want the next thing minuted before anybody is tempted to be sentimental. He died four months later and it had nothing to do with me. I will not have his death made a consequence of a conversation with me. Making the dead about oneself is the second-worst thing the living do to them.”
[1316 Body Text] Beatrix said, “What is the worst?”
[1317 Body Text] “Filing it,” said Albrecht Eilstein.
[1318 Body Text] ∗
[1319 First Paragraph] The physics of the ledger took Ramanathan and Fainrose the weekend, and it produced the thing that changed the case.
[1320 Body Text] “Soccermann’s prediction is right and it was worked out properly in nineteen sixty-one,” said Ramanathan on Monday. “It’s Landauer’s principle, and we have used it twice already in this file without noticing that we were being handed something.
[1321 Body Text] “Measurement can be free. Forgetting can’t. Clearing one bit of memory to make room for the next costs at least k_BT ln 2 of heat, and people have measured it, one bead at a time. So a demon with a perfect memory isn’t an impossible engine. It simply never pays, because the payment is forgetting. Its memory is the account.”
[1322 Body Text] Fainrose said, “All of it. Since the beginning.”
[1323 Body Text] “All of it,” said Ramanathan. “Every alternative, perfectly retained, and he cannot stop reciting them when he is distressed, which a physicist in nineteen six recorded as evidence and everybody since has recorded as a mannerism.”
[1324 Body Text] Lolly said, “Is there any relief for him at all?”
[1325 Body Text] “There is exactly one,” said Ramanathan, “and it is worse than none. He could forget. And the moment he does, the stored entropy discharges into the world, all of it, at once, at whatever temperature he happens to be standing in.” She looked up. “So the reason that creature has not permitted himself the smallest mercy since before anybody wrote anything down is not discipline and it is not vanity. He is a full vessel standing in a room with people in it.”
[1326 Body Text] ∗
[1327 First Paragraph] On the Saturday Lolly asked Eilstein to come to Larchfield with her. She did not summon him. She could have, now. She asked, in the lobby, in the old way, and he looked at her for a moment as though she had handed him something that had gone out of production.
[1328 Body Text] “Where?” he said.
[1329 Body Text] “A care home. My father’s.” She had rehearsed the next sentence on the stair and it came out anyway in the wrong order. “On the fourteenth of March he got up at three in the morning and put the kettle on for my mother, who has been dead for twelve years, and at eight he said she wasn’t there and ate his porridge. Nobody was watching. There’s no trace. You’re the only instrument I’ve got that reads what nobody was watching.”
[1330 Body Text] “I should tell you,” he said, “that I do not remember the fourteenth of March as a place. I remember it as a famine. Everywhere. I could not tell you what Pelham Road tasted like at three in the morning, because nothing tasted of anything.” He settled his hat. “But I can tell you what your father tastes like now, if he asks me in, and I gather that is the question underneath.”
[1331 Body Text] It was. She had not known until he said it.
[1332 Body Text] Jago drove them, because Jago was going that way, which was what Jago said about every way. At Larchfield he said he would keep the engine warm, and then, to Eilstein, with unusual gravity: “Mind the step. He’s proud of that step. Got the council to paint the edge.”
[1333 Body Text] Her father was at the card table by the window with the Roberts. It was a good day; she could tell from the way he looked up, at her first and then past her, at the old man in the doorway with his hat in his hands.
[1334 Body Text] “Lol. Who’s your friend?”
[1335 Body Text] “This is Mr Eilstein, Dad. He’s — he works with the department.”
[1336 Body Text] “Well, don’t stand in the door like the gas man,” said Dennis Wren. “Come in, sit down, you’re letting the heat out.”
[1337 Body Text] Eilstein came in. He did it slowly, with the care of someone crossing a floor that had been polished for him, and he sat in the low chair opposite her father and put his hat on his knee.
[1338 Body Text] “That is kind,” he said. “It is a long time since anybody asked me in that quickly.”
[1339 Body Text] “You want to get that seen to,” said her father, peering at the window behind him, where the glass held the radiator, the card table, Dennis, Lolly, and an empty chair. “Your reflection. My brother-in-law had that. Turned out to be the lighting.” He went back to the radio. “You a valve man?”
[1340 Body Text] “I knew a man at Mullard,” said Eilstein.
[1341 Body Text] “Everybody knew a man at Mullard.” Dennis was delighted. “Which man?”
[1342 Body Text] For forty minutes Lolly sat on the footstool and listened to her father and a scavenger of discarded possibilities older than writing discuss the decline of the British thermionic valve. Eilstein had known the man at Mullard, in 1931, and described the long shed at Mitcham so exactly that her father put the screwdriver down; and once, when Eilstein misremembered the type number of a rectifier, her father corrected him with such joy that Eilstein bowed in his chair.
[1343 Body Text] Then the Roberts found a station playing something with a piano, and her father said “There,” and turned it up, and closed his eyes, and Eilstein sat back and did nothing at all.
[1344 Body Text] Lolly watched him do nothing. She had never seen it before. In every room she had ever shared with him he had been, under the courtesy, a creature attending to a meal, the way a man in a restaurant attends to the kitchen door. Here he sat with his hands still on his hat, and his face had gone, for a moment, almost young.
[1345 Body Text] In the corridor afterwards he stood by the fire door and did not speak until Tomasz had gone past with the tea trolley.
[1346 Body Text] “I can tell you about the fourteenth of March,” he said, “after all. Not from memory. From the room.” He looked back towards the lounge. “Ms Wren, your father is the most expensive thing in this building.”
[1347 Body Text] “Expensive.”
[1348 Body Text] “In Mrs Kind’s sense. Fat. Every hour he is choosing. The bus or the radio. Correct the old fool about the rectifier or let it go. Small choices, and all of them real, all of them live, because somebody keeps bringing him things that are broken and a window keeps bringing him buses.” He turned his hat once in his hands. “On the fourteenth of March a door opened in your father’s room, as it opened in every room, and he got up and put his coat on and filled the kettle, and then he looked about him and found that he still had somewhere real to be. It was the chair by the window. So he sat in it. At eight he ate his porridge.”
[1349 Body Text] “And my mother.”
[1350 Body Text] “The parents’ evening. The seaside. The shop on a Friday with the fish coming at half past five. I sat across from him for forty minutes, Ms Wren, and there was nothing in any of it for me to eat. They are not branches he has discarded. He keeps them. I do not eat what is kept.” He touched the brim of his hat. “I spend my existence unable to lose anything I have taken. Your father is losing nearly everything and declining to drop the few things he loves. We have been doing the same arithmetic from opposite ends, and I find I prefer his.”
[1351 Body Text] Lolly found she could not say anything for a moment. Eilstein waited. He was good at waiting; he had been standing in lobbies since before there were doors.
[1352 Body Text] “There’s a form,” she said eventually. “Two boxes. Croydon, or upstairs with no window. Neither has the bench or the buses.”
[1353 Body Text] “Then do not write in a box,” said Albrecht Eilstein. “Write what keeps him expensive.” He held the fire door for her. “I am told that is the department’s whole policy. It seemed to me, in May, a very good one. It would be a pity to apply it to everyone in the country except your father.”
[1354 Body Text] On the way back Lolly opened the notebook on her knee and wrote, under Person’s own views: Expensive. Keep him expensive.
[1355 Body Text] ∗
[1356 First Paragraph] The way out came on the Tuesday, at seven in the morning, from Hendrik van Casteel, in a telephone box in Cumbria. “If you want the Fourth Concordance kept among the nine,” he said, “bring their delegate and their archive to my mine by Thursday noon. The Office’s motion is listed for Friday. I will need four hours and a decision that is not mine to make.”
[1357 Body Text] So on the Thursday Lolly, Beatrix, Fainrose and Miss Pike went three hundred and forty metres down a potash level west of Whitehaven in a cage that smelled of wet rope, with the Concordance’s delegate between them. Her name was Ysolde Marr. She was tall and grey, and she carried the archive on her knees: a squat brass cylinder the size of a biscuit tin, holding the Concordance’s half of four hundred years of correlations. The other half had been a star.
[1358 Body Text] Van Casteel had not shaved. His bench stood in a whitewashed gallery behind two doors: a torsion balance under a bell jar, a reference cell, a heat bath the size of a coffin, and a camera on a boom. Somewhere along the level a dewatering pump thumped forty minutes on and twenty off, and van Casteel had timed his day to its silences; the thump came up through the rock and into the fibre.
[1359 Body Text] “You keep asking whether an unreadable archive is a record or a rumour,” he said. “That is a librarian’s question. Mine is what you can get out of it.” He put his hands flat on the bench. “If my memory is correlated with a system, I can draw work from a single heat bath in proportion to what I know. Not a joule more. That has been done with a bead and a camera. If that cylinder holds four hundred real years, there is work in it. If it is a fabrication, marks correlated with nothing, there is none. Not a little less. None. You cannot fake mutual information. It is not a property of the marks. It is a relation, and an invented relation has no engine in it.”
[1360 Body Text] Beatrix said, slowly, “You can certify the archive without reading it.”
[1361 Body Text] “I can tell you how much is in it and that it is real, and I cannot learn one word of what it says, and I regard that as a feature.” Then he turned to Ysolde Marr, and his voice changed. “But I cannot weigh it for nothing, madam. Whatever work I draw out, I draw out of the correlation. The part I weigh is spent. It will still be there, and it will hold nothing. To prove the four hundred years I must burn one of them, and I need a whole year, or the balance will not see it.”
[1362 Body Text] The pump stopped, and the gallery went still.
[1363 Body Text] “Which year?” said Ysolde Marr.
[1364 Body Text] “Your choice,” said van Casteel. “I am told that is the policy now.”
[1365 Body Text] She stood with her hands on the cylinder for some time.
[1366 Body Text] “The two hundred and twelfth,” the delegate said at last. “It was a plague year. The keepers who made it were ill, and they kept it better than any year before or after, because they believed nobody would come after them to keep the next.” She looked at Beatrix. “You will think I should give you a bad year. Anybody can spare a bad year. If we are to show that we kept them, let it cost us the one we are proudest of.”
[1367 Body Text] Beatrix wrote it down, the number and the reason, in her own hand. It was the only part of that afternoon she would not let anyone else minute.
[1368 Body Text] The first run began in the pump’s silence and failed when it came back on and the floor began, faintly, to shiver, and van Casteel swore in Dutch and reset the balance and waited another forty minutes with his arms folded. The second run went cleanly. The fibre turned through a fraction of a degree, and the number came up on the screen in joules with a great many zeros in front of it and a figure at the end, and the figure was not zero.
[1369 Body Text] Then van Casteel ran the control: a second cylinder he had filled on Monday with marks generated from the serial numbers of a Cumbrian bus timetable, four hundred years of perfect-looking records correlated with nothing. The fibre did not move. The screen said what a forged archive says, which is nothing at all.
[1370 Body Text] Fainrose had chalk in her pocket. She always did. She wrote on the whitewashed wall, beside the bench: Paragraph 11 does not require readable copies. It requires copies whose correlation with the certified subject can be demonstrated in work.
[1371 Body Text] “And no fabricated archive can satisfy it at any price,” she said, “because—”
[1372 Body Text] “Because a forger would have to actually correlate his archive with an actual four hundred years,” said Miss Pike, “which isn’t forgery. That’s just doing it.”
[1373 Body Text] Fainrose wrote it on the wall exactly as Miss Pike had said it, and initialled it A.P., and it is still there, three hundred and forty metres under Cumbria, where the dewatering pump goes on thumping forty minutes on and twenty off for nobody.
[1374 Body Text] In the cage going up, Lolly asked Ysolde Marr, since somebody ought to, whether the Concordance would be told which year had gone.
[1375 Body Text] “They will be told,” said the delegate. “I shall tell them myself. It is three hundred and ninety-nine years now, and every one of them is admissible, and I do not yet know how I feel about the price.”
[1376 Body Text] ∗
[1377 First Paragraph] Lolly went to Elm Grove on the Friday evening, back from Cumbria with whitewash on her sleeve, because Mrs Chain had told Miss Pike that she had made a cake and could not be expected to eat it alone.
[1378 Body Text] The photograph was on the wall in the front room: two women on a windy promenade, one laughing at something out of the frame with her whole face, the other trying not to and failing.
[1379 Body Text] “Margaret was laughing at a man’s hat,” said Mrs Chain, putting the tea down. “I have never known what was wrong with it. She said if I couldn’t see it she wasn’t going to spoil it.” She cut the cake. It was a Victoria sponge, slightly sunk in the middle, which she pointed out before Lolly could fail to. “The oven runs hot. I have complained to the oven.”
[1380 Body Text] Then she said, “Your Mr Eilstein. Miss Pike tells me he wept over a letter from a dead physicist.”
[1381 Body Text] Lolly told her: the letter, the third time of asking, and what Eilstein had said about the two worst things the living do to the dead. When she reached filing it, Mrs Chain put her cup down.
[1382 Body Text] “Yes,” she said. “He is quite right.” She looked at the photograph. “When Margaret went, the Ministry sent a letter. It said her case had been administratively concluded. I had it on the mantelpiece for a week and then I put it in the fire, and I am a woman who regrets the fire on principle.” She was quiet for a moment. “Your green book is different. When you wrote her name down, that was not filing. Filing is when you write somebody down so that you can stop thinking about them. You wrote her down so that somebody would have to keep on.”
[1383 Body Text] “And your father,” said Mrs Chain. “That form of theirs. It wants to file him. Two boxes and a signature and concluded.” She picked up her cup again. “Do not let them. That is all. You are very clever and you have a department and I have an umbrella, and between us I think we can be a great deal of trouble.”
[1384 Body Text] At the door, with her coat on, Lolly looked back at the photograph once more. Margaret was still laughing at the hat.
[1385 Body Text] “I’d like to know what was wrong with it,” she said.
[1386 Body Text] “So would I,” said Mrs Chain. “That is rather the point of her.” And she shut the door, not unkindly, on the cold.
[1387 Body Text] ∗
[1388 First Paragraph] Lolly wrote, that night, and it took her longer than usual:
[1389 Body Text] We filed Soccermann for a hundred and twenty years because nobody submitted a request. I have put in a request for a translation of every document in that strong room. There are four hundred and nine.
[1390 Body Text] 144 days.
[1391 Heading 2] Chapter Thirteen
[1392 Heading 3] Mr Drill Is Not Quotable
[1393 First Paragraph] The letter of instruction came from the nine, jointly, which had never happened, and it was one sentence long.
[1394 Body Text] The nine require that the department’s finding on the fourteen be independently reviewed before it is relied upon in the treaty audit, and have appointed Mr N. Drill.
[1395 Body Text] Beatrix read it and said, “Who is N. Drill?”
[1396 Body Text] Mrs Kind, who by then had a card index, said: “He built the Programme’s benches. All of them, from nineteen fifty-eight to nineteen ninety-four.”
[1397 Body Text] Nelson Drill was eighty-eight and arrived by taxi with a driver who came in to help him with the door and then stayed seven minutes to hear the end of something.
[1398 Body Text] He had been a goalkeeper for a provincial Danish club, briefly and without distinction, and then, for thirty-six years, the man who designed test benches for people who needed to measure something no one had measured before. He had never published a paper. He had a way of speaking that Lolly was entirely unprepared for.
[1399 Body Text] “I should say at once,” he said, “or rather — no. Let me put that differently, because ‘at once’ suggests I have a position and I do not have a position, I have an objection, which is a smaller thing and I should not inflate it. Or — well. It is not smaller. It is differently shaped. May I begin again?”
[1400 Body Text] “Please,” said Fainrose.
[1401 Body Text] “I have read your finding on the fourteen six times and I believe it is invalid, and I want to be careful, because I do not believe it is wrong.” He sat down slowly. “Those are not the same, and the difference is all I have come to say. You may have reached a correct conclusion by a route that is not permitted, and in my trade that is worse than an error, because an error can be found.”
[1402 Body Text] It took the whole morning. He would reach the end of a proposition, say that it was too strong, and take a piece back. By eleven Lolly had stopped trying to take him down verbatim, and by half past twelve Miss Pike had stopped writing and started drawing benches, which turned out to be the correct response.
[1403 Body Text] “Your finding,” said Drill, “rests on two observations, and I am about to be unfair to your Mr Eilstein, so I should like to be unfair accurately.
[1404 Body Text] “Observation one. On the fourteenth of March, fourteen pending states resolved outside their permitted options. Miss Pike made that observation on a bench: the register, the traces, four instruments. A specified arrangement, with a defined coupling, producing marks.
[1405 Body Text] “Observation two. On the fourteenth of March there was no discarded amplitude — nothing to eat. That observation was made by a single organism whose coupling cannot be specified, cannot be repeated, produces no marks, and is available to precisely one observer in the galaxy.
[1406 Body Text] “Your finding then says: since observation two shows no waste, and waste is the signature of selection, the outcomes in observation one were determined rather than selected. That step, Dr Fainrose, combines results from two mutually exclusive arrangements as though they were simultaneous facts about the same systems. A very great man made that error in nineteen thirty-five, and it is the only error in the subject that never announces itself.”
[1407 Body Text] Eilstein, who had been summoned and who had been sitting at the end of the table, said: “Say the nineteen thirty-five error, please. Properly. I have heard it described nine ways and I have never once heard it from somebody who built the apparatus.”
[1408 Body Text] Drill did it with two pencils and a matchbox. “Two particles, correlated, flying apart. Measure where the near one is, or how fast it goes, and you learn that about the far one. The paper of nineteen thirty-five says both facts were there all along. The reply was: you may build this bench, or that bench.” He set the matchbox between the pencils. “Not both. That is complementarity as it was meant, and it is a rule about benches, not about black holes. And now I should like some soup, because I am eighty-eight.”
[1409 Body Text] Eilstein waited until the plates had gone, and then said:
[1410 Body Text] “Mr Drill. I am the man who made the argument, and I will not pretend to be neutral. You say I combined two benches. But I am not claiming to have measured anything. I am reporting an absence of consumption. I ate nothing. That is a claim about whether a transaction occurred, and I am the counterparty.”
[1411 Body Text] “Yes,” said Drill. “Yes. That is — that is the good objection, and I want to give you the answer that I think is true rather than the one that wins.
[1412 Body Text] “Your feeding is a coupling. It is not passive. You consume the difference between what could have happened and what did, so your apparatus, whatever it is, is coupled to the branch structure of the resolution. And an arrangement coupled to branch structure is incompatible with one that records definite outcomes in a register, for the same reason a which-path detector is incompatible with an interference pattern. If you can taste the branches, the branches are not resolved. If the register has an entry, the branches are gone. On the fourteenth of March there were two benches, and you have been treating them as one room.”
[1413 Body Text] Eilstein sat back.
[1414 Body Text] “Then what am I?” he said.
[1415 Body Text] “You are an instrument that has never been characterised,” said Nelson Drill, “and I have spent thirty-six years being paid to tell people that an uncharacterised instrument is not evidence, it is a lead.”
[1416 Body Text] The theorem came after soup, and Drill did it standing, badly. In nineteen sixty-seven, he said, two people had proved that you cannot hand every quantity in a system a definite value in advance; the value depends on what else you chose to measure alongside it. “For sixty years I was told it was mysticism, and now it is sold as a resource for quantum computers, which I find funny. It does not kill determinism. It kills your finding. Fixed is not something a system carries about with it. It belongs to a system and an arrangement together, and on the fourteenth of March you had two.”
[1417 Body Text] Fainrose put it on the board on the Wednesday evening and did not soften it.
[1418 Body Text] “The finding is invalid as reasoned,” she said. “The nine were right to send him. We inferred determination from an absence measured on an uncharacterised instrument, and we published it in a Statement of Coherence.”
[1419 Body Text] Miss Pike said, “Is the conclusion wrong?”
[1420 Body Text] “That’s the thing.” Fainrose turned round. “Mr Drill attacks the route, not the drainage account. And when I redo it his way, the finding gets sharper. There is no fact about which options a pending state ‘has’ apart from the arrangement that defines them. Our register was the arrangement. The permitted options were not two doors that happened to exist in the world; they were the two doors our bench was built to detect.
[1421 Body Text] “So we did not merely fail to notice that Ellen Prosper’s options had closed. We specified what counted as an option for her, we maintained the specification monthly for sixteen months, and while our bench was the only bench in the room, the question of what else was available to her had no answer.
[1422 Body Text] “That is not a failure of monitoring. That is a much older offence and Mr Drill has just handed the plaintiffs a better cause of action than the one they have.”
[1423 Body Text] Beatrix Sloan, who had been silent for two hours, said: “Yes. And I have to advise the nine of it by Friday, and I am going to.”
[1424 Body Text] Friday came, and Beatrix advised the nine, and it went extremely well, except once, and Lolly was in the room for it.
[1425 Body Text] The delegate for Sub-Compact Ferrous, a small, cold signatory that had contributed two junior analysts and a great deal of ice-core data, asked to be heard before the session closed, and was, because the rules Beatrix had drafted still said anyone could ask.
[1426 Body Text] “I have read the membership list,” she said. Her name in the transcript was Fenn Adair, and she had come the long way, on a delegation budget that would not survive the year. “Nine names. I am not one of them. I want it minuted that I understood why before I was told, and that understanding it did not make it correct.”
[1427 Body Text] Beatrix did not try to make it easier for her.
[1428 Body Text] “You are right that Ferrous is not on the list,” she said. “The list was chosen for trust: continuity of contribution, verifiable stake, nothing to do with size or wealth. On every version of that test I could construct, Ferrous came twelfth.”
[1429 Body Text] “Twelfth of forty-one.”
[1430 Body Text] “Yes.”
[1431 Body Text] “Then I was close.”
[1432 Body Text] “You were close,” said Beatrix, “which is worse than being nowhere near it, and I have no remedy to offer you. I built it anyway, because the alternative was building nothing, and I would build it the same way again, and none of that makes you wrong.”
[1433 Body Text] Fenn Adair did not look away.
[1434 Body Text] “I did not come to be told I was wrong,” she said. “I came to be told the truth, which you have now done, twice, and I find I would rather have it from you than a kinder answer from somebody else. Minute it as I asked.”
[1435 Body Text] It was minuted as she asked. Lolly gave it a heading of its own in the green notebook, because it was the only entry in the volume where the department had done something correctly and a person was still, rightly, unhappy about it.
[1436 Body Text] ∗
[1437 First Paragraph] Stephanie Fainrose finished the letter to Mrs Chain at half past ten that night, at the Institute, four months after she had started it.
[1438 Body Text] She had begun it in May, on the evening she issued herself a written warning, and had got as far as Dear Mrs Chain, I was wrong, and then stopped, because it was not yet true in enough detail. She did not like to apologise to an uncertainty of more than five per cent.
[1439 Body Text] It was true in enough detail now. The Ferrous delegate had told Beatrix that afternoon that she would rather have the truth than kindness, and Fainrose had recognised the tone as Mrs Chain’s.
[1440 Body Text] Dear Mrs Chain,
[1441 Body Text] I am writing to tell you that I was wrong, and to tell you exactly how, because I think you would rather have that than a general expression of regret.
[1442 Body Text] In the spring before last I told you that the register of pending states would protect people like your sister from being decided for. I believed it. I designed it. It did protect them, from the thing I was worried about. It did not protect them from us. We kept fourteen people in states whose options no longer existed, and called it stability, and published it monthly, and somebody read our publication and used it. I did not check whether the floors were still there. That was my job and I did not do it.
[1443 Body Text] You asked, in the spring, to be sent the page if the word came back. I want you to know that I have not forgotten, and that the page is not ready, and that I will not let anybody send you a measurement instead. I am not good at many things that are not arithmetic. I am good at not rounding.
[1444 Body Text] Yours sincerely,
[1445 Body Text] S. Fainrose
[1446 Body Text] She read it over. It was four lines too long, and she let it be four lines too long, which was, for her, a kind of tenderness.
[1447 Body Text] Then she sealed it, and stamped it, and walked out into the warm September dark to the postbox on the corner, because a letter you mean to post in the morning is a letter you have decided not to post.
[1448 Body Text] ∗
[1449 First Paragraph] The rescue was Ramanathan’s, on the Monday, when Drill came back to hear the reply, and it was expensive.
[1450 Body Text] “If the objection is that we combined two benches,” she said, “then the answer is not to argue. It is to build one bench. A single arrangement in which the branch-structure coupling and the outcome register are parts of the same characterised instrument, with a specified interaction, calibrated, repeatable, with the error budget written down.”
[1451 Body Text] “You cannot characterise him,” said Miss Pike.
[1452 Body Text] “I can characterise the coupling without understanding the mechanism. I do it every week with detectors I did not design.” Ramanathan looked at Eilstein, and then away, and then back. “Mr Eilstein. To make that finding admissible, you would have to be incorporated, formally, as departmental apparatus. Your feeding would be logged, gated and referenced to the register, and you would be a schedule to the instrument list.”
[1453 Body Text] Eilstein said, “I have been many things.”
[1454 Body Text] “There is a consequence,” said Beatrix, and she was already writing. “If he is departmental apparatus, then what he holds is departmental. Eleven thousand years of unerasable stored entropy, uncleared, undischarged, on our instrument list.”
[1455 Body Text] Fainrose said, “The Crown inherits the ledger.”
[1456 Body Text] “The Crown inherits the ledger,” said Beatrix, “and every discarded branch in it, and the debt cannot be discharged, because discharge means forgetting and forgetting means release into whatever room he is standing in. I would be advising the department to acquire, as a fixed asset, the largest undisclosed liability in the history of the state.”
[1457 Body Text] There was quite a long silence.
[1458 Body Text] Albrecht Eilstein said, “May I say something in my own matter?”
[1459 Body Text] “You may.”
[1460 Body Text] “Do it,” he said. “Not because it is prudent. It is the least prudent thing anybody has proposed since the case began, and Ms Sloan is entirely right about the liability.” He had his hands on the table. “Do it because for longer than you have had instruments I have been an instrument no one characterised, and every wrong I have done has been possible because of that, and Mr Drill was right about what that makes me. I should like to stop being a lead.”
[1461 Body Text] Drill had listened to all of it without revising a word, which Miss Pike said afterwards was a first. When it was over he got up, slowly, for his taxi.
[1462 Body Text] Lolly said, “Mr Drill, could I have your review in writing?”
[1463 Body Text] And Nelson Drill said, from the door, with what Lolly was almost sure was satisfaction:
[1464 Body Text] “You could not. I have never in my life said a sentence twice the same way and I have been assured by two governments that it makes me useless.” He squared his hat. “Though I have noticed, Ms Wren, since you have a President who does his work with true sentences cut loose from the men who said them, that being impossible to quote is not the disadvantage everyone told me it was.”
[1465 Body Text] Lolly wrote it down before she slept:
[1466 Body Text] Drill: impossible to quote. It is the only defence against our President that anybody has found.
[1467 Body Text] 134 days.
[1468 Body Text] ∗
[1469 First Paragraph] The nine sent no letter this time. On the second Monday in October they sent a man, unannounced, who gave his name to the desk as “Arakawa, retired,” and who had spent 1957, at the age of twenty-two, growing the crystals for the first tunnel diodes in a laboratory in Tokyo.
[1470 Body Text] He was ninety-one. He sat down, declined tea, and said, before anyone had asked him anything:
[1471 Body Text] “You are troubled by the fourteen because they went where the barrier said they could not go. I built a career on the fact that this is not troubling. It is as ordinary as a Tuesday.”
[1472 Body Text] “I grew crystals,” he said, when Fainrose asked. “For Leo Esaki, at the company you know as Sony. Germanium, doped so heavily that the junction — the barrier, if you like your word — was a few nanometres thick. We turned the voltage up and the current rose, as it should. Then it fell. More push, less current. Every sensible engineer in Tokyo said the meter was broken. Esaki said the electrons were not climbing the barrier at all. They were going through it. Tunnelling. He got the Nobel Prize sixteen years later, and I got a very good reference.”
[1473 Body Text] Mrs Kind said, “That is the word this department has been arguing about since March.”
[1474 Body Text] “I know,” said Arakawa. “It is why I am here. Everybody in this building has spent the year being astonished, and I am ninety-one and have run out of patience with astonishment.”
[1475 Body Text] He took a pencil and drew a rectangle, and a wavy line running into it and, faintly, out the other side.
[1476 Body Text] “Here is what you keep getting backwards,” he said. “You ask how the fourteen got through when they had no energy to get through, as though the barrier were an obstacle and the particle a traveller who cheated. The particle is not a traveller. It is a wave. Inside a barrier its amplitude thins, fast but not to nothing, and if the barrier is thin there is still some on the far side. Nobody borrowed energy and paid it back. The wave was on both sides before you asked which side it was on.
[1477 Body Text] “A barrier is not a wall. It is a region where the classical description says the amplitude ought to be zero, and it is not. A junction in a crystal, a nucleus deciding whether to decay, or” — he looked at Fainrose — “a permitted option in a register, if your register describes an amplitude at all.”
[1478 Body Text] Mrs Kind, who had been quiet, said: “Dr Arakawa. May I ask a travel agent’s question.”
[1479 Body Text] “You built a business on holidays,” said Arakawa, without looking up. “Mr Drill told me. Ask.”
[1480 Body Text] “When somebody books a package to a country they can’t legally enter, I don’t say they travelled there illegally. I say the booking never described a real trip.” She leaned forward. “Is that what you mean? That the ‘barrier’ the paperwork describes was never a real barrier to begin with?”
[1481 Body Text] “That,” said Arakawa, and for the first time something happened to his face that might have been pleasure, “is better than anything I heard in seventy years of lecture halls. Yes. A tunnelling event is not evidence that something impossible happened. It is evidence that your description of what was possible was wrong.”
[1482 Body Text] He put the pencil down. “I have told you what a barrier is. What you do with fourteen files that crossed one is not a semiconductor’s business.” He stood, declining help. “Though I will say, since your Mr Drill is not here to stop me — if your instrument was never characterised, you do not yet know whether you are describing a barrier at all. You may simply be describing a place where you stopped looking.” He left before tea could be offered a second time.
[1483 Body Text] “Drill told us our route was invalid,” said Fainrose, into the quiet. “Arakawa has told us the crossing was never the anomaly. Our confidence was.”
[1484 Body Text] Beatrix had already opened a new folder. “Then I need to know, before Friday, whether the ‘permitted options’ in that register describe a real forbidden amplitude, or whether we built a diode and called the thing on the far side a crime.”
[1485 Body Text] ∗
[1486 First Paragraph] Beatrix Sloan opened the second folder at twenty past ten that night, at her kitchen table, after she had finished the first.
[1487 Body Text] She did not, as a rule, take work home that was not hers. This folder had a label in her own hand that said WREN, D. and, under that, in smaller letters, because she was a precise woman even with herself, not instructed.
[1488 Body Text] She had seen the form on Lolly’s desk on Friday: BI-2, two boxes, and under Person’s own views the words Expensive. Keep him expensive, underlined harder than Lolly usually underlined. She had read it upside down while walking past, which was a skill, and had not mentioned it, which was a discipline.
[1489 Body Text] She wrote:
[1490 Body Text] 1. The decision is one of accommodation, to be made in Mr Wren’s best interests under section 4 of the Mental Capacity Act 2005, he having been assessed as lacking capacity to make it himself.
[1491 Body Text] 2. The decision-maker must, so far as reasonably practicable, permit and encourage Mr Wren to participate (s.4(4)), and must consider his past and present wishes and feelings (s.4(6)(a)). These are recorded overleaf in his own words. They were taken down on a Tuesday in July by a witness of good character, and read back to him, and he corrected one of them. I attach the correction.
[1492 Body Text] 3. The decision-maker must take into account the views of anyone engaged in caring for him or interested in his welfare (s.4(7)(b)). There are at least two such persons who are not family and who are not presently on the consultation list. I name them.
[1493 Body Text] 4. Before the decision is made, regard must be had to whether its purpose can be as effectively achieved in a way that is less restrictive of his rights and freedom of action (s.1(6)). The form offers two options. Neither is the least restrictive. A third exists. I describe it.
[1494 Body Text] She stopped there and read it back, and found that it was good, and found that she was angry, which was unusual enough that she put the pen down to examine it.
[1495 Body Text] She was not angry with Ms Breen, who was overworked and decent, or with Larchfield, closing a floor because a floor cost money. She was angry with the boxes themselves, with their small printed confidence, with the fact that a man who had spent forty-one years telling customers what was really wrong with the set was about to be offered two answers neither of which contained the fault.
[1496 Body Text] It was the register again: a list of permitted options drawn up by somebody who had never met the person, and a department that would, unless somebody objected in writing, describe the misfit as a problem with the person.
[1497 Body Text] She wrote 5. and then sat for a while.
[1498 Body Text] Her own father had been a judge. He had died at home in Surrey, in his own bed, in a room with a window on the garden, attended by a nurse who called him Your Honour because he liked it, and nobody had ever offered him a form. Then she wrote:
[1499 Body Text] 5. The department’s interest in this matter is nil. Mine is personal and is declared. I act pro bono and without instruction, and I shall withdraw this letter without complaint if Ms Wren asks me to.
[1500 Body Text] In the morning she put it on Lolly’s desk, under the form, and went to her own office and closed the door, and did not open it again until Lolly knocked.
[1501 Body Text] “You’ve cited the Act at them,” said Lolly. She had the letter in her hand and her face was doing something complicated.
[1502 Body Text] “I have drawn the decision-maker’s attention to provisions of which she is no doubt already aware,” said Beatrix. “That is a different thing. It is more polite, and much harder to ignore.” She looked at the letter, not at Lolly, which was a courtesy. “You may tear it up. I meant that.”
[1503 Body Text] “I’m not going to tear it up.”
[1504 Body Text] “Then I shall need to know who the two persons are. I have guessed. I would like to be told.”
[1505 Body Text] “Gideon,” said Lolly. “And Mrs Chain.”
[1506 Body Text] Beatrix wrote both names into paragraph 3, in ink, and initialled the change.
[1507 Body Text] “Good,” she said. “A man who repairs things and a woman who will not pass notes. If I were a panel I should be terrified.”
[1508 Body Text] Lolly wrote, that night, with Beatrix’s letter folded into the back of the notebook:
[1509 Body Text] 119 days. Thirty-one cores still advancing, Ramanathan says. I have not asked her what that means for the count, because I think I know.
[1510 Heading 2] Chapter Fourteen
[1511 Heading 3] Lieutenant Schwarzschild Sends His Regrets
[1512 First Paragraph] The letter did not come from the nine, or from the Office, or from any living correspondent, and that was the first thing wrong with it.
[1513 Body Text] It came from the registry’s oldest holding, an envelope catalogued in 1917 and never opened because the docket said hold pending confirmation of addressee’s survival, and nobody, in over a century, had thought to check whether the confirmation had ever arrived. Mrs Kind found it while looking for something else entirely, read the covering slip, and brought it to Fainrose without a word.
[1514 Body Text] From: Lt. K. Schwarzschild, Russian Front. To: whichever office of His Majesty’s Government concerns itself with the shape of things. Enclosed: a solution, obtained between duties, to Herr Einstein’s field equations, for the case of a single point mass. I do not expect to require it returned.
[1515 Body Text] He was right. He did not require it returned. He died of a rare autoimmune disease contracted at the front a few months after posting it, and the solution reached Einstein, who was startled that anyone had found an exact answer to his own approximate one so quickly, and who read it to the Prussian Academy on the absent man’s behalf.
[1516 Body Text] What had never reached anyone, until Mrs Kind opened it, was the second half of the letter, which Karl Schwarzschild had thought too speculative for Einstein and had sent instead to the office that was the Ministry’s direct ancestor.
[1517 Body Text] Fainrose read it aloud to the department on the Tuesday morning, and her voice did something to the second page that Lolly had never heard it do before.
[1518 Body Text] My solution has a curious feature which I set aside from my note to Einstein because it did not seem a matter for a paper written from a trench, though I now believe it a matter for exactly that.
[1519 Body Text] At the radius where I set my metric’s coefficient to zero — the radius, I am told since, which some of my colleagues are already calling by my name, though I would rather they did not — the mathematics does not stop. It changes character. Time and space exchange their roles inside that radius, and the equations, extended, describe not a single region collapsing inward, but two regions, joined at a throat, neither of which is the other, connected by a passage which the mathematics permits and which no matter travelling forward in time may ever cross back through.
[1520 Body Text] I do not know what is at the other end. I strongly suspect nothing is, in the sense of there being no matter that put it there; I suspect it is a property of the equations themselves, the way a corridor is a property of a building’s plan before anyone walks down it. But I have solved the equations for a single point mass, alone in an otherwise empty universe, and the geometry I obtain is not one region with a dense centre. It is two.
[1521 Body Text] I mention this to your office rather than to the Berlin physicists for the following reason. If two regions of an otherwise unconnected universe can be joined by a throat that the equations permit but which no traveller may ever use twice, then the question of what lies “outside” a thing is a question your office presumably already has cause to ask of stranger objects than mine. I am a lieutenant of artillery and I do not expect to see the end of this war. I should like this observation to survive me, in case it is of use to whoever eventually has to explain how two things came to be joined without either of them having travelled to reach the other.
[1522 Body Text] K.S., somewhere on the Eastern Front, 1916.
[1523 Body Text] Ramanathan had turned the Cumbrian plot face down. “That is not merely tunnelling,” she said. “That is the other thing. That is the thing Eilstein’s horizon has been doing to my instruments since March and I called it noise.”
[1524 Body Text] “Go on, then,” said Fainrose.
[1525 Body Text] “His solution — the real Schwarzschild solution, maximally extended, which no one worked out fully until nineteen sixty — does not describe one hole. Extended honestly, the maths gives you two asymptotically flat regions, joined at a throat, at a single instant, with the throat pinching shut faster than anything could cross it. A bridge between two elsewheres that exists for less time than it would take to use.
[1526 Body Text] “And your fourteen went missing on the fourteenth of March. Not destroyed — the March account was careful about that, and the entropy accounting gave us a place for what’s consumed and what’s stored. But there was a residue nobody could source, in my own words from March, that behaved like it belonged to two files at once.”
[1527 Body Text] Miss Pike said, slowly, “Are you saying the fourteen went through a wormhole?”
[1528 Body Text] “I am saying,” said Ramanathan, “that a lieutenant of artillery worked out, in a trench, in 1916, using nothing but Einstein’s equations and appalling personal circumstances, that the exact geometry of a mass alone in empty space comes with a second region attached to it in the mathematics, whether or not anyone can reach it, and Albrecht Eilstein has inherited a horizon with a hundred centuries behind it.”
[1529 Body Text] Eilstein, summoned again, arrived within the hour, and for once did not sit.
[1530 Body Text] “I have never used it,” he said. “I want that said before anyone asks the question shaped like an accusation. I did not know it was there in the sense your lieutenant means. I knew there was a place behind me I do not go, and I assumed, as anyone assumes of the inside of their own throat, that it was simply more of me — darkness, mine, private, unremarkable. Nobody had told me it was, structurally, a second building.”
[1531 Body Text] Fainrose had the Cumbrian records out within the hour, and it was van Casteel who put the shape of it together. He had spent nineteen years thinking about what could be measured across a gap nothing should cross.
[1532 Body Text] “A throat that pinches shut before anything can cross it is not useful to anyone,” he said. “That is the classical Schwarzschild wormhole and it has always been a curiosity rather than a route — not traversable even in principle. Every physicist since nineteen sixty has said the same thing: lovely mathematics, no application, don’t build anything on it.
[1533 Body Text] “But there is a way to hold a throat open. You need something threading it with negative energy density — a substance that violates the ordinary energy conditions, that gravitates the wrong way, propping the corridor rather than letting it collapse.
[1534 Body Text] “And this department’s own vacuum accounting identified precisely such a substance, and none of you noticed, because you filed it under vacuum energy and moved on.” He looked at Fainrose. “Casimir energy is negative. I have measured it directly with plates less than a thousandth of a millimetre apart for nineteen years. It is small. It is laughably small, by the standard of what you would need to prop open a throat between galaxies. But it is not zero, and it is not hypothetical, and it is the one substance in this department’s files that has the correct sign.”
[1535 Body Text] Nobody moved.
[1536 Body Text] “You are not proposing we built one,” said Beatrix carefully.
[1537 Body Text] “I am proposing,” said van Casteel, “that something has been holding a throat open in the geometry behind Mr Eilstein using the same resource this department has spent the year measuring in a Cumbrian mineshaft, and that no one thought to ask what a horizon older than the Bronze Age, fed continuously, and never once permitted to evaporate, might have had time to become on the inside.”
[1538 Body Text] The best sentence of the afternoon, when it came, was not a physicist’s, and Lolly was careful to record that it was Eilstein’s, because everybody afterwards tried to give it to Ramanathan.
[1539 Body Text] “May I say the thing your lieutenant could not finish,” Eilstein said, “because he ran out of paper, or ran out of life, and either way it is the same interruption.”
[1540 Body Text] “Please,” said Fainrose.
[1541 Body Text] “He said he did not know what was at the other end, and suspected it was nothing — a property of the equations, like a corridor in the plan of a building nobody has walked into yet. He was careful. He was a soldier writing to a government office and he did not want to be thought fanciful.
[1542 Body Text] “I have had a very long time to not be careful, so I will say what he would not. A corridor that exists in the plan before anyone walks down it is not nothing. Two regions are joined, in the mathematics, the instant the mass exists, whether or not a single particle ever crosses. The bridge is not built when it is used. It is built when the equations are satisfied. Using it is a separate and much harder question, and this department, which has spent the case asking whether things are permitted, should notice that your lieutenant answered a bigger question first.
[1543 Body Text] “Two places do not need a history of travel between them to be connected. They need only the same equation satisfied at both ends. What I inherited has carried the far end of such a bridge since before the pyramids, and I did not know it, because I had never been introduced to a man clever enough, and brave enough, and short-lived enough, to tell me what I was standing on.”
[1544 Body Text] For a while no one spoke. Then Barbarian, from the corner: “He posted that letter and went back to his guns.”
[1545 Body Text] “He went back to his guns,” said Fainrose, “and he was dead within the year, and the Prussian Academy heard his first solution read aloud by a man too astonished to wait for the war to end, and his second solution sat in a drawer in this building addressed to nobody in particular for more than a century because the docket said pending confirmation of survival and nobody checked.”
[1546 Body Text] Mrs Kind said, and it was the travel agent in her that could not let it go, “He would have laughed. A booking held open since 1916, pending confirmation of a passenger who was never coming back to collect it.”
[1547 Body Text] “He would not have laughed,” said Eilstein. “I never met him. I wish I had. I would have told him what is actually behind me, and let him finish the sentence he was too disciplined to finish in a letter he expected to be his last.”
[1548 Body Text] What it meant for the case, Beatrix laid out on the Wednesday, grim about it.
[1549 Body Text] “If van Casteel is right that something has propped Eilstein’s throat open with a Casimir-sign resource, then the region behind him is not a mathematical curiosity. It is a place. Unaudited, unvisited, and — if the fourteen went somewhere on the fourteenth of March that this department could not source — possibly not empty.”
[1550 Body Text] “You think the fourteen are through it,” said Miss Pike.
[1551 Body Text] “I think,” said Beatrix, “that we have spent the case asking whether fourteen files were destroyed, stored, or forgiven, and Lieutenant Schwarzschild has just handed us a fourth category, which is that they were posted, to an address no one has ever checked, because until this morning nobody knew there was another end.”
[1552 Body Text] Ramanathan was already reaching for the Cumbrian instruments. “Then I need to know if a throat held open by a Casimir-sign source is traversable enough for a resolved amplitude to pass through it, or only large enough for the mathematics to be technically true and practically useless.”
[1553 Body Text] “That,” said van Casteel, standing, “is a bench I can build.”
[1554 Body Text] Gideon had been at the back with his arms folded, which was what he did when the thing in the room was not yet a mechanism. He unfolded them.
[1555 Body Text] “I’ll go down the shaft with him,” he said. “The cable is the part that lies, and he’ll be thinking about the plates.”
[1556 Body Text] Lolly looked at him. He had already decided. That was how he stayed beside her. He arrived at the job while she was still counting what it would cost, and he began.
[1557 Body Text] ∗
[1558 First Paragraph] Delia Price came in on the Friday, five months after Inverness, and brought Mrs Kind a tin of shortbread, which was not done, and Mrs Kind accepted it, which was not done either.
[1559 Body Text] “It was lovely,” said Mrs Price, in the doorway of Kind & Farrow, with her coat buttoned to the neck. “Sad. But lovely. My niece’s husband did a reading and got the name of the hymn wrong and everybody laughed, and Joan would have laughed. I’m glad I went. I’m glad it was Inverness.” She hesitated. “I don’t know what you did. With the other one. The coach.”
[1560 Body Text] “I booked you on a different coach,” said Mrs Kind. “That is all a travel agent ever does.”
[1561 Body Text] Mrs Price looked at her as if she knew perfectly well that it was not, and decided, with great courtesy, to let it be.
[1562 Body Text] When she had gone Mrs Kind sat for some time with the shortbread tin unopened on the desk in front of her and the margin note she had written in May, three words in pencil, Who made the booking, under her hand.
[1563 Body Text] She had spent twenty-two years in travel, and her father forty before that, and in all that time no trip had ever happened without somebody asking for it. Not one. People thought of travel as the aircraft, the hotel, the coach. It was not. Travel was a request, made by somebody, at a time, through someone, and paid for. Everything else was logistics. If you wanted to know why a person had ended up somewhere they had not meant to be, you did not look at the aircraft. You looked at the booking.
[1564 Body Text] The department had spent seven months looking at the aircraft. Rewrites, screens, vacuum, throats. Very interesting aircraft. The department had treated the booking as a metaphor. It had not asked to see the order. It had been a fluctuation. An event. Something that happened to the surface of the country the way weather happens.
[1565 Body Text] But Mrs Kind had said it herself, in May, in that room, over the case: an airline does not know what Croydon is. Somebody had read the register. Somebody had wanted fourteen thin records. And somebody who wants a thing sends for it.
[1566 Body Text] She opened the shortbread. She ate one piece, standing, which was not done. Then she picked up the telephone and rang Marsham Street and asked for Miss Pike. Miss Pike was the person in that building who knew where things went.
[1567 Body Text] “Adaeze. A question. Does the department keep a note of who it sends things to?”
[1568 Body Text] “Of course,” said Miss Pike. “Everything that leaves this office goes in the system.”
[1569 Body Text] “And outside the system?”
[1570 Body Text] There was a short pause on the line.
[1571 Body Text] “I keep a ledger,” said Miss Pike, slightly defensively. “By hand. Everybody laughs.”
[1572 Body Text] “Nobody will laugh,” said Mrs Kind, “in about three months’ time. Keep it somewhere safe, dear. And when Ms Wren is less busy — not this week; she has a wormhole — tell her a travel agent said to read it.”
[1573 Body Text] ∗
[1574 First Paragraph] Mrs Kind’s ninth of February had been in the notebook since May, with its two instructions underneath. By October it was the only item on the backlog that no one had touched. Nobody knew what touching it looked like, and on the Monday of the lieutenant’s week Mrs Kind said that she did, and that it looked like a mailshot.
[1575 Body Text] “You write to them,” she said. “All three hundred and eight. You don’t tell them anything. You remind them they’ve got options. People forget. I had a man in 1994 who’d forgotten he had a return half.”
[1576 Body Text] The letter was drafted by committee, which was the first mistake, and the committee was the department, which was the second. Mrs Kind wanted it warm. Beatrix wanted it unactionable. Fainrose wanted every adjective justified. Stadthof, who happened to be in the building about an organ, wanted a closing line with a cadence in it, and got one. Lolly read the ninth draft at a quarter to midnight, could not find anything in it that was untrue, and signed it.
[1577 Body Text] Dear Resident, it began. The department would be grateful to learn whether you have reached a decision in the matter recorded against your name. If you have not, the department wishes you to know that every option open to you remains open, and that further options may be available on application. A reply slip is enclosed for your convenience. You need not use it. We should, however, be glad if you did.
[1578 Body Text] The reply slip had three boxes: I have decided. I would like more time. I would like to talk to someone.
[1579 Body Text] Three hundred and eight went out on the Tuesday. On the Thursday an evening paper got hold of one from a man in Bexley who had read further options may be available on application aloud in a pub, and the Friday edition carried it on page five under WHITEHALL TO HAND OUT CHOICES, with a photograph of Marsham Street and the words open to the public Saturday, which no one in the department had said, written or believed.
[1580 Body Text] By half past eight on the Saturday morning there were eleven hundred and forty people in a queue that went round two sides of the building and into Horseferry Road.
[1581 Body Text] “Well,” said Mrs Kind, at the window, in her good coat. “We’ll need tables.”
[1582 Body Text] They had tables by nine. Mrs Kind ran it the way she had run the Croydon holiday fair every February for twenty-two years, which was to say that within forty minutes there were numbered tickets, a separate queue for people with prams, and a tea table, and a sign in her own capitals that said ENQUIRIES HERE, which Beatrix asked her to take down and then, on reflection, asked her to change to QUESTIONS. Miss Pike took names. Mavis-9 printed receipts, because receipts were the one thing she could certify, and she certified them with a small electric sigh that the queue came to find reassuring.
[1583 Body Text] Stadthof found a harmonium. Nobody ever established where. By ten he was playing hymns at the top of the stairs, very well and slightly too loud, and a woman from Pimlico asked Mrs Kind whether it was church, and Mrs Kind said it was government, which was similar but had fewer exits.
[1584 Body Text] The public address system hummed. It had hummed since the doors opened, a low, ugly, steady note under every announcement, and Gideon was four hundred metres under Cumbria, and the man from Facilities said the system was fine and the building was old. Lolly stood under the speaker on the second floor and listened to it and thought about her father, without knowing why.
[1585 Body Text] People wanted things. A man from Lewisham wanted a third option. He had two, he said, and they were both his sister’s idea. Mrs Kind asked what the third one would be. He said that was the point, he didn’t know, that was why he’d come, and she gave him a brochure for the Lake District and told him to start there. A woman from Stockwell wanted to hand one of hers back. She’d had three, she said, and it was too many, and could the government take the one about her ex-husband’s brother. Miss Pike looked at Beatrix. Beatrix said, after some thought, that the department had no power to accept a surrendered option but could note that one had been offered. Mavis-9 issued a receipt. OPTION RETURNED (1). NOT ACCEPTED. NOTED. The woman had it laminated, and afterwards wrote to say so.
[1586 Body Text] At half past eleven a pensioner from Peckham asked Stadthof whether he took requests and Stadthof said only Bach, and the pensioner said fair enough, and asked for Jerusalem, and got it, and the queue on the stairs sang, and Fainrose came out of her office to find out what the noise was and stood on the landing with a cup of tea she did not drink and her face doing nothing at all.
[1587 Body Text] “Is it working?” said Lolly.
[1588 Body Text] “Eleven hundred people have come to a government building on a Saturday to be reminded that they may change their minds,” said Fainrose. “I am not reassured.”
[1589 Body Text] Mrs Chain came at one. She had a letter in her gloved hand, and she did not queue, and nobody asked her to.
[1590 Body Text] “You have written to my sister,” she said. She put the letter on Lolly’s table. Dear Resident. M. Chain. Register number 117. “She would like to know whether she has decided.”
[1591 Body Text] Lolly looked at it. She had signed three hundred and eight of them at midnight. She had not read the names.
[1592 Body Text] “I’m sorry,” she said. “She’s on the schedule as a record. The mailing list was drawn from the schedule. Nobody took her off it because nobody is supposed to—”
[1593 Body Text] “I know why.” Mrs Chain took the chair opposite, and the umbrella went across her knees. “I am not here to complain. I am here because Margaret would have come. She liked a queue. She liked to see who was in it.” She looked down the line of tables. “Your urn is stewing.”
[1594 Body Text] It was. It was a tea urn borrowed from the canteen, the size of a dustbin, and it had been on since eight, and it had been plugged in by somebody from Facilities who had also, to be safe, clipped its earth strap to the radiator. Mrs Chain got up, crossed the floor, and switched it off at the wall on the grounds that tea which has stewed for five hours is no longer tea but a statement.
[1595 Body Text] The hum stopped.
[1596 Body Text] It stopped everywhere: under the speaker on the second floor, at the top of the stairs under Stadthof’s harmonium, in the lobby. For a moment the whole building was quiet, and eleven hundred people looked up at the ceiling, and Mrs Chain looked at the urn.
[1597 Body Text] “Well,” she said. “That was the urn.” She did not switch it back on.
[1598 Body Text] Lolly wrote urn — radiator — hum on the back of a reply slip and put it in her pocket for Gideon.
[1599 Body Text] Eilstein came at three. Ramanathan wanted a reading in the lobby, and he was on the instrument list now and could be asked. He stood outside the barrier with his hat in his hand. A woman at the back of the queue, seventy at least, in a headscarf, looked at him standing there and said, “Come in, love, you’ll catch your death,” and held the rope up, and he was lawfully invited, by a member of the public, into a building with eleven hundred people in it, every one of whom had come to decide something.
[1600 Body Text] He went up one flight. He stood at the top of the stairs beside the harmonium for perhaps a minute, with his face gone the colour of the plaster. Then he came down again, carefully, holding the banister, and went out through the barrier, and stood on the pavement in the cold.
[1601 Body Text] Lolly went out after him.
[1602 Body Text] “Are you all right?”
[1603 Body Text] “You have laid on a banquet,” said Albrecht Eilstein, “and invited a man who has sworn off food.” He pulled the hat down. “It is a very fine thing you are doing in there. I should like to be a long way from it.” He looked at the queue. “I find I would like a list, Ms Wren. Of what I may take and when. In public. So that the next time a kind woman holds a rope up for me I will know before I go in whether I am a guest or a burglar.”
[1604 Body Text] It was, Lolly thought afterwards, the afternoon Article Two’s schedule began.
[1605 Body Text] They closed the doors at six. Mrs Kind counted the tickets. Mavis-9 had issued four hundred and two receipts and said she would like it minuted that none of them had been disputed. Stadthof played a last chorale to an empty staircase and said it was the best acoustic in Westminster and no one had ever told him.
[1606 Body Text] “Best-run fair I ever saw,” said Mrs Kind, putting her coat on. “And I’ve seen Croydon at Easter.”
[1607 Body Text] ∗
[1608 First Paragraph] That night, in the notebook, Lolly wrote:
[1609 Body Text] Options Day. Eleven hundred and forty in the queue, three hundred and eight letters, one harmonium, four hundred and two receipts. Mrs Chain cured the public address system by switching off an urn. Eilstein was invited in and could not stay.
[1610 Body Text] Karl Schwarzschild never saw the end of his war. I would like, once, to finish something before the paper runs out.
[1611 Body Text] 108 days.
[1612 Body Text] Under the notebook, on the kitchen table, was the first reply slip back, which had come in Friday’s post and which Mrs Kind had given her to keep because it was the first. Lolly had not turned it over. DECIDED. THANK YOU. M. TULL (212).
[1613 Heading 2] Chapter Fifteen
[1614 Heading 3] The Remaining Possibilities
[1615 First Paragraph] The bench van Casteel had promised took nine days to build and forty minutes to use, and Gideon spent most of the nine days four hundred metres underground, holding things.
[1616 Body Text] It was a lower level of the mine van Casteel had worked in for nineteen years, abandoned in the seventies and left to the pumps, a long way below the leased chamber and, by his own insistence, nowhere near it. Two plates, a torsion balance recalibrated to Ramanathan’s specification, a vacuum can the size of a dustbin, and a length of superconducting cable run down from the surface for no reason van Casteel ever fully explained except that a man is entitled to one extravagance per experiment and this was his. Gideon ran the cable. He opened a knuckle on the ladder on the second day and did not mention it, and van Casteel noticed on the third and did not mention it either, which Gideon took as a sign that they were going to get on.
[1617 Body Text] Van Casteel talked while he worked. He talked about the plates the way other men talk about horses. “Gold over silicon,” he said. “Flat to a few nanometres. A sphere would be easier, everybody uses a sphere now, a sphere does not care about parallelism. But Ms Wren’s question is about two walls and a gap, and I am a sentimental man.” He did not look sentimental. He looked like a man who had been disappointed by vacuum for forty years and was prepared to be disappointed again, carefully.
[1618 Body Text] On the fifth day Eilstein came down.
[1619 Body Text] That had been Gideon’s idea, and he had been surprised to have it. He had been sitting on the step of the cage at the bottom of the shaft eating a pasty, watching van Casteel take the same reading for the third time because the second had looked too pleased with itself, and he had thought: the whole point is whether the number has anything to do with him. So run it with him here. Then run it with him not here. If it’s him, it’ll change. He had said it out loud, and van Casteel had put down his pen and looked at him as though the pasty had spoken.
[1620 Body Text] “That is a control,” van Casteel said. “You have just designed a control experiment, Mr Gideon, while eating a pie.”
[1621 Body Text] “Pasty.”
[1622 Body Text] “I withdraw the pie. The point stands.”
[1623 Body Text] Jago drove Eilstein up. It took seven hours with the stops, and Jago talked for six and a half of them, and Eilstein said afterwards that not even the memory he carried of the Council of Constance contained so much continuous speech. He came down the shaft in the cage with his hat in his hand, having been invited down it in writing by the leaseholder, which van Casteel had insisted on as a matter of form. He stood where he was told to stand, two metres from the can, in the light of the work lamps, and cast no shadow on the rock behind him, which the shift foreman, who had stayed late to see the posh Londoners, noticed and decided not to have noticed.
[1624 Body Text] They ran it with him there. They ran it with him back up the shaft and in the van with the engine running and Jago telling him about a man in Whitehaven who owed him a fiver. They ran it twice more each way, alternating, so that neither run could borrow anything from the time of day.
[1625 Body Text] The plates moved by four hundred and eleven attometres every time.
[1626 Body Text] Eilstein there: four hundred and eleven. Eilstein in the van: four hundred and eleven. Van Casteel read the figures out in a flat voice and wrote them in a column and drew a line under the column.
[1627 Body Text] “It is not him,” he said.
[1628 Body Text] “No,” said Gideon.
[1629 Body Text] “Then it is something, and it is not him, and it is not, I would bet my lease, the far end of anything.” Van Casteel took his glasses off. “It is the bench. It is always the bench. I have spent forty years learning that the universe is rarely trying to tell you something and the apparatus very often is.”
[1630 Body Text] Gideon did not answer. He was looking at the log.
[1631 Body Text] He had been keeping it by hand. Van Casteel kept everything by hand and it had seemed rude not to, and Dennis Wren had told him once, with the back off a Murphy, that a man who only looks at the meter is a man who has never seen what the meter does when he isn’t looking. He had written down every reading with the time beside it. And the residual — the little extra, the four hundred and eleven, the thing that was not ordinary Casimir — was not quite steady. It stepped. Once an hour it went up by a hair, stayed up for forty minutes, and came back down for twenty.
[1632 Body Text] “What runs forty minutes in every hour,” Gideon said, “down here?”
[1633 Body Text] Van Casteel put his glasses back on.
[1634 Body Text] It was the dewatering pump. The mine had to be pumped or it filled, and the pump on the level below cycled on its float switch, forty minutes on and twenty off, and drew enough current that some of it, as currents will, came home through the earth instead of the wire. Gideon found it on the seventh day, on his back under the frame with a torch in his teeth and the multimeter on his chest. The superconducting cable’s shield was bonded to earth at the surface, at the winding house, which was correct. It was also bonded at the bottom, to the balance frame, which was not. Somebody had done it to be safe. Somebody always does.
[1635 Body Text] “A ground loop,” said Gideon, from under the frame. He could hear Dennis saying it, about the hum and the two earths. He could hear Lolly, too, on the phone on Saturday night, telling him about an urn and a public address system. “The pump’s stray current goes up the hill through the rock and comes back down your shield. The shield doesn’t care, it’s superconducting. But the bolts at the bottom aren’t. Nor’s the plate mount. There’s a millivolt or so across them when the pump’s running, and next to nothing when it isn’t.”
[1636 Body Text] Van Casteel lay down on the floor of the mine beside him, at sixty-eight, in a good suit, and looked.
[1637 Body Text] “A millivolt between two plates a micron apart,” he said, after a while, “is an electrostatic pull of about a third of one per cent of the Casimir force. Which is exactly the size of the thing I came here to find.” He started to laugh. It was not a happy laugh and not an unhappy one; it was the laugh of someone finding his own fingerprint on the evidence. “The force goes as the square of the voltage over the square of the gap. Everyone who has ever measured Casimir has had to fight it. Lamoreaux fought it. The patch potentials on the gold alone would keep a man awake. And I built a beautiful instrument to see a sign correction from a wormhole, and I bonded the shield at both ends.”
[1638 Body Text] “You didn’t,” said Gideon. “Somebody did. Before. It’s got a tag on it from 2011.”
[1639 Body Text] “Then I failed to check it, which is worse.” Van Casteel got up, slowly. “Lift the bottom bond. Earth the whole bench at one point. Then we sweep a bias across the plates, and find the bottom of the parabola, and null whatever contact potential is left. Properly. The way the people who do this for a living do it.”
[1640 Body Text] Gideon lifted the bond. They ran it again on the eighth day, with the pump running and with it stopped, with Eilstein back in the van — he had asked to stay, on the grounds that he had come a long way and had never before been eliminated as a variable — and the residual was gone. Not smaller. Gone. The plates felt the Casimir force, the ordinary one, the one Casimir had calculated in 1948 for two perfect mirrors in the dark, corrected for the gold, the temperature, and the roughness, to within the error bars, which were small, because van Casteel had built the instrument.
[1641 Body Text] The ninth day was for writing it down.
[1642 Body Text] ∗
[1643 First Paragraph] “There is no throat,” said van Casteel, over the telephone, at half past six on the Monday morning, to a room in Marsham Street that had come in specially. “I want to say that first and plainly, because I was the one who raised it, a fortnight ago, and a man who raises a door owes the room the courtesy of closing it.”
[1644 Body Text] Lolly had her notebook open on her knee. She did not write anything for the first minute.
[1645 Body Text] “I said, in this room, that if there was a bridge behind that horizon, propped open by negative energy, the vacuum near it would carry a correction, and my plates could feel it. I built the plates. They felt something. It was a ground loop. Mr Gideon found it by keeping a handwritten log, which I recommend to every physicist in Europe, and he found it in the place nobody was pointing, which I gather is a family principle.” He turned a page. “With the loop removed the plates see Casimir and nothing else, at a sensitivity where a throat of the kind I was imagining would have shown. Not proof of absence, Ms Sloan will want me to say. A null at a stated sensitivity. But the theory had already told us what to expect, and I should have listened. Schwarzschild’s bridge pinches shut faster than light can cross it. To hold one open you need negative energy, and quantum field theory permits negative energy only in small amounts for short times, with the amount and the time traded against each other. The bounds are Ford and Roman’s. They are not generous. A throat a man could walk through is not forbidden by any single law. It is forbidden by all of them acting together, the way a loan is forbidden by a bank that will lend you anything you like for one second.”
[1646 Body Text] Ramanathan said, “And the fourteen.”
[1647 Body Text] “The fourteen went nowhere,” said van Casteel. “Ms Prosper is on a boat out of Peterhead. The girl of nineteen is a caseworker in Dundee. I have read your files. Every one of them is somewhere ordinary, living a life that was not on your register’s list of permitted options. They did not leave the universe. They left the list.”
[1648 Body Text] Eilstein, by the window, said nothing. Van Casteel seemed to hear him say it.
[1649 Body Text] “Mr Eilstein will forgive me,” he said. “He told this department he had carried the far end of a bridge on his back for longer than there have been bridges. He has carried a great deal on his back. Not that. Behind his horizon there is a great deal of him, and none of anyone else.”
[1650 Body Text] “I find,” said Eilstein, “that I am relieved. I had not liked to think of myself as a corridor.”
