[1651 Body Text] There was a silence in the room. Lolly heard Mrs Kind put her pen down.
[1652 Body Text] “Which leaves the only question that was ever real,” said van Casteel, “and it is not a question for a physicist. A screen does not rewrite itself. Something lowered the price of nothing on the fourteenth of March, and fourteen pending states — the fourteen thinnest, the fourteen with almost no margin left between two options that had already gone — fell through into whatever was actually available, which in Ms Prosper’s case was a survey boat. Dr Arakawa told you what a barrier is. The barrier was the list. The question is not where they went. The question is who held the pen.”
[1653 Body Text] Lolly wrote, then, for the first time that morning: Who held the pen. She did not need to write the second line. It had been in the notebook since August, under Mavis-9’s single certified copy, and the Office had since refused her Rule 40 application twice, the second time on paper so heavy that the envelope had needed extra stamps. She wrote it again anyway, and underlined it: Who asked for it sorted.
[1654 Body Text] Gideon came in from the early train at nine, with the knuckle still open and van Casteel’s handwritten log under his arm, and stood in the doorway of the meeting room.
[1655 Body Text] “I didn’t nudge it,” he said. “When the number went, it went. I want that written down.”
[1656 Body Text] “It’s written down,” said Lolly.
[1657 Body Text] “Good.” He looked at the whiteboard, where Ramanathan had already rubbed out the word throat and written ground loop and underlined it twice. “Your dad would’ve found it in a day,” he said. “I took seven.”
[1658 Body Text] “He’s had sixty years’ practice.”
[1659 Body Text] “He’ll say I was doing it wrong.”
[1660 Body Text] “You were,” said Lolly, and Gideon, for the first time in nine days, sat down.
[1661 Body Text] Drill’s instrument, invoked one last time, closed the liability question, and it was Beatrix who did it, on the Thursday, in front of the nine’s own representative, who had come in person for the first time since the case opened.
[1662 Body Text] “Mr Drill taught this department what to do with an instrument nobody has characterised,” she said. “Mr van Casteel has now characterised two instruments: his plates, and Mr Eilstein. There is no basement under the ledger and no corridor behind him. There is a full vessel, declared, in a room with people in it. An insurer can be told about that without being lied to, and I would rather hand the Crown that than the metaphor I believed a fortnight ago.”
[1663 Body Text] The nine’s representative — a small, precise woman named Okonkwo-Barrett who had said almost nothing throughout — asked one question only: “Is one Mr Eilstein adequate to the department’s purposes, going forward, or does the department require a second?”
[1664 Body Text] “God, no!” said Eilstein, before anyone else could answer, and it was the first time since the case began he had used the word without apparent irony, and no one remarked on it, though Lolly wrote it down twice.
[1665 Body Text] Okonkwo-Barrett wrote that answer in a book the size of a postcard and set the book on the schedule. She took her coat. She did not ask a second question.
[1666 Body Text] The nine undecidable cores were not resolved by the plates either, and Fainrose said so in four minutes. The department had been carrying a temptation for a fortnight and she wanted it put down. Nine undecidable stars and a hole behind Mr Eilstein in the same month, and the human mind wanted them to be one thing. They were not. The spectral-gap problem was undecidable in general, a theorem about Hamiltonians and not about Marsham Street, and no plate in Cumbria would turn those nine into yeses or noes. “The paperwork has no honest box for them,” she said, “and it should not be made to grow one.”
[1667 Body Text] Lolly said: “Then we file them as in transit. We don’t force the box. We are not the last people who will ever look at this register, and the next people will thank us for leaving the question open rather than for closing it wrong out of impatience.”
[1668 Body Text] The motion passed the departmental panel with every vote in the room, and Fainrose, who had been about to propose it herself, minuted that she had been beaten to it and was glad.
[1669 Body Text] Eilstein’s clause was Eilstein’s own idea, the one he had taken away from the pavement outside Options Day, and he brought it to Beatrix rather than waiting to be summoned, which no one in the department had ever seen him do.
[1670 Body Text] “I should like Article Two amended,” he said. “Not the invitation clause. Leave that as it stands; it is the only rule in my existence that has ever actually protected anyone, including, on one occasion I am not proud of, me. I should like a schedule added. A public one. Every discarded branch I take, from this day, logged and dated the moment before I take it, so that if anybody ever needs to ask what I have eaten, there is a manifest.”
[1671 Body Text] Beatrix put her pen down. “That is not a legal instrument. That is a confession, filed in advance, of everything you have not yet done.”
[1672 Body Text] “Yes,” said Eilstein. “Mr Drill told this room what I am. I have decided I would rather be evidence than a lead, for the remainder of however long I am obliged to exist, and I find I would rather start now than wait to be asked again by somebody with a warrant.”
[1673 Body Text] Lolly, watching Beatrix draft the schedule that afternoon, thought that Albrecht Eilstein had never before asked to be held to something before anyone had required it of him. She did not say so. It was not the kind of thing one said to Beatrix Sloan, or to him.
[1674 Body Text] ∗
[1675 First Paragraph] The letter from the Member of Parliament came on the Friday, addressed to the Permanent Secretary and copied, because somebody in the Member’s office was thorough, to the Ministry’s inquiry unit, which had not had a complaint about the department since the Fainrose Unpleasantness and opened it the way a dog opens a parcel.
[1676 Body Text] It concerned a constituent’s mother. Mrs Maureen Tull, of Harrow. Register number 212.
[1677 Body Text] Lolly took the slip out of the back of the notebook, where it had been for a fortnight, and turned it over for the first time. On the back, in the same blue biro, smaller: You can come and see what you did, if you like. Saturdays.
[1678 Body Text] “That’s an invitation,” said Mrs Kind, reading it upside down. “I’ve read enough booking forms. That’s her initiative, dear. Not yours.”
[1679 Body Text] Lolly went on the Saturday.
[1680 Body Text] Maureen Tull lived at the end of a cul-de-sac in a bungalow with a new ramp no one had used yet. She was seventy-nine, small and upright, and she smelled of Pears soap, and the hall smelled of a dog that had died some time ago and not been replaced. She wore a hearing aid that she switched off when she disagreed with something. She switched it on to let Lolly in.
[1681 Body Text] The register had held her for two years, under a heading in Schedule 4: whether to sell and go to her son in Leeds, or stay. Two options, both live, a wide margin. One of the fattest records on the list. Lolly had been pleased with it, in May.
[1682 Body Text] “I had your letter,” said Mrs Tull. It was on the mantelpiece, propped against the clock. “Lovely paper.”
[1683 Body Text] “It wasn’t meant to ask you anything,” said Lolly. “It was meant to offer.”
[1684 Body Text] “It had a slip. Three boxes.” Mrs Tull sat down. “I’m seventy-nine. Nobody sends me three boxes unless they want me to tick one. So I ticked one.”
[1685 Body Text] “Which?”
[1686 Body Text] “Stay.” She looked out at the ramp. “I’d been leaving it open for him. Every time Gary rang I’d say I’m still thinking, love, and he’d say take your time, Mum, and on the second Sunday he’d come down and look at the stairs, and I’d look at him looking. Two years. The nicest two years we’ve had since his dad. Then your letter came and I thought, they want to know, and I’m not having the government think I can’t make my mind up. So I made it up. I rang him and told him, and he said, right, and he hasn’t been down since. He’s having the spare room done for a lodger.” She switched the hearing aid off, and then on again. “I was keeping it open for him, all that time. Your letter shut it.”
[1687 Body Text] Lolly sat with her hands round a cup she was not drinking.
[1688 Body Text] “I’m afraid not,” she said. “There isn’t anything I’m allowed to—”
[1689 Body Text] “I know. I read the back of your form. Paragraph nine.” Mrs Tull’s mouth twitched. “You asked. I answered. You don’t get to ask again. That’s the only fair thing on it.”
[1690 Body Text] On the doorstep she said, “Gary’s written to his MP. I told him not to. He said somebody ought to be told. I expect he’s right. Somebody ought.” She shut the door.
[1691 Body Text] ∗
[1692 First Paragraph] Beatrix had the defence drafted by Sunday night, and it was good, and on Monday morning it lasted nine minutes.
[1693 Body Text] “It was an offer, not an enquiry,” she told the inquiry officer, a man from the Cabinet Office called Pell, with a voice like a drawer being closed. “The letter offered assistance. Nothing in it required an answer.”
[1694 Body Text] “It enclosed a reply slip,” said Mr Pell, “with a box headed I have decided. Ms Sloan, I have been reading reply slips for thirty years. A reply slip is an enquiry with a stamp on it.”
[1695 Body Text] Beatrix looked at the slip for some time. Then she said, “Yes. It is,” and sat down, which Lolly had never seen her do in the middle of an argument.
[1696 Body Text] Paragraph nine had a sanction, because Lolly had written one into the notebook the year before last, on the grounds that a protection without a sanction is a decoration. The officer responsible was to be suspended pending inquiry, on the signature of the department’s oversight. The department’s oversight was Stephanie Fainrose.
[1697 Body Text] Fainrose signed it at Lolly’s desk, at Lolly’s request, and not in a corridor. She used the green pen. Her hand did not shake. Afterwards she capped the pen and looked at it as if it had done something without asking her.
[1698 Body Text] “I am not reassured,” she said, “by any part of this.”
[1699 Body Text] “You’d be worse at it if you were,” said Lolly.
[1700 Body Text] Then Mr Pell asked for the notebook.
[1701 Body Text] He was entitled to. The letter to the three hundred and eight had been drafted from it, Make everybody fat, and it was the only contemporaneous record of the decision. Lolly took it out of her bag. Volume five, green, three-quarters full, PRIVATE / UNHELPFUL across the cover in her own capitals. Mr Pell put it in a clear plastic bag, sealed the bag, and wrote on the label Exhibit LW/1. He was not unkind about it. He did not need to be.
[1702 Body Text] At two minutes past eleven her pass stopped opening the third-floor door. Mavis-9, who had never been told anything about her except that she was an officer, informed her from the speaker by the lifts that her appeal against the second Rule 40 refusal had lapsed, the applicant no longer holding a qualifying office, and thanked her for her enquiry.
[1703 Body Text] She went home at a quarter past eleven in the morning, which she had never done, on a bus that was nearly empty, and found that she did not know what to do with her hands. At the Post Office on the corner she bought a pad of lined paper. She did not write in it.
[1704 Heading 2] Chapter Sixteen
[1705 Heading 3] The Fifteenth Line
[1706 First Paragraph] The Best Interests meeting was held on Thursday the twelfth of November in the Larchfield lounge, because the room booked for it at the council offices had been double-booked with a meeting about room bookings. Lolly went as a private person, which was what she was that month. It turned out to be the right thing to be.
[1707 Body Text] Lolly had expected a table. There was a coffee table, and round it, in chairs of four different heights, Ms Hollis Breen with the file, Sandra from the night shift who had swapped onto days for it, a manager from the provider’s regional office called Mr Ashe who had driven from Reading and wanted it known, Gideon in a jacket he had clearly bought for a funeral, and Mrs Chain with the umbrella across her knees. Her father sat by the window, where he always sat, and had been told what the meeting was for twice, and the second time had said, “I know, Lol, I’m not deaf, I’m demented,” which was a new joke, and he was pleased with it, and Sandra laughed, and Mr Ashe did not know whether he was allowed to.
[1708 Body Text] Ms Breen did it properly. Lolly had been afraid she would not, and she did. She read out the options. She read out Beatrix’s letter, all five paragraphs, without comment, though at paragraph 4 she looked up at Lolly over her glasses. She asked Dennis where he wanted to live.
[1709 Body Text] “Here,” said Dennis. “Downstairs. By that.” He pointed at the window. “And Sundays.”
[1710 Body Text] “What happens on Sundays, Mr Wren?”
[1711 Body Text] “He comes,” said Dennis, pointing at Gideon, “and I tell him what he’s doing wrong.”
[1712 Body Text] Ms Breen wrote that down. Then she asked Gideon, as a person interested in Mr Wren’s welfare, whether that was accurate.
[1713 Body Text] Gideon had prepared. He had rung her on Tuesday to ask whether it was all right to say diode in a Best Interests meeting. He did not say any of what he had prepared.
[1714 Body Text] “He’s right,” Gideon said. “I am doing it wrong. Mostly. He catches it about one time in three, which is better than me.” He looked at his hands. “Last Sunday he found a dry joint in a Hacker by smell. I’d had the back off an hour. If that goes, I’d want it written down that it went, and when. But it hasn’t gone.”
[1715 Body Text] “And Mrs Chain.”
[1716 Body Text] Mrs Chain said, once and plainly, what Dennis Wren had said to her on a Tuesday in July: the window, the buses, Sundays, and someone to find fault with. She gave the circumstances. She gave the correction. Broken by fools. Mr Ashe wrote that down, and then looked at it, and then crossed out fools and wrote it again more neatly.
[1717 Body Text] “Option C,” said Ms Breen, at the end, “as I understand Ms Sloan’s letter, would be a room on the ground floor, which the residential side is keeping, rather than either of the two moves.”
[1718 Body Text] “The ground floor’s nursing,” said Mr Ashe. “It’s not the same registration category.”
[1719 Body Text] “It’s dual-registered,” said Sandra. Everybody looked at her. “I checked. It’s on the certificate in the office. They did it in 2019 for Mr Okafor. We’ve got one room left on the garden side that isn’t commissioned. Garden Room. It’s got a window on the bus stop. It’s got a sink.” She folded her arms. “And there’s a cupboard by the laundry that’s empty since they took the ironing machine out, and it’s got a socket, and I’m just saying.”
[1720 Body Text] “A socket,” said Mr Ashe.
[1721 Body Text] “For a bench,” said Sandra. “Sundays. Supervised. The residents’ committee voted for it in September and nobody wrote it up.”
[1722 Body Text] “Electrical items brought onto the premises,” said Mr Ashe, with the despair of a man watching a policy go, “have to be PAT-tested.”
[1723 Body Text] “I’ll PAT-test the PAT tester,” said Dennis Wren from the window, and that was the end of Mr Ashe.
[1724 Body Text] It was not the end of the process. Nothing ever was. Ms Breen said that she would recommend Option C to the funding panel as the least restrictive option consistent with Mr Wren’s needs, and that she could not promise what the panel would do. Mr Ashe said he would need to take it back to Reading. Sandra said Reading could come here if it wanted, there was a bus.
[1725 Body Text] Afterwards, in the car park, in the dark, Mrs Chain put up her umbrella, though it was not raining.
[1726 Body Text] “You said it once,” said Lolly. “Plainly.”
[1727 Body Text] “I did.”
[1728 Body Text] “Thank you.”
[1729 Body Text] “Do not thank me. I was asked. That is the whole of it.” Mrs Chain looked back at the building, at the lit window on the ground floor where Dennis Wren was explaining something to Gideon with both hands. “Margaret would have liked him,” she said. “He laughs at the wrong jokes. Mostly his own.”
[1730 Body Text] On the bus home Lolly thought about Sandra, who had checked. The provider’s system had said one thing, and Mr Ashe had believed the system, and a care worker on a swapped shift had walked down a corridor and read a piece of paper in a frame. Systems withheld. Paper mostly just sat there and waited for somebody to read it.
[1731 Body Text] And there was a woman in Marsham Street who wrote everything down twice.
[1732 Body Text] She could not ask as an officer; she was not one. She could not ask Mavis-9; Mavis-9 no longer believed she existed. She could ask a friend a question, as a private person, and let the friend decide whether to answer. Paragraph nine had nothing to say about that. She checked, on the bus, against the copy she knew by heart, and it had nothing to say.
[1733 Body Text] She rang Adaeze Pike from the stop. “You don’t have to answer this,” she said. “Not tonight, not ever. Not the system. Your book. The one certified copy of Schedule 4. When you’re ready, would you tell me what the line says?”
[1734 Body Text] The line was quiet for long enough that Lolly thought it had dropped.
[1735 Body Text] “I don’t need to look,” said Miss Pike. “I’ve known since August. I need to be ready.” She rang off.
[1736 Body Text] The panel met in January. It approved Option C on the fourteenth, by letter, in two paragraphs, with a note about PAT testing. Her father moved downstairs on a Saturday with the Roberts under his arm, refusing help on the stairs and accepting it on the corridor, and the first thing he did in the Garden Room was turn the chair to face the window.
[1737 Body Text] ∗
[1738 First Paragraph] Adaeze Pike came to Lolly’s flat on a Tuesday evening in January, with the ledger in a carrier bag. She had known what the line said since August, she said, and had spent the weeks since the bus stop being brave enough, and Mrs Kind had rung her at Christmas and told her that in a travel agent’s experience knowing and being brave were not the same thing and only one of them was required.
[1739 Body Text] Lolly read it at the kitchen table anyway, page by page, from March, the way she read everything, though Miss Pike had marked the page with a bus ticket. Miss Pike sat opposite with her coat on and watched her.
[1740 Body Text] “The second of March,” said Lolly. “Requested by telephone. These are your initials.”
[1741 Body Text] “Yes.”
[1742 Body Text] “Who telephoned?”
[1743 Body Text] Miss Pike looked at the line. Her own handwriting looked back at her, neat and level and satisfied with itself. Statement of Coherence, with Schedule 4 (monthly margins, all 308 states, sorted thinnest first), one certified copy.
[1744 Body Text] “A man,” she said. “He said he was calling from the Office of the President, and he gave a reference, and the reference was correct. He was very polite. He said that the Office would find the schedule more useful sorted by margin than by district, thinnest first, and asked whether that would be a great deal of trouble.” She swallowed. “It was not a great deal of trouble. It was a sort. I did it in the system and printed it and certified it and wrote it in the book, and Jago took it, and I thought, I remember thinking, how nice, somebody in Vienna is actually going to read it properly.”
[1745 Body Text] Lolly did not answer at once. It was, Miss Pike thought later, the kindest thing she could have done.
[1746 Body Text] “I sorted it for them,” said Miss Pike. “Sir. Ms Wren. I sorted it so that the first fourteen were on the top of the first page. And in August I told you the system said withheld, and it did, and I let you think that was all there was.” Her voice stayed level. She had decided long ago that it would, whatever the rest of her did, and it did. “I would like to offer my resignation. I’ll draft it tonight.”
[1747 Body Text] “I’m suspended, Adaeze. I can’t accept it.”
[1748 Body Text] “Then—”
[1749 Body Text] “And I’m not going to let you give it to anyone who can.” Lolly turned the ledger round on the table between them, open at the line. “Somebody rang a junior officer with a correct reference and asked a reasonable question, and the junior officer did the reasonable thing, and then she did the only thing in this whole year that nobody else did. She wrote down who asked and how. In ink. In a book the system can’t edit.” She was pale. “There are three hundred and eight people on that schedule, and fourteen of them were taken off the top of it, and the only reason anybody on earth can prove it is that you bought a ledger in Victoria Street the morning after Column B. Beatrix is going to need an affidavit.”
[1750 Body Text] Miss Pike looked at the line for a long time.
[1751 Body Text] “It will have to say that I sorted it,” she said. “And August.”
[1752 Body Text] “It will say exactly what happened,” said Lolly. “That’s what it’s for.”
[1753 Body Text] When Miss Pike had gone, Lolly sat with the line. Jago took it. Jago Flint had never in his life handed anything over without a signature, on the principle that a signature was a favour in writing. She rang him.
[1754 Body Text] “Jago. The third of March. An envelope from Adaeze to the Strand. Do you keep dockets?”
[1755 Body Text] “Do I keep dockets,” said Jago, wounded to the soul, and went out to the van in his slippers.
[1756 Body Text] ∗
[1757 First Paragraph] Jago Flint found the docket in the glovebox of the van, under a road atlas of 1997 and a sandwich that had achieved a kind of geological peace.
[1758 Body Text] He had been asked to look for it, which was new; people mostly asked Jago to stop doing things. And the docket was there, in the clip with all the others, because Jago kept every docket he had ever been handed, not out of diligence, which he would have denied with real hurt, but because a docket was proof that somebody owed you a favour, and Jago’s whole economy ran on the conviction that one day everybody would pay.
[1759 Body Text] 3 March. Collected Marsham St, 08:40, one envelope, certified, from A. Pike. Delivered Strand, 09:25, to bearer for the Office of the President (Vienna bag). Signed: and a signature like a man falling downstairs, and under it, in the runner’s careful capitals because Jago had made him do it, a name.
[1760 Body Text] He looked at it for a while in the car park, with the engine running and the wipers going for no reason.
[1761 Body Text] He remembered the runner. A young man in a good coat, impatient, who had not wanted to print his name, and Jago had said, cheerfully, that nothing left his hands unsigned, not since 2009, not since an incident in a stairwell, and the young man had printed it to get rid of him. Jago remembered thinking it was a lot of fuss for a list of margins. He remembered that Miss Pike had lent him eleven pounds forty that morning, for the congestion charge, because he had come out without his wallet, and that he had promised to pay her back on the Friday.
[1762 Body Text] He had not paid her back on the Friday.
[1763 Body Text] He drove to Lolly’s flat, and parked where the ticket would be worth discussing, and put the docket on Lolly’s kitchen table. She was the only person he knew who would know what it was and not be allowed to do anything about it, and he found he liked that. Then he drove to Marsham Street and went up to Miss Pike’s desk and put eleven pounds forty on it, in coins, in a neat column, which took some time because a good deal of it was in twenty-pences.
[1764 Body Text] “It’s from March,” he said.
[1765 Body Text] Miss Pike looked at the column of coins. She looked at Jago. Something happened to her face that had not happened to it since the autumn.
[1766 Body Text] “I wrote it down,” she said. “You’re in the back of the ledger. Under owed.”
[1767 Body Text] “Course I am,” said Jago. “Cross me off, Adaeze. I’m a reformed man. Ask anyone. Don’t ask Croydon.”
[1768 Body Text] ∗
[1769 First Paragraph] The letter reached the Collegium at Brask on a Monday and Professor Tengelman read it standing up, in the porter’s lodge, with his coat still on.
[1770 Body Text] It was two pages long. The first page set out, without adjectives, the dispatch ledger, the docket, the schedule and the fourteen lines. The second page asked one question.
[1771 Body Text] You told the Office that rewriting a screen preserves what was on it. I do not dispute it. I would like to know what you were asked, and when, and whether you were told what was written on the screen.
[1772 Body Text] It was written on her own notepaper, not the department’s, because that winter she was not allowed the department’s. It was signed L. Wren, in green, and under the signature, in pencil, as if added afterwards by somebody who had decided it was rude to leave it off: Please.
[1773 Body Text] He took it up to his rooms. He had been, for six years, a man who answered the question he was asked. He had built a life on it, a reputation, a retainer, a car. It was a real virtue, he had always believed; the world was full of men who answered questions they had not been asked, and they did a great deal of harm. He took the March letter out of the old folder, and read it again, and then read his own reply, three lines long, and correct, and the worst thing he had ever written.
[1774 Body Text] On the desk, under the March letter, was the one that began Dear Ms Sloan and did not go on. He had been not-writing it since June.
[1775 Body Text] He did not finish it now either. He took a fresh sheet, and wrote Dear Ms Wren, and then: I was asked whether the rewrite would be unitary. I was not told what was on the screen. Nor did I inquire. I enclose the letter. I will come to Vienna, at my own expense, and say so to the court, and I would be grateful if you would arrange for somebody to ask me, because I find, at my age, that I still do not entirely trust myself to stand up without being asked.
[1776 Body Text] He posted it himself, on the way to his lecture, and was four minutes late for the first time in thirty years.
[1777 Body Text] ∗
[1778 First Paragraph] The appeal was heard in Vienna on Thursday the twenty-eighth of January, twelve days before the deadline, in the same building off the Ringstrasse, in a smaller room with no painted ceiling and three judges who had each, Lolly was told, read the papers.
[1779 Body Text] She had not meant to be the one who spoke. Beatrix had the argument, and it was a better argument than it had been in the spring. Ramanathan had given it a characterised instrument and van Casteel had given it a force. But the argument answered only half of the question the Office had put: whether anything had been harmed. It did not answer the other half, which nobody had put at all, and which Lolly had been carrying since March: who had done it, and how they had known which fourteen to take.
[1780 Body Text] Miss Pike had brought the answer to her kitchen on a Tuesday in January, in the dispatch ledger, on the line for the second of March:
[1781 Body Text] Certified copy of the Statement and of Schedule 4, thinnest first. To: Office of the President, Galactic Counsel, Vienna. Requested by telephone. Fee paid.
[1782 Body Text] Twelve days, Lolly counted, before the fourteenth of March.
[1783 Body Text] “Ms Wren,” said the presiding judge, a grey-haired woman from a signatory whose name the interpretation rendered as the Hollow Reaches. “You are, I understand, a suspended officer of the department, not its counsel. Ms Sloan tells us you have something the papers do not.”
[1784 Body Text] “I have a ledger, a courier’s docket, and a list,” said Lolly. Her voice was steadier than she had expected. She had written the sentence nine times on the train. “Schedule 4 is a list of three hundred and eight people, sorted by how little choice they had left. We published it because the Act required it, and we were proud of it. On the second of March the Office of the President bought a certified copy, by telephone, and asked for it sorted thinnest first. Miss Pike’s affidavit is at tab nine. On the third of March it was signed for at the Strand by the Office’s own runner. The docket is at tab ten. On the fourteenth of March, fourteen of them stepped outside their permitted options.” She laid the schedule on the table and put the department’s list of the fourteen beside it. “They are the first fourteen lines of the schedule. All of them, and nobody else.”
[1785 Body Text] Counsel for the Office, a young man who had clearly been told the hearing would be about physics, said that a fluctuation in the vacuum would naturally take the weakest records first, and that the correspondence was what one would expect.
[1786 Body Text] “Then it would take most of them,” said Lolly. “Not exactly the first fourteen and not the fifteenth. The fifteenth had a margin one part in a thousand wider than the fourteenth. If the field had been doing it, the fifteenth would have gone too, or one of the fourteen would have survived. Dr Ramanathan has run it. A process that is only following the margins does not stop at the bottom of a page.” She took a breath. “Somebody stopped at fourteen because fourteen was the number they had asked for.”
[1787 Body Text] The young man looked at his papers. The papers did not help him.
[1788 Body Text] Then he found something in them after all. “The court may wish to know,” he said, “that the witness is suspended for a breach of the very schedule she relies on. Paragraph nine. An enquiry put to a Mrs Tull of Harrow without her initiative.”
[1789 Body Text] “It was,” said Lolly. “I put it. I sent her a letter asking whether she had decided, and she decided, and it cost her son his place in her house. I’m suspended for it and I should be. It’s at tab eleven. I asked Ms Sloan to include it.” She did not look at Beatrix. “The Office bought a list of the people with the least choice left and spent it. I wrote to people on the same list and asked them to spend some. The difference is that I wrote mine down, and I’m here.”
[1790 Body Text] The presiding judge made a note. “And who asked for it sorted, Ms Wren?”
[1791 Body Text] Lolly turned to the end of the table, where a man in a brown suit had been sitting for an hour without moving with a folder on his knees.
[1792 Body Text] Professor Tengelman had answered her letter by return. He had come at his own expense. He stood up now without being asked, which, Beatrix said afterwards, was the moment she knew they had won, because the Office had kept him on a retainer for six years and had never once seen him stand up without being asked.
[1793 Body Text] “On the ninth of March I received a letter from the Office of the President,” he said. “I have it here. It says that the Office meant to rewrite fourteen records as a pilot on the fourteenth, to show the signatories, ahead of the session, that discontinuation would destroy no information. It asked me to confirm that such a rewrite is unitary.” He put the letter on the table, beside the schedule. “I confirmed it. It is true. I was not told that the records were people. I did not ask. I am asking now that the court note that I should have.”
[1794 Body Text] The room let it stand.
[1795 Body Text] Then Beatrix stood up and gave the argument she had been refining since the spring, the one about a conservation law not being a defence to a charge of taking, and this time it was not an argument about a hypothetical harm. It was an argument about fourteen named lines on a schedule, a pilot with a date on it, a dip on four instruments, and a list of two hundred and six cores. She took twelve minutes. Lolly did not hear most of it. She was watching the three judges read Tengelman’s letter, one after another, and pass it along.
[1796 Body Text] The ruling came at four. The decision to discontinue the Galactic Health Support Programme was set aside, on the ground that the session had voted on a brief that withheld a material fact: that the Office had already tested its premise, on fourteen citizens, without their consent. The Programme’s map of thin places was to be returned to the Programme within seven days and to be held by no one else. The conduct of the Office in March was referred to the Counsel’s inspectorate. The word supernova was in the second paragraph.
[1797 Body Text] On the steps outside, in the cold, Beatrix put her hand briefly on Lolly’s shoulder, which she had never done before.
[1798 Body Text] “You read the ledger,” she said.
[1799 Body Text] “It’s what I’m for,” said Lolly. “I write things down, and then I read them.”
[1800 Body Text] Tengelman came down the steps behind them, buttoning his coat. He stopped as if he meant to say something, and then he only nodded to Lolly, the nod of someone who had answered a narrow question accurately for six years and had just answered a wider one, and went off towards the tram.
[1801 Heading 2] Chapter Seventeen
[1802 Heading 3] Nothing Happens on the Ninth
[1803 First Paragraph] The inquiry into Mrs Tull reported on the Wednesday after Vienna, in a room on the fourth floor with the radiator off. Mr Pell read it out himself. The breach was proven. There had been no bad faith and no benefit, and the officer had reported the breach against herself to an appeal court, which Mr Pell said he had not encountered before and did not wish to encourage. A final written warning, to stand for two years. Reinstatement from the following Monday, on conditions.
[1804 Body Text] “The conditions,” said Mr Pell, and turned the page as if it weighed something. “Correspondence with registered persons is removed from the officer’s duties and given to a two-signature procedure, neither signature hers. The officer may not write to Mrs Tull, or to any member of her family, about the matter, for any purpose, including apology. And Exhibit LW/1 is retained.” He looked at the evidence bag on the table between them. “Mr Gary Tull’s complaint is with the Ombudsman, and it is open. Until it closes, the notebook is evidence. After it closes it goes to the National Archives under the retention schedule, class B, closed for twenty years, as the only contemporaneous record of a decision of state.” He paused. “I am told that is a compliment. It does not feel like one from this side of the table either.”
[1805 Body Text] Fainrose handed her the warning. It was in green.
[1806 Body Text] “I have issued one of these before,” she said. “To myself. In May. For the state of the floors.” She waited while Lolly read it. “It does not get easier to sign. That is how you know it is the right size.”
[1807 Body Text] Mr Pell did not return the notebook. He gave her a receipt for it, which she signed, and a photocopy of its last page, which she had not asked for and found she could not look at. At her desk she folded the warning into the Post Office pad, the one with nothing written in it, next to Beatrix’s letter.
[1808 Body Text] She wanted, very much, to write to Mrs Tull. She did not. That was the point of paragraph nine, and now it was also the point of condition two, and she found it was possible to agree with a rule completely and still sit at a desk for an hour with her coat on.
[1809 Body Text] On the Friday a letter came to the department addressed to Miss Wren, Ministry, the one with the paper, in blue biro. The two-signature procedure opened it, held a short meeting about whether it was allowed to, and gave it to her.
[1810 Body Text] Dear Miss Wren. You are not to write back, I know the rules, I read the back of the form. The spare room is done. The lodger is a nurse called Precious from Northwick Park, nights, and she does my bins without being asked, which is more than Gary ever did. Gary has not been. He rang on my birthday and we talked about the weather in Leeds for nine minutes. I am not writing to make you feel bad. I am writing because somebody ought to know the room is done, and you are the one who knows what it was for. Yours sincerely, M. Tull (212).
[1811 Body Text] Lolly put it in the pad with the warning and did not answer it, and it was the hardest piece of filing she did that year.
[1812 Body Text] ∗
[1813 First Paragraph] On the Sunday before the ninth of February, Lolly rang the number on Ellen Prosper’s card. The office in Peterhead said the vessel was at sea and the set was not answering. She rang the partner in Sligo at six. The woman said Ellen had been at sea more than ashore since the autumn, and had taken neither the course in Newcastle nor the shop, and was, as far as the woman could tell over a bad line, happy, though she would not have put it that way herself, and would not thank anybody for asking.
[1814 Body Text] Lolly did not alter the trace. She wrote nothing on the cover that was not already there. Mrs Chain had asked for the page if the word came back. There was no page to send.
[1815 Body Text] ∗
[1816 First Paragraph] Two hundred miles east of Peterhead, on a Sunday, Ellen Prosper came up from the winch deck with her hands numb and found a message on the board in the wheelhouse, in Callum’s capitals, taken down off the satellite phone.
[1817 Body Text] NIAMH SAYS: a woman from the Ministry rang her. Wren. Says no trouble. Says she’d like to speak to you, not about you. Rang back twice to say “not about you” again.
[1818 Body Text] Ellen read it, and stood in the warm fug of the wheelhouse with her gloves under her arm and the sea going past grey and enormous outside, and did not ring back.
[1819 Body Text] Not because she did not want to. Because she did not know yet what she would say.
[1820 Body Text] She could not have told anybody when she had decided. That was the strange thing, the thing she had tried once to explain to Niamh over a bottle of something terrible in Lerwick and given up. There had been two things she was supposed to choose between — the course in Newcastle, her mother’s shop — and she had been choosing between them for so long that the choosing had become a kind of room she lived in. And then both of them had gone, quietly, one in the January and the other in the September, and nobody had told her, and she had gone on living in the room. And then, some morning in March, she had simply been somewhere else. Down at the harbour. Asking about a berth. As if a door had opened in a wall she had stopped seeing, and she had walked through it before she noticed it was a door.
[1821 Body Text] She did not think of it as a loss. She thought of it, when she thought of it at all, as the morning she stopped waiting.
[1822 Body Text] But someone had been keeping her card. That was what not about you meant. Somebody in an office in London had kept a card with her name on it on the outside of a file for a year, and had rung a boat in the North Sea on a Sunday to ask to speak to her rather than about her, and had rung back twice to make sure that was understood.
[1823 Body Text] Ellen wiped the board clean with her sleeve, and then, after a moment, wrote on it again, in her own hand: WREN — ring when we’re in. Not yet.
[1824 Body Text] Then she went back down to the winch, because the sea did not care about any of it, and that was one of the things she loved about it.
[1825 Body Text] ∗
[1826 First Paragraph] On the night of the eighth Lolly went to Larchfield.
[1827 Body Text] She had not planned to. She had planned to sit in the flat with the notebook and the countdown and a glass of wine she would not drink, and wait for the morning like everybody else in the department. At half past nine she found she had her coat on. She got the last 412 and then, when it terminated early for reasons the driver described as the depot, walked the last mile in the cold.
[1828 Body Text] Sandra let her in without asking why. Nineteen years on nights had taught her that people generally brought the answer with them.
[1829 Body Text] “He’s awake,” she said. “He’s usually awake. He likes the buses. There’s only the night one, after one, but he likes knowing it’s there.”
[1830 Body Text] The Garden Room was dark except for the streetlight. Her father was in the chair by the window, in his dressing gown, with the Roberts on his knee, switched off. He did not seem surprised to see her. He seemed, if anything, to have been expecting her slightly earlier.
[1831 Body Text] “Lol,” he said. “Pull up a chair. Mind the cable. The big lad’s left a soldering iron on the side and I’ve told him.”
[1832 Body Text] She pulled up a chair. She sat with him.
[1833 Body Text] Nothing happened. She had half known that nothing would; she had come anyway, the way you go back to check a gas tap you know you turned off. The streetlight buzzed. Down the corridor a door opened and closed and Tomasz’s trolley went past with one wheel complaining. At twelve minutes past three the night bus came round the corner, lit up, empty, and stopped at the stop for nobody and went on, and her father watched it all the way out of sight.
[1834 Body Text] “There,” he said, satisfied.
[1835 Body Text] “Dad. On the fourteenth of March. When you heard the door.”
[1836 Body Text] “I know.” He did not look away from the window. “I’ve told everybody. I heard it, and there was no door.” He was quiet for a while. “You’re going to tell me what it was. Your lot.”
[1837 Body Text] “I don’t know what it was. Not all of it. Somebody did something to — to everything, for six hours. You heard a door. I can’t tell you what anyone outside our records heard.”
[1838 Body Text] “And what were you waiting for?”
[1839 Body Text] She had not expected that. She sat in the dark and looked at it.
[1840 Body Text] “For you to be all right,” she said.
[1841 Body Text] “Well, I’m not,” said Dennis Wren, kindly. “I’m going. I know I’m going. But I’m going from here, with the light on, and the buses, and somebody to tell they’re doing it wrong, and that’s not nothing, Lol. That’s a good bench to go from.” He patted the Roberts. “You know where the fault is, and it isn’t where they point. You’ve always known that. You got that from me, not your mother. Your mother’d point at it and be right.”
[1842 Body Text] She laughed. It came out wet. He did not seem to mind.
[1843 Body Text] At twenty past five he turned the Roberts on without being asked, because it was twenty past five, and the shipping forecast came through, Dogger and Fisher and German Bight, every word in its place, and he mouthed the areas along with the announcer the way other men mouth hymns.
[1844 Body Text] At six Sandra brought tea, two cups, and put them down without comment, and her father drank his and said it was stewed, which it was, and that June would have had something to say, which she would. Lolly drank hers. At seven she kissed him on the top of the head, which she had not done since she was a child, and he said “Get off, you’ll have my hair out,” and went back to watching the buses, which had started again.
[1845 Body Text] She took the first bus home to change.
[1846 Body Text] ∗
[1847 First Paragraph] The ninth of February arrived on a Tuesday, cold, with nothing scheduled after ten, which was itself unprecedented.
[1848 Body Text] Lolly stood in it for a moment before she left the flat. Eleven months ago she had gone home most nights without checking whether the flat had a smell of its own. She had not much let herself think she would be staying in it. She checked, now. It smelled of the coat she had not moved from the hook by the door since March — which she had been meaning to move for eleven months, and had, at some point she could not date, stopped minding.
[1849 Body Text] She had once thought that a coat on a hook was the whole of what the year had cost her. It was not. It had cost Maureen Tull a son in her front room on the second Sunday of the month, and it had cost Lolly the one thing she had been proudest of: that her law had never yet been used against anybody. It had now, and she had been the one it was used on. The warning was in the Post Office pad, in green, and the notebook was in an evidence bag in a Cabinet Office cupboard, waiting for the Ombudsman, and after the Ombudsman for Kew. She did not think it was a fair price, since most of it had not been hers to pay, and she went out.
[1850 Body Text] Mrs Chain was on the landing bench, the umbrella across her knees. She had been offered a chair in the office. She had not taken it.
[1851 Body Text] She did not ask about the plates. She asked whether anyone had spoken to Ellen Prosper.
[1852 Body Text] “No,” said Lolly.
[1853 Body Text] “Then the page is not ready,” said Mrs Chain. “I asked to be sent it if the word came back. It has not come back. I will not have a measurement in its place. Speak to Ellen, when you can. Until then leave the card where it is.” She stood, and she went down the stair. She did not stay to hear the file read out.
[1854 Body Text] Inside, Lolly found Mrs Kind already at her desk, doing what she had done since the holiday argument: closing out the file the way a travel agent closes out a booking, checking every leg had actually been flown, not merely paid for.
[1855 Body Text] “The fourteen, not as a route,” she read aloud, for the record, one last time. “Three hundred and eight recovered, of the original tally, the fourteen among them, accounted a different way. All two hundred and nine monitored states dipped: fourteen left their permitted options, and the other hundred and ninety-five returned to where they had been. Nine cores in transit, correctly logged as such and not otherwise. Thirty-one cores, as of Friday’s reading, still advancing — Ramanathan says that number has not moved in six weeks, which she says, for a supernova countdown, is the best news she has had all year, because it means the field maintenance the appeal restored is holding.” She looked up. “Twelve days since Vienna. I keep expecting somebody to ring and take it back.”
[1856 Body Text] Ellen Prosper’s card was still on the outside of the file. Lolly did not take it off. The accounting had changed. The promise had not. Her request still stood, and no word had come.
[1857 Body Text] “They won’t,” said Lolly. “It’s in the ruling this time. With the word in it.”
[1858 Body Text] Gideon was in the doorway with a paper bag held in the hand whose knuckle had healed badly. By the look of him he had been waiting since March for a morning like this one, and had brought pastries in case it came on a Tuesday.
[1859 Body Text] She stayed on her side of the doorway. He held the bag clear, and said, “A morning with no countdown. I’ve brought something that counts as breakfast if you don’t inspect it.”
[1860 Body Text] “Stay,” she said.
[1861 Body Text] “I was going to,” he said. “I like hearing the instruction. It files cleaner.”
[1862 Body Text] He remained in the doorway, which was as far into a quiet room as he trusted himself, and she went back to the desk.
[1863 Body Text] She wrote on the first page of the Post Office pad, which was the first page of volume six whether the stationery knew it or not, with the whole department silent around her and Eilstein, who had asked to be present and been permitted on Article Two’s new terms, standing by the window with his hands behind his back and no reflection in the glass:
[1864 Body Text] The fourteen left the list, not the world. Somebody asked for the list sorted thinnest first, and we sold it to them, and Adaeze wrote it down. Schedule 4, lines one to fourteen, and not the fifteenth.
[1865 Body Text] Mrs Tull, 212. I asked, and she answered, and I may not ask again. That is my law and it is a good one, and I am the first person it caught. The room is done.
[1866 Body Text] And last, because it was the only entry that mattered on this particular day:
[1867 Body Text] Nothing detonated. Nobody was uninvited. The register closed clean, at 308 P-9, with an appendix nobody was ashamed to sign.
[1868 Body Text] There is no countdown tomorrow. I find I do not know what to do with a morning that doesn’t have one.
[1869 Body Text] Gideon is in the doorway with breakfast and a knuckle he will claim is nothing. He found the fault in Cumbria. He was on the stair in March. He has not asked me to call either of those things by a larger name. Dad
 is in the Garden Room with the window. I sat up with him last night and nothing happened, and I am writing that down because it is the most important nothing in this notebook.
[1870 Body Text] Ellen Prosper’s card is still on the outside of the file. Volume five of Lolly Wren’s green notebooks is in a cupboard in the Cabinet Office, waiting for the Ombudsman and then for Kew. Volume six is lined, from the Post Office on the corner, and has one page used.
[1871 Body Text] Q.E.D.
[1872 Heading 2] For Curious Readers: The Lectures
[1873 Body Text] This part is optional. The novel ends at Q.E.D., and nothing in it depends on what follows. For readers who want the physics under the story, one lecture follows for each chapter, and two of them are shared by a pair of chapters.
[1874 Body Text] Chapter One. Outside the Permitted Options
[1875 Body Text] Chapter Two. The Cost of Nothing
[1876 Body Text] Chapter Three. No Dice
[1877 Body Text] Chapter Four. The Same Holiday, Described Twice
[1878 Body Text] Chapters Five and Six. Mrs Kind Reads the Footnotes; The Motion Was Carried
[1879 Body Text] Chapter Seven. An Eternal Golden Braid
[1880 Body Text] Chapter Eight. The Game with No Optimal Play
[1881 Body Text] Chapter Nine. The Manufacture of an Agreed World
[1882 Body Text] Chapter Ten. Which Way the Galaxy Is Running
[1883 Body Text] Chapter Eleven. The Argument from Being Here
[1884 Body Text] Chapter Twelve. Ludger Soccermann Is Filed
[1885 Body Text] Chapter Thirteen. Mr Drill Is Not Quotable
[1886 Body Text] Chapter Fourteen. Lieutenant Schwarzschild Sends His Regrets
[1887 Body Text] Chapter Fifteen. The Remaining Possibilities
[1888 Body Text] Chapters Sixteen and Seventeen. The Fifteenth Line; Nothing Happens on the Ninth
[1889 Heading 2] Lecture. Chapter One
[1890 First Paragraph] The novel opened on quantum tunnelling. It is the gentlest of the ideas the later meetings keep trying to promote into a destination.
[1891 Body Text] The effect. Classically, a particle with less energy than a potential barrier cannot cross it: it arrives, it is reflected, that is the end of the matter. Quantum mechanically the wavefunction does not terminate at the barrier’s edge. Inside a classically forbidden region the solution to the Schrödinger equation becomes exponentially decaying rather than oscillatory, and if the barrier is thin enough, the amplitude on the far side is small but nonzero. The particle therefore has a finite probability of being found beyond an obstacle it never had the energy to climb. It does not go over. It does not tunnel a hole. The amplitude was simply never zero anywhere.
[1892 Body Text] The transmission probability falls off roughly as
[1893 Source Code] T ≈ e^(−2κL), κ = √(2m(V₀ − E)) / ħ
[1894 Body Text] which is why the effect is exquisitely sensitive to barrier width and height: thin barriers are practically transparent, and a barrier twice as thick can suppress transmission by many orders of magnitude. It is not exotic. It is why the Sun shines (protons fuse only by tunnelling through their mutual electrostatic repulsion), why alpha decay happens at all, why the tunnel diode and the scanning tunnelling microscope work, and why flash memory can be written and erased.
[1895 Body Text] The distinction the chapter turns on. A state cannot resolve into an outcome that is not in its state space. But “the state space” in any practical account is a model, and a truncated model has an edge where the real space does not. Fainrose’s correction, that a state missing from your description of the space cannot be tunnelled to, is the novel’s governing suspicion, and it is a direct escalation of Book One’s central error. The Ministry once mistook a subspace for reality and flattened houses into it. The new question is whether everyone, including the people trying to fix it, has been doing a milder version of the same thing all along.
[1896 Body Text] What Chapter One was actually showing you. The scene with the observatory’s non-signal was not really about astronomy. It was about the fact that “impossible” and “improbable” are different words for a reason, and that Book One’s whole crisis was built on a case where the department forgot which one it was looking at.
[1897 Heading 3] Going deeper
[1898 First Paragraph] Where it points. Tunnelling has a cosmological cousin. If the vacuum state we occupy is not the lowest available one but merely a local minimum — a false vacuum — then it is metastable, and it can decay by tunnelling to a lower state, nucleating a bubble that expands at nearly the speed of light. Whether our vacuum is stable, metastable, or unstable depends on parameters including the Higgs and top quark masses, and current measurements sit uncomfortably close to the boundary between stability and metastability. The relevant timescales, on standard estimates, are enormously longer than the present age of the universe.
[1899 Body Text] The observatory’s six hours, in which the vacuum appeared cheaper in one direction, are not a false-vacuum decay. A decay of that kind does not restore itself by breakfast. The liaison refused the word signal. This lecture refuses the word crossing. Fourteen people are missing from a model. The chapter does not watch them arrive anywhere.
[1900 Heading 3] What the reader learns
[1901 First Paragraph] A quantum system can pass a barrier it lacks the energy to cross, because its amplitude decays inside the barrier rather than vanishing.
[1902 Body Text] Transmission depends exponentially on barrier width — small changes to a barrier produce enormous changes in what becomes possible.
[1903 Body Text] Tunnelling is mundane and technological, not speculative: fusion, radioactive decay, microscopy, memory chips.
[1904 Body Text] A state cannot leave its space, but it can leave your model of its space, and the two are easily confused.
[1905 Body Text] Barriers do not thin themselves, which means something thinned this one.
[1906 Heading 2] Lecture. Chapter Two
[1907 First Paragraph] Chapter Two introduces vacuum zero-point energy and the Casimir effect. Neither is an established cause of quantum tunnelling; the novel links them through its speculative mechanism.
[1908 Body Text] The vacuum is not empty. In quantum field theory the lowest-energy state of a field is not a state of nothing happening. Each field mode behaves like a harmonic oscillator and retains a zero-point energy of one-half hbar omega in its ground state. Summing all modes requires renormalisation; it does not establish a directly measured absolute energy density of empty space. Vacuum-fluctuation calculations contribute to observed effects such as the Lamb shift and spontaneous emission; the anomalous magnetic moment is another precise QED test. These observations do not directly measure absolute vacuum energy.
[1909 Body Text] The Casimir effect. Hendrik Casimir, working at Philips Research in 1948 on the practical question of why colloidal suspensions in industrial products behaved as they did, calculated what happens when two uncharged parallel conducting plates are placed very close together. Between the plates, only field modes with wavelengths fitting the cavity are permitted; outside, all modes are available. The mode density between the plates is therefore lower than outside, and so is the vacuum energy density. The result is an attractive force per unit area
[1910 Source Code] F/A = −π²ħc / (240 d⁴)
[1911 Body Text] falling off as the fourth power of separation — feeble at a micron, substantial at tens of nanometres, and a genuine engineering nuisance in microelectromechanical devices. It was measured decisively by Lamoreaux in 1997 and many times since.
[1912 Body Text] The Casimir effect shows that boundary conditions affect allowed field modes. The measured force reflects an energy difference between boundary configurations; it does not directly measure absolute vacuum energy.
[1913 Body Text] Why a cheaper floor thins a barrier. Tunnelling probability depends exponentially on the barrier action. For a rectangular barrier, transmission includes exp(-2 k L), where k is proportional to sqrt(V0 - E). Changing the potential can alter that exponent, but shifting the energy reference alone does not necessarily change the physical barrier. The novel proposes that a small change in the vacuum floor alters the effective barrier. That is speculative; its numerical effect cannot be inferred from the Casimir effect alone.
[1914 Body Text] What Chapter Two was actually showing you. Within the novel's speculative model, a lower vacuum floor changes the effective barrier, and the fourteen are caught where it thins first. Known Casimir effects do not establish this mechanism.
[1915 Heading 3] Going deeper
[1916 First Paragraph] The honest caveats, which van Casteel supplies himself and which the novel keeps:
[1917 Body Text] Casimir energies are produced by boundaries at nanometre scales. Nothing in known physics imposes such conditions across astronomical baselines, and the story is explicit that this is ludicrous by present standards.
[1918 Body Text] Two matching numbers are a coincidence until there is a mechanism. Fainrose’s position — act, but do not publish — is the correct posture and is the discipline she learned in Book One.
[1919 Body Text] The vacuum-energy discrepancy is a major unsolved problem in theoretical physics. Estimates using a Planck-scale cutoff differ from the observed cosmological value by roughly 120 orders of magnitude; the comparison depends on how the quantum-field estimate is defined.
[1920 Body Text] And the structural point, which is Eilstein’s rule from Book One arriving in a new costume: making the vacuum cheaper in one place does not create anything. The Casimir cavity is only cheap relative to the unbounded space around it. Somebody has lowered a floor along a bearing, and floors are not lowered for free — so the question the novel asked is not what came past on the fourteenth of March, but where the difference went.
[1921 Heading 3] What the reader learns
[1922 First Paragraph] Quantum-field models assign zero-point energy to the vacuum, and vacuum fluctuations help explain observed effects. Those effects are measured; absolute vacuum energy has not been directly measured.
[1923 Body Text] Boundary conditions change the allowed field modes and produce a measurable Casimir force. The force reflects an energy difference between configurations; it does not measure absolute vacuum energy.
[1924 Body Text] Tunnelling rates depend exponentially on the gap between barrier and available energy, measured from the local floor.
[1925 Body Text] The novel's premise is that a change in the vacuum reshapes the effective barrier. Tunnelling rates depend exponentially on barrier action, so a change in the profile could affect the rate. Known Casimir effects do not demonstrate a planet-scale change or this mechanism.
[1926 Body Text] The event did not select anyone: it swept past, and everything unresolved in its path was briefly given a door.
[1927 Heading 2] Lecture. Chapter Three
[1928 First Paragraph] Chapter Three brings in determinism versus irreducible chance, and the specific methodological claim behind Einstein’s most-quoted and most-misused sentence.
[1929 Body Text] What Einstein actually objected to. In a 1926 letter to Max Born he wrote that the theory “says a lot, but does not really bring us any closer to the secret of the ‘old one’. I, at any rate, am convinced that He is not playing at dice.” (That is the published English translation; the letter is dated 4 December 1926.) The remark is routinely misread as a mystic’s discomfort with randomness. His actual objection was methodological and rather dry: quantum mechanics yields probabilities, and Einstein held that a probability is what a theory offers when its description is incomplete. Statistical mechanics gives probabilities for a gas, but nobody thinks a molecule’s trajectory is undetermined — the statistics are a confession of ignorance about the microstate. Einstein’s position was that the Born rule occupies the same epistemic role and that the underlying variables remain to be found. This is the position Eilstein articulates, and it is respectable as method even where it fails as physics.
[1930 Body Text] Hidden variables, and where Einstein was wrong. The 1935 EPR paper argued that quantum mechanics must be incomplete on the grounds that one can predict with certainty the result of a distant measurement without disturbing the distant system — implying a pre-existing element of reality the formalism omits. The argument assumed locality. Bell’s theorem (Book One, Chapter Thirteen) later showed that locality and pre-existing definite properties cannot both survive, and experiment has ruled against their conjunction. Einstein was therefore wrong about locality. He was not thereby shown wrong that a completion exists: pilot-wave theory (Book One, Chapter Twelve) is a fully deterministic hidden-variable account that reproduces all quantum predictions, at the price of explicit nonlocality. In such a theory, outcomes that look random are fixed by initial positions we do not know.
[1931 Body Text] The chapter’s actual test, and why it is not hand-waving. Eilstein does not claim to prove determinism. He infers it from an alleged observable cost: in his model, stochastic outcomes leave traces and determined outcomes do not. This is a premise of the novel, not a diagnostic established by Landauer’s principle.
[1932 Body Text] Landauer's principle sets a minimum heat cost of k_B T ln 2 for erasing one bit at temperature T under idealized conditions. The bound applies to erasure; it does not establish a universal thermodynamic cost for every physical outcome.
[1933 Body Text] In Eilstein’s model, a transition with one available final state counts as no selection. That is a story premise, not a thermodynamic result.
[1934 Body Text] So “no leavings” is Eilstein’s fictional inference. Landauer’s principle alone cannot show that an outcome was predetermined; measurement and erasure costs depend on the apparatus, protocol and thermodynamic state.
[1935 Body Text] The tunnelling mechanism underneath. This is the novel's speculative mechanism, not a result of standard quantum mechanics. Tunnelling rates depend on the actual potential barrier and particle energy; a change in the energy reference alone changes no physics. In the story, the register keeps fourteen superpositions over options that have ceased to exist. Fainrose interprets the mismatch as administrative confinement, then proposes that a six-hour change in the vacuum floor removed the remaining barrier. The distribution was not sampled, she argues; it was drained. That explanation belongs to the novel's model, not to established physics.
[1936 Body Text] The caveat worth keeping. The chapter does not settle whether quantum indeterminacy is fundamental; no accepted hidden-variable completion is required by the evidence. Within the story, Eilstein interprets fourteen records as evidence that the outcomes were fixed and that the reported 6.7% rate came from counting a population rather than examining its initial conditions. This is the characters' inference from fictional evidence, not a scientific demonstration of determinism.
[1937 Body Text] What Chapter Three was actually showing you. The department’s own paperwork had been quietly deciding what counted as “nothing left to choose between” for sixteen months, which meant the real offence long preceded whatever thinned the vacuum in Chapter Two.
[1938 Heading 3] Going deeper
[1939 First Paragraph] One further shadow, which the novel leaves open: the only remaining loophole in Bell tests is superdeterminism — the possibility that measurement settings and system properties share a common prior cause. It is widely regarded as unmotivated. It becomes considerably less unmotivated in a story where something can adjust the vacuum across a planet for six hours.
[1940 Heading 3] What the reader learns
[1941 First Paragraph] “God does not play dice” was an argument that probabilities signal incomplete description, not a distaste for uncertainty.
[1942 Body Text] Einstein’s EPR argument assumed locality and was refuted on that point; the possibility of a deterministic completion was not thereby closed.
[1943 Body Text] Landauer’s bound applies to logically irreversible erasure under stated conditions, not universally to every measurement or outcome. In the story, “no leavings” is an invented diagnostic, not an established test for determinism.
[1944 Body Text] Eilstein treats the absence of an erasure cost as evidence of determination. This is his fictional diagnostic, not a consequence of Landauer’s principle.
[1945 Body Text] A superposition maintained over options that no longer exist is confined by paperwork, not by physics, and has nothing beneath it.
[1946 Body Text] A rate can conceal a reason: counting a population is not reading it.
[1947 Heading 2] Lecture. Chapter Four
[1948 First Paragraph] Chapter Four brings in the holographic principle and the entropy bounds that force it.
[1949 Body Text] The bound that starts it. Ordinary intuition says the information capacity of a region scales with its volume. Bekenstein’s 1972 work on black hole thermodynamics broke that. A black hole’s entropy is proportional not to its volume but to the area of its event horizon:
[1950 Source Code] S_BH = k_B c³A / (4Għ) = k_B A / (4ℓ_P²)
[1951 Body Text] A black hole's entropy scales with its horizon area. This result motivates holographic bounds for gravitating regions. Extending the bound to all space remains a theoretical principle, not an experimentally measured universal capacity.
[1952 Body Text] From a bound to a principle. 't Hooft and Susskind took a further theoretical step: if information in a region can be represented on its boundary, the boundary may provide a complete account. The interior and boundary are not interchangeable in every setting. In the AdS/CFT correspondence (Maldacena, 1997), a gravitational theory in a (d+1)-dimensional anti-de Sitter bulk is conjectured to be dual to a conformal field theory without gravity on its d-dimensional boundary. Within this conjecture, the descriptions are expected to yield equivalent physics; this does not establish which account is real.
[1953 Body Text] This is Mrs Kind’s holiday. Two hotels producing an identical set of relations are not two holidays with a hidden fact about which you were in. There is no further fact. Duality is that claim, made precise.
[1954 Body Text] Why a travel agent, and why baggage. The chapter’s device is not arbitrary. Mrs Kind arrives at the bound through the observation that everything a client can ever report has to fit in the boxes of a booking form — a finite surface constraining an apparently unbounded interior experience. Leonard Susskind was a plumber before he was a physicist; the through-line of these books is that the people who noticed the strange thing were rarely the ones trained not to.
[1955 Body Text] The mechanism she proposes. Her physical claim - that the vacuum became less subscribed - is speculative fiction. De Sitter horizon entropy and temperature are related to geometry, but no established theory predicts that locally reducing information on a screen changes local vacuum energy. The ramp-plateau-ramp profile is also invented.
[1956 Body Text] The entropy accounting. The proposed link between a pending state’s options, surface information and barrier height is the novel’s invented model, not a consequence of holographic entropy bounds.
[1957 Body Text] The recommendation and its fictional physics. "Make everybody fat" is Mrs Kind's fictional recommendation. More recorded options or correlations are not known to physically harden a tunnelling barrier.
[1958 Body Text] What Chapter Four was actually showing you. Mrs Kind’s holiday was not a parlour trick. It was the same claim the department needed to hear about the fourteen: two descriptions, exactly as complete as each other, and a person insisting on asking which one is “the real one” is asking a question the physics has already declined to answer.
[1959 Heading 3] Going deeper
[1960 First Paragraph] What Eilstein is warning about. In specific holographic models, a unitary boundary theory provides theoretical evidence for unitary bulk evolution. This is not an experimental resolution for astrophysical black holes. Unitarity conserves information globally; it does not guarantee practical access to it.
[1961 Heading 3] What the reader learns
[1962 First Paragraph] Information capacity scales with boundary area, not enclosed volume — about one bit per 2.8 Planck areas (one natural unit of entropy per four).
[1963 Body Text] Black hole entropy set that bound, since nothing denser than a black hole fits in a region.
[1964 Body Text] AdS/CFT is a conjectured duality studied in specific mathematical models; it is not experimentally established as a description of our universe.
[1965 Body Text] When two descriptions produce identical relations, there is no further fact about which is real.
[1966 Body Text] Horizon entropy and vacuum energy are related in specific theoretical settings. No established result supports the novel’s proposed local link between screen information and vacuum energy.
[1967 Body Text] Unitarity conserves information; it does not guarantee you can still get at it.
[1968 Heading 2] Lecture. Chapters Five and Six
[1969 First Paragraph] Chapters Five and Six bring in black hole complementarity and the firewall problem and, through item seven of Annex C, the tunnelling basis of stellar fusion and how core-collapse supernovae are triggered. President Wexley, Professor Tengelman and the Collegium at Brask are invented and are not portraits of anyone.
[1970 Body Text] Complementarity. Susskind, Thorlacius and Uglum proposed in 1993 that black-hole complementarity might address the information paradox by giving outside and infalling observers different accounts. In the proposal, an outside observer sees information encoded on a stretched horizon and returned in radiation; an infalling observer sees an ordinary horizon. The conjecture says no observer can compare both accounts. Its application to astrophysical black holes remains debated and has no experimental confirmation. This is the doctrine the Office of the President relies upon, and it is stated fairly in the chapter.
[1971 Body Text] The tension: the firewall argument. In 2012, Almheiri, Marolf, Polchinski and Sully (AMPS) argued that several assumptions cannot all hold together; this is not an experimentally confirmed result. For the outside account to work, a late Hawking quantum must be maximally correlated with the radiation already emitted. For an infalling observer to encounter an unremarkable horizon, that quantum must also be correlated with its partner just inside. Monogamy of entanglement prevents both correlations from being maximal:
[1972 Source Code] if I(A:B) is maximal, then I(A:C) = 0
[1973 Body Text] Something must give. If the outside account is preserved, the near-horizon partner correlation is absent, and an infalling observer meets not empty space but a wall of high-energy quanta — a firewall. The paradox remains unresolved. In specific semiclassical models, island and quantum-extremal-surface calculations reproduce Page-curve-like behavior for radiation entropy. These results are theoretical; their application to real astrophysical black holes remains unsettled. In the novel, Beatrix’s argument follows from the story’s premises. Its reported S = 2.41 reading is fictional: a real CHSH violation would test Bell-locality under stated assumptions, but would not by itself demonstrate black-hole information loss or a firewall. The value 2 sqrt 2 (about 2.828) is the ideal quantum maximum for CHSH, not a generic prediction.
[1974 Body Text] Unitarity is not a defence. The Office’s third argument is true and irrelevant, and the distinction deserves stating plainly. Unitary evolution conserves information globally. It says nothing about locality, accessibility, or custody. Information can be perfectly conserved and simultaneously scrambled beyond any practical recovery, or removed to a screen no party can read. “The accounts balance” and “nothing has happened” are different propositions, and conflating them is the oldest move in institutional history.
[1975 Body Text] What Chapters Five and Six were actually showing you. Beatrix’s treaty argument runs on exactly this: the Office’s defence — nothing is lost, only re-encoded, so there is no harm — is complementarity’s own defence, and it has the same expiry date the firewall paradox gave it.
[1976 Heading 3] Going deeper
[1977 First Paragraph] Item seven: why stars are the most sensitive instrument in the sky. The Sun’s core is at roughly 1.5 × 10⁷ K, corresponding to particle energies near 1 keV. The Coulomb barrier between two protons is of order 1 MeV — a factor of about a thousand too high. Classically, the Sun cannot burn. Fusion proceeds entirely by quantum tunnelling, with the penetration probability given by the Gamow factor:
[1978 Source Code] P ~ exp(−√(E_G / E))
[1979 Body Text] The exponential sensitivity of tunnelling is real. The proposed vacuum-floor change and its stellar effect belong to the novel’s invented mechanism; multiplying a hypothetical rate change by 10^56 nuclei does not establish a measurable change in stellar energy generation.
[1980 Body Text] Advancing, not causing. Ramanathan’s distinction is the physically honest one. A core-collapse supernova occurs when an iron core exceeds its Chandrasekhar mass (≈ 1.4 M☉) and electron degeneracy pressure — which does not increase with temperature — can no longer support it. Collapse follows in about a second, and 99% of the released energy leaves as neutrinos. Nothing external is needed to cause it; the star’s own fuel exhaustion guarantees it. What a six-hour reduction in the vacuum floor can do, in the novel’s premise, is tip a star that is already in its last burning stages across a stage change early. Fusion rates (tunnelling rates) set when each stage ends and the next one lights, and a head start is inherited by every later stage. Six hours of slightly faster burning does not, by itself, buy thousands of years; the size of the advance is the premise and a model’s output, not a measurement. On stellar timescales an advance of a few thousand years is nothing. On the timescale of anything living within thirty light years of the progenitor, it is everything, and the chapter’s true horror is Annex C’s willingness to list this as item seven of nine.
[1981 Body Text] Eilstein’s rule, applied to a budget line. The Programme’s fatal weakness is structural rather than political, and it generalises well beyond fiction: a service whose only success condition is that nothing occurs cannot demonstrate value, accumulates no constituency, and is therefore the cheapest line to cut in any body that has been persuaded to simplify. This is Book One’s central rule arriving at galactic scale — any institution that promises to make things simple is proposing to move complexity somewhere you cannot see — with the additional and grimmer observation that the move can be executed entirely out of true statements.
[1982 Heading 3] What the reader learns
[1983 First Paragraph] Black hole complementarity is a proposed framework for reconciling exterior and interior accounts; whether it resolves the information paradox remains debated.
[1984 Body Text] The AMPS argument uses entanglement monogamy to show that its assumptions cannot all hold together. A firewall is a possible implication, not an observed feature; the paradox remains open.
[1985 Body Text] Unitarity conserves information globally but guarantees nothing about where it is or who can reach it; “the accounts balance” is not “no harm occurred”.
[1986 Body Text] Stars burn only by tunnelling — the Coulomb barrier is about a thousand times core thermal energies — which makes stellar cores exponential amplifiers of vacuum conditions.
[1987 Body Text] Core collapse is triggered when an iron core passes the Chandrasekhar mass and degeneracy pressure fails; external perturbations do not cause supernovae, they advance them.
[1988 Body Text] A programme whose success looks identical to inaction is structurally the easiest thing in any institution to defund.
[1989 Heading 2] Lecture. Chapter Seven
[1990 First Paragraph] Chapter Seven brings in Gödel’s incompleteness theorems, their computational twin the halting problem, and the fact — not the metaphor — that undecidability occurs in physics.
[1991 Body Text] The first incompleteness theorem. Gödel’s 1931 theorem shows that any consistent, effectively axiomatised system strong enough for arithmetic cannot decide every sentence in its language. The original proof required a stronger consistency condition; Rosser later showed that consistency alone suffices for an undecidable sentence. The familiar description ‘true but unprovable’ requires suitable soundness and interpretation in the standard model. The machinery is Gödel numbering: every formula and every proof is assigned a natural number, so that statements about provability become statements about arithmetic, and the system acquires the capacity to talk about itself. The diagonal lemma then permits a sentence G asserting, in effect, that G is not provable in F. If F proves G, it can also verify that G has a proof, contradicting what G asserts; F would then be inconsistent. If F is sound, it cannot prove G, and G is true in the intended arithmetic model. Adding G as an axiom does not help; the enlarged system generates a new such sentence. Incompleteness is a property of formal expressive power, not of any particular axiom set.
[1992 Body Text] The second theorem, which is the one Beatrix needs, says that no such system, if it is consistent, can prove that it is. F ⊬ Con(F). A body that certifies itself has, by that act, said nothing — and if it insists, the insistence is evidence against it.
[1993 Body Text] Turing’s version. Turing showed in 1936 that no algorithm can decide, for arbitrary program and input, whether the program halts. This is the same phenomenon in computational dress, and it is the form in which incompleteness leaks into the physical world.
[1994 Body Text] Undecidability in physics, which is the chapter’s real weight. In 2015, Cubitt, Pérez-García and Wolf proved that the spectral gap problem is undecidable: given an exact description of a translationally invariant many-body Hamiltonian on a lattice, there is no algorithm that determines in finite time whether the system is gapped or gapless. The proof embeds a universal Turing machine in the ground-state structure. Related results establish undecidability for whether certain quantum measurement outcomes ever occur, and for various questions in classical dynamics.
